Dimension reduction for derivative-informed operator learning: An analysis of approximation errors
Dingcheng Luo, Thomas O’Leary-Roseberry, Peng Chen, Omar Ghattas

Abstract
We study the derivative-informed learning of nonlinear operators between infinite-dimensional separable Hilbert spaces by neural networks. Such operators can arise from the solution of partial differential equations (PDEs), and are used in many simulation-based outer-loop tasks in science and engineering, such as PDE-constrained optimization, Bayesian inverse problems, and optimal experimental design. In these settings, the neural network approximations can be used as surrogate models to accelerate the solution of the outer-loop tasks. However, since outer-loop tasks in infinite dimensions often require knowledge of the underlying geometry, the approximation accuracy of the operator’s derivatives can also significantly impact the performance of the surrogate model. Motivated by this, we analyze the approximation errors of neural operators in Sobolev norms over infinite-dimensional Gaussian input measures. We focus on the reduced basis neural operator (RBNO), which uses linear encoders and decoders defined on dominant input/output subspaces spanned by reduced sets of orthonormal bases. To this end, we study two methods for generating the bases; principal component analysis (PCA) and derivative-informed subspaces (DIS), which use dominant eigenvectors of the covariance of the data or the derivatives as the reduced bases, respectively. We then derive bounds for errors arising from both the dimension reduction and the latent neural network approximation, including the sampling errors associated with the empirical estimation of the PCA/DIS. Our analysis is validated on numerical experiments with elliptic PDEs, where our results show that bases informed by the map (i.e., DIS or output PCA) yield accurate reconstructions and generalization errors for both the operator and its derivatives, while input PCA may underperform unless ranks and training sample sizes are sufficiently large.



Keywords
: Operator learning, derivative-informed neural operators, Sobolev training, dimension reduction, universal approximation

†
†
footnotetext:
*Corresponding author: dc.luo@utexas.edu

1
Introduction

In this work, we consider the task of learning neural network representations of nonlinear operators between infinite-dimensional spaces in terms of both their outputs and their derivatives.
Such operators arise, for example, as the solution operators of partial differential equations (PDEs),
which model many physical systems and play important roles in scientific discovery and engineering design.
For complex PDEs, their solution operators can only be accessed using numerical methods with significant computational costs, which often renders their use in simulation-intensive outer loop tasks—such as simulation-based design, inference, and uncertainty quantification—extremely computationally expensive, if not intractable.
To this end, neural network approximations of the expensive-to-solve PDEs can be effective surrogate models
that have the potential to significantly accelerate the aforementioned outer-loop tasks.

Over the past years, a vast selection of neural network architectures for operator learning has been proposed. Well-known examples include
those using reduced bases representations (PCANet, POD-DL-ROM)
[
25
,
5
,
19
]
,
DeepONets
[
38
,
39
,
27
]
,
Fourier neural operators
[
35
,
9
]

along with many more recent developments (see
[
29
,
30
]
for more comprehensive discussions).
Typically, these neural network representations are trained in a conventional, supervised manner,
where the PDE’s input parameters and solutions are used as input-output pairs
to define a loss function based on the misfit between the neural network predictions and the true outputs.

In addition, it has been demonstrated that, when available,
derivatives of the underlying input-output maps (or approximations thereof) can be utilized to improve the accuracy of the surrogates.

[
36
,
17
,
10
,
54
,
56
,
46
]
.
In particular, derivatives of the input-output maps can be used as training data (in addition to the input-output pairs used in conventional supervised learning),
and be incorporated into the training loss with a Sobolev-type norm,
thereby encouraging the resulting function approximation to be accurate not only in its predicted output values, but also its derivatives with respect to the input.
This has been considered for constructing finite-dimensional neural network approximations in works such as
[
36
,
17
,
10
,
54
,
56
]

where the approach has been termed
gradient-enhanced neural networks
or
Sobolev training
.

In
[
46
]
, Sobolev training is considered for the approximation of formally infinite-dimensional mappings that arise from the solution operators of (PDEs), where it is given the name
derivative-informed neural operators
(DINOs).
Additional works have shown that Sobolev training of neural operators (which we will also refer to as derivative-informed operator learning)
can lead to notable benefits when used to construct surrogates for
outer-loop tasks involving PDEs
[
10
,
40
,
20
,
12
,
21
,
11
]
.
For example, derivative-informed surrogates have been
applied to PDE-constrained optimization, both deterministic and under uncertainty
[
10
,
40
]
,
acceleration of geometric Markov Chain Monte Carlo and measure transport algorithms for Bayesian inverse problems
[
12
,
11
]
,
and Bayesian optimal experimental design
[
20
,
21
]
.
Due to the important roles played by the derivatives in these outer-loop tasks,
the improved derivative accuracy in the surrogates provided by Sobolev training can be crucial to obtaining the observed performance gains.

Since the input and output spaces in operator learning can be arbitrarily high dimensional upon discretization, the discretized derivative (i.e., the Jacobian matrix) can be extremely expensive to compute. To address this,
[
46
]
, along with subsequent works
[
40
,
20
,
12
,
48
,
21
,
11
]
, have leveraged the reduced basis neural operator (RBNO) architecture for the approximation to enable scalable and efficient Sobolev training.
That is, the operator surrogate uses linear encoding and decoding of the input and output spaces given in terms of reduced bases.
These are constructed before training the neural network, which defines only the mapping between the reduced (latent) representations of the inputs and/or outputs.
This choice of architecture allows the data generation and training costs associated with the derivatives to scale only with the intrinsic dimensionality of the mapping
(i.e., rank of the reduced bases), as only the action of the derivative within the reduced subspaces are needed in training.

In this approach, the selection of the reduced bases, or dimension reduction technique, plays an important role.
A common approach is the principal component analysis (PCA) on function space,
which uses the dominant eigenvectors of the covariance operators of the input and outputs as reduced bases
[
25
,
5
,
19
]
.
The active subspace (AS) method
[
14
,
57
]
has also been considered for input dimension reduction in
[
44
,
47
]
.
This approach essentially uses the dominant eigenvectors of the covariance of the derivative operator’s adjoint to construct
a subspace of the input space to which the outputs are most sensitive.

In this work, our goal is to analyze the approximation error of reduced basis architectures in the context of derivative-informed operator learning.
Specifically, we focus on operators between (real-valued) infinite-dimensional separable Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, where the input space is equipped with a Gaussian measure
\gamma
.
We are interested in studying the not only the approximation error of the operator in the usual
L^{2}_{\gamma}
Bochner sense, but also the approximation error of the derivative. That is, we consider the approximations of operators the
H^{1}_{\gamma}
Sobolev norm over infinite-dimensional Gaussian measures as defined in
[
8
,
7
]
, which is closely related to the classical Sobolev spaces for finite-dimensional inputs.

Additionally, since the choice of dimension reduction plays an important role in reduced basis architectures, we investigate how different choices of reduced bases impact the overall approximation error.
In addition to the PCA, we also consider a generalization of the AS method to both input and output dimension reduction, where the output dimension reduction uses the covariance of the derivative operator instead of its adjoint.
We refer to this approach as the input/output derivative-informed subspaces (DIS).
Our goal is then to obtain combined approximation error bounds in terms of both the dimension reduction and the neural network approximation, where we are additionally interested in the statistical properties of the dimension reduction errors due the use of sample-based covariance estimators in computing the PCA and DIS.

1.1
Related works

Many of the existing works for analyzing the approximation errors of operator surrogates focus on the approximation of the operator’s output values (e.g.,
[
38
,
28
,
5
,
33
,
32
]
),
typically providing error bounds in terms an
L^{2}_{\gamma}
Bochner norm or an
L^{\infty}
norm over a compact set.
In this context, the works of
[
5
,
32
]
are most closely related to ours. These works establish approximation errors bounds for PCANet—a reduced basis architecture that uses PCA for dimension reduction—in which the error decomposes into dimension reduction errors and neural network approximation errors.
Additionally, we mention that the approximation error of linear encoder-decoder architectures for operator learning has also been studied in
[
24
,
1
,
3
,
49
,
2
]
, which additionally establish approximation rates with respect to the number of neural network parameters and/or training sample size under assumptions such as holomorphy and Lipschitz continuity, utilizing specific parametrizations of the neural network based on polynomial chaos expansions.

On the other hand, we are not aware of approximation results for operator surrogates involving the derivative accuracy.
The approximation accuracy of neural networks in finite-dimensional settings is considered in works as early as that of
[
26
]
,
which proves universal approximation results of functions in
C^{k}
(the space of
k
times continuously differentiable functions)
by single-layer neural networks using smooth, sigmoid-like activation functions,
with errors measured in either weighted Sobolev norms or
C^{k}
norms over compact sets.
In our work, we make use of this result of
[
26
]
to develop our universal approximation results, providing extensions to deep neural network architectures and commonly used smooth and non-saturating activation functions such as softplus, sigmoid linear unit (SiLU), and Gaussian error linear unit (GeLU).
We note that more modern results for approximation in classical Sobolev norms are available, e.g.,
[
22
,
45
,
18
]
,
often with precise estimates on depth and width of the neural network,
though these are typically formulated for ReLU activation functions and over bounded domains.

Our analysis of dimension reduction via the DIS also draws on the works of
[
15
,
57
,
31
,
16
,
4
,
13
]
.
For Gaussian input measures, our input DIS coincides with the vector-valued active subspace (AS)
[
57
]
, which has an established bound on the
L^{2}_{\gamma}
error when used for input dimension reduction.
The results of
[
57
]
have subsequently been applied to finite-dimensional neural network approximations in
[
44
,
47
]
.
Moreover, the DIS also coincides with the likelihood-informed subspace (LIS) in the context of input dimension reduction for Bayesian inverse problems
[
15
,
58
,
16
]
as well as subsequent extensions to joint input and output dimension reduction
[
4
,
13
]
.
Again, the aforementioned works typically considered finite-dimensional input/output spaces.
Additionally, the input DIS (LIS) is considered for an infinite-dimensional input space but a finite-dimensional output space in
[
12
,
11
]
.

Sampling errors associated with PCA in Hilbert spaces are well studied (e.g.,
[
6
,
41
,
50
]
).
Analogous results are also used in the analyses of PCANet in
[
5
,
32
]
.
Additionally,
[
31
,
16
]
consider the sampling errors associated with constructing the AS/LIS matrices, though in the finite-dimensional setting.

1.2
Contributions

In this work, we analyze the errors associated with approximating both an operator and its derivatives by an RBNO architecture.
Our main contribution is a universal approximation result in the
H^{1}_{\gamma}

that decomposes the error into neural network errors, dimension reduction errors, and sampling errors.
These errors contributions are then numerically studied in the context of learning the solution operator of elliptic PDEs with Gaussian random field inputs.

In developing our main result, we also make the following contributions:

•

We formulate the input/output DIS (AS/LIS) in the Hilbert space setting
and develop analogous results for the reconstruction errors and ridge function errors in the infinite-dimensional setting based on the theory of Sobolev classes over infinite-dimensional Gaussian measures.

•

We follow the approaches of
[
50
,
5
,
32
]
to provide bounds for the statistical sampling errors associated with the DIS operators in the Hilbert space setting.

This allows us to unify several earlier results
that have been developed for various choices of input and output bases in either the finite- or infinite-dimensional settings

[
15
,
57
,
5
,
16
,
4
,
44
,
47
,
32
,
12
,
13
,
11
]
.
A comparison of the problem settings in several existing works is presented in
Table

1
.

H^{1}_{\gamma}
.
Note that our use of the term DIS additionally includes the AS and LIS strategies.

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows.
In
Section

2
, we introduce the mathematical setup of derivative-informed operator learning with reduced basis architectures over an infinite-dimensional Gaussian input measure,
including the definition of Sobolev classes over Gaussian measures and the formulations for PCA and DIS.
In
Section

3
, we present our approximation error results, providing bounds for the different sources of error—dimension reduction, neural network approximation, and sampling.
For clarity, many supporting lemmas and proofs for these results are provided in the Appendices.
We then present a suite of numerical experiments in
Section

4
,
where we validate our theoretical bounds while numerically comparing the effectiveness of PCA and DIS as dimension reduction strategies in derivative learning with RBNOs.
Finally, we conclude in
Section

5
with a summary of our findings and discuss potential future directions.

2
Learning an operator and its derivatives

2.1
Mathematical setup and notation

Let
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
be real separable Hilbert spaces with inner products
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}
and
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}}
.
We will consider the case where
\mathcal{X}
is equipped with a Gaussian measure
\gamma=\mathcal{N}(0,C_{\mathcal{X}})
defined over the Borel sigma algebra
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{X})
,
assumed to be centered without loss of generality.
In this setting, the covariance operator
C_{\mathcal{X}}=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\gamma}[x\otimes x]
is a trace-class operator on
\mathcal{X}
, which we assume is non-degenerate.
Additionally, let
\mathcal{E}=\mathrm{Range}(C_{\mathcal{X}}^{1/2})
be the Cameron–Martin space associated with
\mathcal{X}
,
which itself is a separable Hilbert space with the inner product
\left\langle w_{1},w_{2}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{E}}=\left\langle C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1/2}w_{1},C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1/2}w_{2}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}
.

For a pair of separable Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
, we will use

\mathcal{L}_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2})
to define the space of continuous
k
-linear operators, with
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2})=\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2})
.
For
k>1
, we will write
A(h_{1},\dots,h_{k})\in\mathcal{H}_{2}

for the action of
A\in\mathcal{L}_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2})
on input directions
h_{1},\dots,h_{k}\in\mathcal{H}_{1}
.
Moreover, we write
A(h_{1},\dots,h_{k-1},\cdot)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2})
as the linear operator defined by leaving one of the input directions open (in this case, the
k
th input direction).
The operator norm for
A\in\mathcal{L}_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2})
is given by

\|A\|_{\mathcal{L}_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2})}=\sup_{h_{1},\dots h_{k}\neq 0}\frac{\|A(h_{1},\dots,h_{k})\|_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}}{\|h_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\cdots\|h_{k}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}}.

Similarly, we will consider the space of
k
-linear Hilbert–Schmidt operators
\mathrm{HS}_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2})
,
where the inner product generating the Hilbert–Schmidt norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{HS}_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2})}
is given by

\left\langle A,B\right\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2})}=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}=1}^{\infty}\left\langle A(e_{i_{1}},\dots,e_{i_{k}}),B(e_{i_{1}},\dots,e_{i_{k}})\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{2}},

with any orthonormal basis
(e_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
of the input space
\mathcal{H}_{1}
.
Specifically, we write
\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2})=\mathrm{HS}_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2})

with inner product

\left\langle A,B\right\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2})}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\langle Ae_{i},Be_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\langle e_{i},A^{*}Be_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}=\mathrm{Tr}(A^{*}B)

for
A,B\in\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2})
, where
\mathrm{Tr}(\cdot)
denotes the trace and
A^{*}
denotes the adjoint of
A
.
Note that the Hilbert–Schmidt norm of
A
is simply
\sqrt{\mathrm{Tr}(A^{*}A)}
.

For any (real) separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, let
L^{p}(\gamma,\mathcal{H})
denote the space of functions from
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathcal{H}
that is integrable in the Bochner sense with finite
p
th moments,
p\geq 1
,
such that
\|F\|_{L^{p}(\gamma,\mathcal{H})}^{p}=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|F\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{p}]<\infty
for
F\in L^{p}(\gamma,\mathcal{H})
.
We can then introduce the Sobolev class of functions
W^{m,p}(\gamma,\mathcal{H})
, which are functions with Sobolev derivatives up to order
m
that belong to
L^{p}
.
This has a number of equivalent definitions.
Here, we describe the definition using the completion of smooth cylindrical functions, and refer to
[
8
,
42
]
for further details.

Let
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
denote the collection of functions
F:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}

that have the representation

F(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\phi_{i}(\left\langle v_{1},x\right\rangle_{\mathcal{X}},\dots,\left\langle v_{n},x\right\rangle_{\mathcal{X}})u_{i},

for some
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
, where
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
,

u_{1},\dots,u_{m}\in\mathcal{Y}
, and
\phi_{1},\dots\phi_{m}\in C^{\infty}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R})
, where
C^{\infty}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R})
refers to the space of smooth and bounded functions from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
\mathbb{R}
.
The class
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
is referred to as the class of smooth cylindrical functions.
For
F\in\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, the restriction of the derivatives
D^{k}F
to directions in the Cameron–Martin space, denoted by

D_{\mathcal{E}}^{k}F(x)=D^{k}F(x)|_{\mathcal{E}}
,
is Hilbert–Schmidt (i.e.,
D_{\mathcal{E}}^{k}F(x)\in\mathrm{HS}_{k}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})
), and has bounded moments.
We define the norm
\|\cdot\|_{W^{m,p}(\gamma,\mathcal{H})}
as

\|F\|_{W^{m,p}(\gamma,\mathcal{H})}=\left(\sum_{k=0}^{m}\int\|D_{\mathcal{E}}^{k}F(x)\|_{\mathrm{HS}_{k}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{p}\;\gamma(dx)\right)^{\!\!\frac{1}{p}}=\left(\sum_{k=0}^{m}\|D_{\mathcal{E}}^{k}F\|_{L^{p}(\gamma,\mathcal{X};\mathrm{HS}_{k}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{p}\right)^{\!\!\frac{1}{p}}.

(1)

The space
W^{m,p}(\gamma,\mathcal{H})
is then defined as the completion of
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
under
\|\cdot\|_{W^{m,p}}
.
For functions
F\in W^{m,p}(\gamma,\mathcal{H})
, we define their Sobolev derivatives
D_{\mathcal{E}}F

as the unique limit of sequences
(D_{\mathcal{E}}F_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
for
F_{i}\in\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
that converges to
F
in
W^{m,p}(\gamma,\mathcal{H})
.
Notably, the Sobolev derivatives
behave as the stochastic Gâteaux derivatives (see
[
8
, Chapter 5]
).
That is, for
F\in W^{1,p}(\gamma,\mathcal{H})
, the residual

\frac{F(x+th)-F(x)}{t}-D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x)h

(2)

converges to zero in measure
\gamma
as
t\rightarrow 0
for any
h\in\mathcal{E}
.
Analogous results hold for higher-order derivatives.

In this work, we focus on the case
p=2
,
for which we introduce the notation
H^{m}(\gamma,\mathcal{H}):=W^{m,2}(\gamma,\mathcal{H})
,
along with the shorthand
L^{2}_{\gamma}:=L^{2}(\gamma,\mathcal{Y})
,
H^{m}_{\gamma}:=H^{m}(\gamma,\mathcal{Y})
, and
W^{m,p}_{\gamma}:=W^{m,p}(\gamma,\mathcal{Y})
specifically when the output space is
\mathcal{Y}
.
Moreover, we will often consider mappings
F
that are additionally twice continuously Fréchet differentiable,
F\in C^{2}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
,
where the first and second derivatives have bounded second moments,
i.e.,
DF\in L^{2}(\gamma,\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}))
and
D^{2}F\in L^{2}(\gamma,\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}))
.
Note that Fréchet differentiability is a stronger notion of differentiability than the Sobolev differentiability that defines
W^{m,p}(\gamma,\mathcal{Y})
.
Since the inclusion map
\iota:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
is in
\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{X})
, the restriction of an operator
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
,
A|_{\mathcal{E}}=A\circ\iota
, is also in
\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})
.
In particular, this implies
DF|_{\mathcal{E}}=D_{\mathcal{E}}F
and its adjoint with respect to
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})
is given by
D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x)^{*}=C_{\mathcal{X}}DF(x)^{*}
, where
DF(x)^{*}
is the adjoint of
DF(x)
defined on
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
.
Thus, these assumptions on Fréchet differentiability would imply that
F\in H^{2}(\gamma,\mathcal{Y})
.

For such
F
, we aim to analyze the approximation accuracy of operator surrogates
\widetilde{F}\approx F
,
quantified in terms of both the function value and its derivatives.
We will measure the function value in the
L^{2}_{\gamma}=L^{2}(\gamma,\mathcal{Y})
norm,
and the derivative with the seminorm
|\cdot|_{H^{1}_{\gamma}}
defined as,

|F|_{H^{1}_{\gamma}}^{2}:=\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}].

(3)

That is, we consider overall approximation error estimates in the
H^{1}_{\gamma}
norm,

\|F\|_{H^{1}_{\gamma}}=\left(\|F\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}+|F|_{H^{1}_{\gamma}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

(4)

2.2
Operator surrogates using deep neural networks

In this work, we consider operator surrogates based on feedforward neural networks.
For an activation function
\psi:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
we say
\varphi_{\psi,\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}}

is a (constant width, feedforward) neural network with input size
d_{\mathrm{in}}
, output size
d_{\mathrm{out}}
, activation function
\psi
,
depth
d_{L}
, and width
d_{W}
, if it takes the form

\varphi_{\psi,\theta}(x)=\mathbf{W}_{d_{L}}\psi(\mathbf{W}_{d_{L}-1}\dots W_{2}\psi(\mathbf{W}_{1}x+\mathbf{b}_{1}))+\mathbf{b}_{d_{L}},

(5)

where
\theta=(\mathbf{W}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i})_{i=1}^{d_{L}}
are the parameters of the neural networks,
consisting of weights
\mathbf{W}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}\times{d_{W}}}
,

\mathbf{W}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{W}\times d_{W}}
for
i=2,\dots,d_{L}-1
,

\mathbf{W}_{d_{L}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{W}\times d_{\mathrm{out}}}
,
and biases
\mathbf{b}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{W}}
, for
i=1,\dots,d_{L}-1

and
\mathbf{b}_{d_{L}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}}
.
Our focus will be on activation functions that are smooth and ReLU-like,
such as the softplus, Gaussian-error linear unit (GeLU), and the Sigmoid-weighted linear unit (SiLU).
Specifically, we will consider the following definition.

Definition 1
.

We say
\psi:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
belongs to the class of smooth,
ReLU-like activation functions, which we denote by
\mathcal{A}^{\infty}_{b}
,
if
\psi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})

and satisfies the following:

1.

(Boundedness for negative inputs) There exists a constant
a_{-}\geq 0
such that
|\psi(t)|\leq a_{-}
for
t\leq 0
.

2.

(Non-saturation for positive inputs) The function
\psi
is strictly increasing for
t>0
and non-saturating as
t\rightarrow\infty
(i.e.,
\psi(t)
tends to
\infty
).

3.

(Boundedness of derivatives) For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a constant
a_{k}>0
such that the
k
th derivative
|\psi^{(k)}(t)|\leq a_{k}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

In particular, examples like softplus,
\mathrm{GeLU}
and
\mathrm{SiLU}
belong to
\mathcal{A}_{b}^{\infty}
(see examples in
Section

B.1
).

With activation functions
\psi\in\mathcal{A}^{\infty}_{b}
, we can show that deep neural networks with
d_{L}\geq 2
layers are universal approximators in Sobolev norms.
Specifically, we provide an extension to the result of
[
26
]
for functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
over bounded measures.
Given a bounded measure
\nu
over
\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}}
,
we follow
[
26
]
to define the class of functions

C^{m,p}_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}},\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}}):=\{f\in C^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}},\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}}):\|f\|_{W^{m,p}(\nu,\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}})}<\infty\},

where the
W^{m,p}(\nu,\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}})
norm is defined as

\|f\|_{W^{m,p}(\nu,\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}})}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|f(x)\|_{2}^{p}d\nu(x)+\sum_{1\leq|\alpha|_{1}\leq m}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\partial^{\alpha}f(x)\|_{2}^{p}d\nu(x)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

(6)

Here,
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{d_{\mathrm{in}}})
,
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
is a multi-index,

\partial^{\alpha}=\partial_{x_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{d_{\mathrm{in}}}}^{\alpha_{d_{\mathrm{in}}}}
denotes the partial derivatives with order
\alpha_{i}
in coordinate
x_{i}
,

|\alpha|:=\sum_{i=1}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}}\alpha_{i}
denotes the total order of differentiation,
and
\|\cdot\|_{2}
refers to the standard Euclidean norm on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}}
.
Note that this definition of the
W^{m,p}(\nu,\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}})
norm is equivalent to our earlier definition of the
W^{m,p}(\gamma,\mathcal{Y})
norm when
\nu=\gamma
is a Gaussian over the finite-dimensional space
\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}}
and
\mathcal{Y}=\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}}
is also finite-dimensional.
The original result of
[
26
]
then states that single-layer neural networks with smooth and bounded activation functions
\psi\in C^{m}_{b}(\mathbb{R})

are universal approximators of the class of functions
C^{m,p}_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
in the
W^{m,p}(\nu,\mathbb{R})
norm (we restate this in
Theorem

44
).

In practice, deep neural networks with non-saturating activation functions are preferred. We have the following extension for deep neural networks with activation functions
\psi\in\mathcal{A}^{\infty}_{b}
.
The proof of this result is presented in
Section

B.3
.

Theorem 2

(Deep universal approximation with smooth, ReLU like activation functions)
.

Suppose
g\in C_{\nu}^{m,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}},\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}})
for some
m\geq 0

and
\nu
is a finite measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}}
.
Let
p\in[1,\infty)
,
\psi\in\mathcal{A}^{\infty}_{b}
, and
d_{L}\geq 2
.
Then, for any
\epsilon>0
,
there exists a
d_{L}
-layered neural network
\varphi_{\psi,\theta}

with activation function
\psi
such that

\|g-\varphi_{\psi,\theta}\|_{W^{m,p}(\nu,\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}})}\leq\epsilon.

(7)

2.3
Reduced basis neural operators

Due to the high-dimensionality of the spaces
\mathcal{X}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}
, a naive feedforward neural network may consist of a large number of weights and biases, and can be prone to overfitting.
Instead, more appropriate architectures have been considered for the approximation of PDE solution maps, such as the Fourier neural operator

[
34
]
,
DeepONet
[
38
]
,
and reduced basis architectures such as PCANet
[
5
]
and DIPNet
[
44
]
.
We will specifically focus on reduced basis architectures,
which is a type of encoder-decoder architecture that uses a neural network to approximate the mapping
F
within suitably-chosen
r_{\mathcal{X}}
- and
r_{\mathcal{Y}}
-dimensional subspaces of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, respectively.
This allows the neural network to be constructed as a mapping between the low-dimensional latent spaces,
reducing the required dimensionality of its parameters (and thereby the risk of overfitting) as well as the training cost.

To this end, let us first introduce linear encoder and decoder operators

\mathfrak{E}_{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{r})
and
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{X})
on the input space
and
\mathfrak{E}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{R}^{r})
and
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{Y})
on the output space.
Note that for clarity of presentation, we will assume
r_{\mathcal{X}}=r_{\mathcal{Y}}=r
in our analysis. Cases with
r_{\mathcal{X}}\neq r_{\mathcal{Y}}
follow as corollaries.
We will then consider neural operator surrogates of the form

\widetilde{F}_{\theta}(x)=\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}\circ\varphi_{\psi,\theta}\circ\mathfrak{E}_{\mathcal{X}}+b

where
b\in\mathcal{Y}
is a bias term, and

\varphi_{\psi,\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{r}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{r}
is a feedforward neural network with activation function
\psi
that maps between the latent spaces.

In the reduced basis architecture,
the encoder and decoder operators are defined using orthonormal bases.
For the output space, we consider
r
orthonormal basis vectors in
\mathcal{Y}
,
U_{r}=(u_{i})_{i=1}^{r}
.
The corresponding decoder for this reduced basis is given by

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\xi)=U_{r}\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\xi_{i}u_{i}
for
\xi=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{r})\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
,
where we have overloaded the notation
U_{r}
to additionally denote the decoding operator
U_{r}=\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{Y})
.
Similarly, for the input space, we consider
r
orthonormal basis vectors in the Cameron–Martin space
\mathcal{E}\subset\mathcal{X}
,
V_{r}=(v_{i})_{i=1}^{r}
, and analogously write the decoder as

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{X}}(\xi)=V_{r}\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\xi_{i}v_{i}.

The corresponding encoders
\mathfrak{E}_{\mathcal{X}}={V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}
and
\mathfrak{E}_{\mathcal{Y}}={U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}
can then be defined as left inverses of the decoders.
Note that we will also refer to the size of the reduced basis
r
as its rank.
Evidently, the approximation properties of
\widetilde{F}_{\theta}
depend on the choices of the bases
U_{r}
and
V_{r}
.

Remark 3

(Comment on notation)
.

In the remainder of the paper, unless otherwise specified, we will use
\lambda
,
u
,
U
,
P
to denote eigenvalues, eigenvectors, bases/decoders, and projections on the output space
\mathcal{Y}
, and
\mu
,
v
,
V
, and
Q
for that on the input space
\mathcal{X}
.

2.4
Sobolev training of reduced basis neural operators

To construct operator surrogates that are accurate in the
H^{1}_{\gamma}
norm,
one can pose the supervised learning problem directly in the
H^{1}_{\gamma}
norm.
That is, for a neural operator architecture parametrized by
\theta
, one solves the following minimization problem,

\min_{\theta}\|F-\widetilde{F}_{\theta}\|_{H^{1}_{\gamma}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|F-\widetilde{F}_{\theta}\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}]+\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F-D_{\mathcal{E}}\widetilde{F}_{\theta}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}].

(8)

The incorporation of derivatives in the training loss is termed Sobolev training,
and its application to operator learning leads to the so-called derivative-informed neural operators.
When using a reduced basis architecture of the form
\widetilde{F}_{\theta}=U_{r}\circ\varphi_{\psi,\theta}\circ{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}+b
, where
U_{r}
and
V_{r}
are fixed orthonormal bases of
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{E}
, the
H^{1}_{\gamma}
training problem can be equivalently written in the latent/reduced spaces as

\min_{\theta}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\gamma}[\|{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}(F(x)-b)-\varphi_{\psi,\theta}({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}(x))\|_{2}^{2}]+\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\gamma}[\|{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x)V_{r}-\varphi_{\psi,\theta}({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}x)\|_{F}^{2}],

(9)

where
\|\cdot\|_{2}
refers to the
\ell^{2}
norm on
\mathbb{R}^{r}
and
\|\cdot\|_{F}
refers to the matrix Frobenius norm on
\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
(see the error decomposition in
Proposition

16
)

In practice, the training problem is solved using a finite number of training samples,
which are tuples of the form
(x_{k},F(x_{k}),{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x_{k})V_{r})
,
k=1,\dots,N
, where
x_{k}
are sampled from the input measure
\gamma
.
Notably, the derivative-informed training problem requires evaluations of both the operator’s output values and its derivatives at the sampled points.
Since the reduced basis architecture only maps between the subspaces spanned by
U_{r}
and
V_{r}
,
the training problem involves only projection of the derivative
{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x)V_{r}
.
For PDE-based mappings, this can be efficiently computed using forward/adjoint sensitivity methods (see for example
[
46
]
).

Moreover, the training loss is often normalized to balance the contributions of the output and derivative terms.
This leads to the following practical form of the empirical risk minimization problem,

\min_{\theta}\frac{1}{N}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{1}{a_{0,k}}\|{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}(F(x_{k})-b)-\varphi_{\psi,\theta}({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}x_{k})\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{a_{1,k}}\|{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x_{k})V_{r}-D\varphi_{\psi,\theta}({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}x_{k})\|_{F}^{2}\right),

(10)

where
(a_{0,k})_{k=1}^{N}
and
(a_{1,k})_{k=1}^{N}
are positive weights scaling the output and derivative terms, respectively.
Common choices of normalization include uniformly normalizing by the second moments of the training data,

a_{0,k}=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\|{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}(F(x_{k})-b)\|_{2}^{2}
and
a_{1,k}=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\|{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x_{k})V_{r}\|_{F}^{2}
,
or normalizing each data point by its norm,

a_{0,k}=\|{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}(F(x_{k})-b)\|_{2}^{2}+\tau

and
a_{1,k}=\|{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x_{k})V_{r}\|_{F}^{2}+\tau
,
where, if required,
\tau
can be chosen to be a small constant to avoid division by zero.

2.5
Choices of dimension reduction

2.5.1
Principal component analysis

We now consider choices of reduced bases
V_{r}
and
U_{r}
for the input and output spaces, respectively.
One approach is to use subspaces spanned by the PCA bases.
For the input space, the PCA coincides with the Karhunen Loève expansion (KLE) of the Gaussian measure
\gamma
.
Since we have assume
\gamma
is centered, we simply consider the eigenvectors of the covariance operator,

C_{\mathcal{X}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}=\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}},

(11)

where the eigenvalues are written in decreasing order
\mu_{1}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\geq\cdots\geq\mu_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\geq\dots>0
.
Since
C_{\mathcal{X}}
is self-adjoint and of trace class, its full collection of eigenvectors,
(v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
, yields an orthonormal basis in
\mathcal{X}
.
Alternatively, they can be normalized in the Cameron–Martin norm such that they are also an orthonormal basis in
\mathcal{E}
.
For consistency, we will take this latter approach.

To reduce to
r
dimensions, we take the first
r
eigenvectors as a reduced basis,

V_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}:=(v_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}})_{i=1}^{r}
.
As previously described, we overload
V_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
to denote the decoder
V_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathcal{X})

along with the corresponding encoder (its left inverse)

{(V_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})}^{\!\dagger}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{r})

such that for
\xi=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{r})\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
and
x\in\mathcal{X}
, we have

V_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\xi_{i}v_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}},\qquad{(V_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})}^{\!\dagger}x=\left(\left\langle C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}v_{1}^{\mathrm{PCA}},x\right\rangle_{\mathcal{X}},\dots,\left\langle C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}v_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}},x\right\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\right).

(12)

In particular, when restricted to elements in the Cameron–Martin space
h\in\mathcal{E}
, we have

{(V_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})}^{\!\dagger}h=\left(\left\langle v_{1}^{\mathrm{PCA}},h\right\rangle_{\mathcal{E}},\dots,\left\langle v_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}},h\right\rangle_{\mathcal{E}}\right).

Thus,

Q_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}:=V_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}{(V_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})}^{\!\dagger}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}v_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\otimes C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}v_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}v_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\otimes v_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}/\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}

(13)

defines an orthogonal projection on
\mathcal{X}
,
and its restriction to
\mathcal{E}
,
Q_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}|_{\mathcal{E}}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}v_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\otimes_{\mathcal{E}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
,
is also an orthogonal projection on
\mathcal{E}
.
Here, we have used the notation
v\otimes v=v\left\langle v,\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}
and
v\otimes_{\mathcal{E}}v=v\left\langle v,\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathcal{E}}
.

In this work, we will assume that the covariance operator
C_{\mathcal{X}}
is known and can be accessed.
This is an appropriate assumption in the setting of operator learning for surrogate modeling,
where the training distribution
\gamma=\mathcal{N}(0,C_{\mathcal{X}})
is often known a priori,
and the covariance operator
C_{\mathcal{X}}
is used to draw samples
x\sim\gamma
to generate the training data.

Analogously, one can consider a PCA of the output space
{\mathcal{Y}}
.
In this work, we will focus on the PCA obtained with the centered covariance

C_{\mathcal{Y}}=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[(F-\bar{F})\otimes(F-\bar{F})],

(14)

which leads to favorable properties for surrogate modeling compared to the uncentered covariance.
We discuss this fact in
Section

2.5.3
.
The output PCA basis is then obtained by solving the eigenvalue problem

C_{\mathcal{Y}}u_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}=\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}u_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}},

(15)

with
\lambda_{1}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\geq 0
.
We can then take the dominant eigenvectors,
U_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}:=(u_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}})_{i=1}^{r}
, as the output basis, such that the decoding and encoding operators are

U_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\xi_{i}u_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}},\qquad{(U_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})}^{\!\dagger}y=\left(\left\langle u_{1}^{\mathrm{PCA}},y\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}},\dots,\left\langle u_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}},y\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}}\right).

(16)

Unlike the covariance of the inputs (which is assumed to be known), this covariance is generally not known,
and in practice, is typically approximated empirically from samples. That is,

\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}(F(x_{k})-\widehat{F})\otimes(F(x_{i})-{\widehat{F}})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}F(x_{k})\otimes F(x_{k})-\widehat{F}\otimes\widehat{F},

(17)

where
\widehat{F}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}F(x_{k})
is an estimator of the mean using
N
i.i.d. samples
(x_{k})_{k=1}^{N}
,
x_{k}\sim\gamma
.
We note that this covariance estimator is biased, where the bias decreases with sample size.
We will write the corresponding eigenvalue problem using the covariance estimator as

\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}\widehat{u}^{\mathrm{PCA}}_{i}=\widehat{\lambda}^{\mathrm{PCA}}_{i}\widehat{u}^{\mathrm{PCA}}_{i},

(18)

again with
\widehat{\lambda}_{1}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\geq\cdots\geq\widehat{\lambda}_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\geq 0
.
Thus, we have
\widehat{U}_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}:=(\widehat{u}_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}})_{i=1}^{r}
and

\widehat{U}_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\xi_{i}\widehat{u}_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}},\qquad{(\widehat{U}_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})}^{\!\dagger}y=\left(\left\langle\widehat{u}_{1}^{\mathrm{PCA}},y\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}},\dots,\left\langle\widehat{u}_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}},y\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}}\right),

(19)

as the decoder and encoder corresponding to the empirical output PCA basis, defined in the same manner as before.
The projection operators

P_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}:=U_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}{(U_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})}^{\!\dagger}\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}:=\widehat{U}_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}{(\widehat{U}_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})}^{\!\dagger}

(20)

are both orthogonal projections on
\mathcal{Y}
.

The input and output PCA bases minimize the mean squared reconstruction errors of the inputs
x
and outputs
F(x)
measured in

\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{X}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Y}}
, respectively. We will state this result in
Section

3
.
However, neither the input nor the output PCA bases explicitly capture the sensitivity of the map
F
.
In particular, the input PCA basis is independent of
F
and hence does not account for the dependence of the outputs with respect to the inputs.
On the other hand, the output PCA basis implicitly captures the dependence of the output with respect to the input, as it is constructed using the dominant features of
F(x)
.
As we will see, this has implications for the approximation errors of the derivatives.

2.5.2
Derivative-informed subspaces

We also consider an alternative set of reduced bases that are constructed directly using the derivatives of the map
F
.
To this end, we define operators
H_{\mathcal{X}}:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
and
H_{\mathcal{Y}}:\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
, where

\displaystyle H_{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\nu}[D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x)^{*}D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x)]=C_{\mathcal{X}}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\nu}[DF(x)^{*}DF(x)],

(21)

\displaystyle H_{\mathcal{Y}}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\nu}[D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x)D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x)^{*}]=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\nu}[DF(x)C_{\mathcal{X}}DF(x)^{*}],

(22)

where the latter equalities in (
21
) and (
22
) hold
if we assume
F\in C^{1}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
with bounded second moments on the derivatives, i.e.,
DF\in L^{2}(\gamma,\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}))
, such that

DF^{*}DF\in L^{1}(\gamma,\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{X}))
.
More generally, when
F\in H^{1}_{\gamma}
, the sensitivity operators
H_{\mathcal{X}}:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
and
H_{\mathcal{Y}}:\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
are both self-adjoint, positive, trace-class operators.
Thus, their eigenvectors form orthonormal bases of
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{Y}
.
The reduced bases are then given by the dominant eigenvectors of
H_{\mathcal{X}}
and
H_{\mathcal{Y}}
.
In this work, we will refer to
H_{\mathcal{X}}
as the input sensitivity operator and
H_{\mathcal{Y}}
as the output sensitivity operator,
and refer to the subspaces generated by this approach as the input/output derivative-informed subspaces.

For the input, we consider the eigenvalue problem

H_{\mathcal{X}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}=\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}},

(23)

with
\mu_{1}^{\mathrm{DIS}}\geq\mu_{2}^{\mathrm{DIS}}\geq\cdots\geq 0
,
and take the first
r
eigenvectors
V_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}:=(v_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}})_{i=1}^{r}
as the input basis/decoder, i.e.,
We recognize that
v_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}
are orthonormal in
\mathcal{E}
, and so for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
we write the decoder and encoder as

V_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\xi_{i}v_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}},\qquad{(V_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})}^{\!\dagger}x:=\left(\left\langle C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}v_{1}^{\mathrm{DIS}},x\right\rangle_{\mathcal{X}},\dots,\left\langle C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}v_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}},x\right\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\right),

(24)

where the encoder is well-defined in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{r})
whenever
C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}v_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}
is well-defined in
\mathcal{X}
(i.e.,
C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}v_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}
are elements of
\mathcal{X}
).
We also note that the projection

Q_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}:=V_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}{(V_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})}^{\!\dagger}

(25)

is not orthogonal in
\mathcal{X}
. However, when restricted to
\mathcal{E}
, we do have

{(V_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})}^{\!\dagger}h=\left(\left\langle v_{1}^{\mathrm{DIS}},h\right\rangle_{\mathcal{E}},\dots,\left\langle v_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}},h\right\rangle_{\mathcal{E}}\right),

such that

Q_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}|_{\mathcal{E}}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}v_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}\otimes_{\mathcal{E}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}

is orthogonal in
\mathcal{E}
.

Remark 4
.

For convenience, in the remainder of this work,
we will assume that the input DIS bases (and their empirical counterparts) do indeed satisfy
C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}v_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}\in\mathcal{X}
.
This allows us to define the encoder
{(V_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})}^{\!\dagger}
and projections
Q_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}
as continuous linear operators over the entire input space
\mathcal{X}
.
As mentioned above, this is true if
F\in C^{1}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
and
DF\in L^{2}(\gamma,\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}))
.

The output derivative-informed subspace is found by the eigenvalue problem

H_{\mathcal{Y}}u_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}=\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}u_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}},

(26)

with
\lambda_{1}^{\mathrm{DIS}}\geq\lambda_{2}^{\mathrm{DIS}}\geq\cdots\geq 0
,
and take the first
r
eigenvectors
U_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}:=(u_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}})_{i=1}^{r}
as the output basis
such that the decoder and encoder are given by

U_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\xi_{i}u_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}},\qquad{(U_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})}^{\!\dagger}y=\left(\left\langle u_{1}^{\mathrm{DIS}},y\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}},\dots,\left\langle u_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}},y\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}}\right).

(27)

This again yields an orthogonal projection on
\mathcal{Y}
,

P_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}:=U_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}{(U_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})}^{\!\dagger}.

(28)

As we will show, by nature of their construction, the subspaces spanned by
V_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}
and
U_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}
have optimal reconstruction properties for the derivative operator.

Similar to the output PCA, the operators
H_{\mathcal{X}}
and
H_{\mathcal{Y}}
need to be approximated using i.i.d. samples
(x_{k})_{k=1}^{N}
, where
x_{k}\sim\gamma
.
This leads to the estimators

\displaystyle\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{X}}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x_{k})^{*}D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x_{k}),

(29)

\displaystyle\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{Y}}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x_{k})D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x_{k})^{*}.

(30)

Their corresponding eigenvalue problems are then given by

\displaystyle\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{X}}\widehat{v}_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mu}_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}\widehat{v}_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}},

(31)

\displaystyle\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{Y}}\widehat{u}_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\lambda}_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}\widehat{u}_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}},

(32)

with again with
\widehat{\mu}_{1}^{\mathrm{DIS}}\geq\widehat{\mu}_{2}^{\mathrm{DIS}}\geq\cdots\geq 0

and
\widehat{\lambda}_{1}^{\mathrm{DIS}}\geq\widehat{\lambda}_{2}^{\mathrm{DIS}}\geq\cdots\geq 0
.
The resulting input and output bases are given by

\widehat{V}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}:=(\widehat{v}_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}})_{i=1}^{r}

and

\widehat{U}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}:=(\widehat{u}_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}})_{i=1}^{r}
,
such that corresponding decoders and encoders are given by

\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}\xi

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\xi_{i}\widehat{v}_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}},

\displaystyle{(\widehat{V}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})}^{\!\dagger}x:=\left(\left\langle C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}\widehat{v}_{1}^{\mathrm{DIS}},x\right\rangle_{\mathcal{X}},\dots\left\langle C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}\widehat{v}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}},x\right\rangle_{\mathcal{X}},\right),

(33)

\displaystyle\widehat{U}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}\xi

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\xi_{i}\widehat{u}_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}},

\displaystyle{(\widehat{U}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})}^{\!\dagger}y:=\left(\left\langle\widehat{u}_{1}^{\mathrm{DIS}},y\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}},\dots\left\langle\widehat{u}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}},y\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}}\right),

(34)

which yield
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{Y}
orthogonal projections

\widehat{Q}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}:=\widehat{V}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}{(\widehat{V}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})}^{\!\dagger},\qquad\widehat{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}:=\widehat{U}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}{(\widehat{U}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})}^{\!\dagger}.

(35)

The operator
H_{\mathcal{X}}
coincides with that of the AS method for finite-dimensional vector-valued functions studied in
[
57
]
.
In the context of Bayesian inverse problems with Gaussian prior and noise,

H_{\mathcal{X}}
and
H_{\mathcal{Y}}
are analogous to the diagnostic matrices used in the dimension reduction strategies of

[
15
,
58
,
4
,
13
]
.
In this work, we will extend the previous analysis by formulating these subspaces for Gaussian measures on infinite-dimensional separable Hilbert spaces.

2.5.3
Satisfaction of non-parametric linear constraints

Before moving on to analyzing the approximation errors,
we point out an additional property of interest for the output bases.
In mappings that arise from PDE solution operators,
it is common for the output
F(x)
, i.e., the solution of the PDE, to satisfy linear constraints,

BF(x)=h
, for some continuous linear operator
B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{H})
mapping to another separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, independent of the input
x\in\mathcal{X}
.
For example, these can arise from Dirichlet boundary conditions or divergence-free conditions on the solution.
When using the centered PCA and DIS bases for the output space,
we can ensure that the surrogates of the form
\widetilde{F}_{\theta}=U_{r}\circ\varphi_{\psi,\theta}\circ{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}+\bar{F}
also satisfy the constraint, i.e.,
B\widetilde{F}_{\theta}(x)=h
.

Proposition 5
.

Suppose
F\in H^{1}_{\gamma}
.
Let
\mathcal{H}
be another separable Hilbert space and

B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{H})
be a continuous linear operator such that

BF(x)=h\qquad\forall x\in\mathcal{X},

(36)

for some fixed
h\in\mathcal{H}
.
Then, the affine space

\bar{F}+\mathrm{span}(u_{1},\dots,u_{r})
corresponding to the output PCA and DIS bases with nonzero eigenvalues

\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{r}>0
satisfies

By=h\qquad\forall y\in\bar{F}+\mathrm{span}((u_{i})_{i=1}^{r}).

The same holds when
\bar{F}
is replaced by the estimator
\widehat{F}
and
when the PCA or DIS bases are replaced by their estimators
(\widehat{u}_{i})_{i=1}^{r}
with nonzero eigenvalues
\widehat{\lambda}_{1}\geq\dots\geq\widehat{\lambda}_{r}>0
.

Proof.

Since
BF(x)=h
for all
x
, we have
B\bar{F}=B\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F]=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[BF]=h
.
Thus, it remains to verify that
Bu_{i}=0
for the reduced bases
u_{i}=u_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}
or
u_{i}=u_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
.
For the centered PCA, we have
u_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}=C_{\mathcal{Y}}u_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}/\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
when
\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}>0
.
This implies

Bu_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}}B\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[(F-\bar{F})\otimes(F-\bar{F})]u_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[B(F-\bar{F})\otimes(F-\bar{F})]u_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}=0.

For the DIS, we begin by noting that
BF(x)=h
implies

D_{\mathcal{E}}\left(BF(x)\right)=BD_{\mathcal{E}}F(x)=0.

We then have,

Bu_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}}BH_{\mathcal{Y}}u_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[BD_{\mathcal{E}}FD_{\mathcal{E}}F^{*}]u_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}=0,

which again holds for all
i\leq r
with
\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}>0
.
Results for the empirical mean
\widehat{F}
and bases
\widehat{u}_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
and
\widehat{u}_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}
can be shown by replacing
\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}
with the sum over the samples, where the result holds almost surely with respect to the sampling.
∎

3
Error analysis

3.1
Main results

In this section, we analyze the approximation errors of reduced basis neural operators (RBNOs) in the Sobolev norm
H^{1}_{\gamma}
, where
\gamma
is a Gaussian measure.
We will focus on the case where the neural operator is constructed as

\widetilde{F}_{\theta}=U_{r}\circ\varphi_{\psi,\theta}\circ{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}+\widehat{F},

with reduced bases
U_{r}
and
V_{r}
that are orthonormal in
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{E}
,
a latent space neural network
\varphi
,
and a sample-based mean estimate
\widehat{F}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}f(x_{k})
.
This represents a practical setup, where the neural operator is constructed to fit the (empirical) mean shifted data

F-\widehat{F}
obtained from the training data.

We begin by presenting our two main results,
Theorem

6
and
Theorem

10
.
Their proofs are presented in the
Section

C.2
and
Section

C.6
using results developed throughout the rest of this section.
Our first main result is that the RBNO architecture is indeed universal in the Sobolev norm
H^{m}_{\gamma}
given orthonormal reduced bases
U_{r}
and
V_{r}
of
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{E}
, respectively.

Theorem 6

(Generic universal approximation)
.

Suppose
F\in H^{m}_{\gamma}
for any
m\geq 0
.
Let
(u_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{Y}
and
(v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{E}
such that
(C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
are well-defined in
\mathcal{X}
.
Additionally, let
\psi\in\mathcal{A}_{b}^{\infty}
and
d_{L}\geq 2
.
Then, for any
\epsilon
,
there exists a rank
r\in\mathbb{N}
and a
d_{L}
-layered neural network

\varphi_{\psi,\theta}
with activation function
\psi

such that the neural operator approximation

\widetilde{F}_{\theta}=U_{r}\circ\varphi_{\psi,\theta}\circ{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}

with the reduced bases
U_{r}=(u_{i})_{i=1}^{r}

and
V_{r}=(v_{i})_{i=1}^{r}

achieves the error bound

\|F-\widetilde{F}_{\theta}\|_{H^{m}_{\gamma}}\leq\epsilon.

(37)

Theorem

6
trivially reduces to the universal approximation theorem of neural networks in finite dimensions.
We note that it does not quantify the effectiveness of the chosen dimension reduction strategies and how they impact the approximation errors.
To more explicitly determine the contribution of errors,
we proceed by performing a more careful accounting of the errors arising from the dimension reduction.

Before presenting this result, we introduce some additional assumptions on the function
F

that are used to bound approximation errors for specific choices of dimension reduction.
First, when using the input PCA, we will require the additional assumption that
F
is twice continuously Fréchet differentiable, and derivatives of
F
also have bounded second moments.

Assumption 7

(Bounded second moments of Fréchet derivatives)
.

Suppose
F\in C^{2}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
,
DF\in L^{2}(\gamma,\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}))
, and

D^{2}F\in L^{2}(\gamma,\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}))
.

We will also make use of
Assumption

8
and
Assumption

9

in the analysis of output PCA and input DIS, respectively.
Intuitively, these state that lower-order derivatives of
F
have control over its higher-order derivatives.
This ensures that subspaces computed from lower-order derivatives have controlled error when approximating higher derivatives of
F
.
We note that measurable polynomials of degree
n
satisfy these assumptions with
K_{D}=n
and
K_{H}=n-1
.
This is made precise in
Proposition

43
presented in the
Section

A.5
.

Assumption 8

(Derivative inverse inequality)
.

Suppose that for
F\in H^{1}_{\gamma}
, there exists a constant
K_{D}=K_{D}(F)\geq 0
such that

\left\langle u,H_{\mathcal{Y}}u\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}}\leq K_{D}\left\langle u,C_{\mathcal{Y}}u\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}}\quad\forall u\in\mathcal{Y}.

(38)

Assumption 9

(Hessian inverse inequality)
.

Suppose for
F\in H^{2}_{\gamma}
, there exists a constant
K_{H}=K_{H}(F)\geq 0
such that

\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}F(x)(\cdot,v)\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]\leq K_{H}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}Fv\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}\right]=K_{H}\left\langle v,H_{\mathcal{X}}v\right\rangle_{\mathcal{E}},\quad\forall v\in\mathcal{E}.

(39)

Here,
D_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}F(x)(\cdot,v)
refers to the linear operator
\mathcal{E}\ni h\mapsto D_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}F(x)(h,v)\in\mathcal{Y}
, such that

\|D_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}F(x)(\cdot,v)\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\|D_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}F(x)(w_{j},v)\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2},

for any orthonormal basis
(w_{j})_{j=1}^{\infty}
of
\mathcal{E}
.

Finally, we also note that to ensure the sampling errors associated with the empirical PCA/DIS are bounded, we additionally require that
F
has bounded fourth moments in its output and its Sobolev derivatives, i.e.,
F\in W^{1,4}_{\gamma}
.
This is typical in the analysis of sampling errors in PCA (e.g.,
[
50
,
5
]
).

We then have our main result, with the proof presented in the
Section

C.6
.
Here, the approximation error can essentially be decomposed into (1) reconstruction errors arising from representing the outputs in reduced bases, (2) ridge function errors arising from truncating the input by the input reduced basis, and (3) neural network approximation errors.
Moreover, when using empirical estimators to construct the PCA/DIS, the reconstruction and ridge function errors additionally have a statistical error component.
Similar to the analyses provided by
[
57
,
5
,
32
]

the reconstruction and ridge function errors can be bounded in terms of summations over trailing eigenvalues (
k>r
) of the covariance or sensitivity operators that are used to define the dimension reduction, while statistical errors have a Monte Carlo type bound related to the sample size
N
. Finally, the neural network approximation error can be bounded using the universal approximation result for weighted Sobolev norms (
Theorem

2
).

Theorem 10

(Detailed universal approximation)
.

Suppose
F\in H^{2}_{\gamma}\cap W^{1,4}_{\gamma}
satisfies
Assumptions
7
–
9
.
Additionally, let
\psi\in\mathcal{A}^{\infty}_{b}
and
d_{L}\geq 2
.
Then, for any
\epsilon>0
,
there exist
d_{L}
-layered neural networks
\varphi_{\psi,\theta}
with activation
\psi
,
dependent on the realization of the samples,
such that for
U_{r}=\widehat{U}_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
or
\widehat{U}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}
and
V_{r}=V_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
or
\widehat{V}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}
,
the neural operator

\widetilde{F}_{\theta}=U_{r}\circ\varphi_{\psi,\theta}\circ{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}+\widehat{F}

simultaneously satisfies the bounds

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{N}[\|F-\widetilde{F}_{\theta}\|_{L_{\gamma}^{2}}^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{\theta}+K_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(1)}\left(\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}{\lambda_{i}}+\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{Y}}(N,r)\right)+K_{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}\left(\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}+\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{X}}(N,r)\right)+\mathscr{E}_{F}(N),

(40)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{N}[|F-\widetilde{F}_{\theta}|_{H_{\gamma}^{1}}^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{\theta}+K_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(2)}\left(\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}{\lambda_{i}}+\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{Y}}(N,r)\right)+K_{\mathcal{X}}^{(2)}\left(\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}+\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{X}}(N,r)\right),

(41)

with sampling errors bounds of the form

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{F}(N)

\displaystyle=\frac{\|F-\bar{F}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}}{N},

(42)

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{X}}(N,r)

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\sqrt{\frac{2rM_{\mathcal{X}}}{N}},\frac{M_{\mathcal{X}}}{(\lambda_{r}-\lambda_{r+1})N}\right\},

(43)

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{Y}}(N,r)

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\sqrt{\frac{2rM_{\mathcal{Y}}}{N}},\frac{M_{\mathcal{Y}}}{(\mu_{r}-\mu_{r+1})N}\right\},

(44)

and

\lambda_{i}=\begin{cases}\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}&\text{if }U_{r}=\widehat{U}_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\\
\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}&\text{if }U_{r}=\widehat{U}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}\end{cases},\quad\mu_{i}=\begin{cases}\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}&\text{if }V_{r}=V_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\\
\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}&\text{if }V_{r}=\widehat{V}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}\end{cases},

(45)

where the expectation is taken with respect to the samples
(x_{k})_{k=1}^{N}\sim\gamma
used to compute

\widehat{F}
,
\widehat{U}_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
,
\widehat{U}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}
, and
\widehat{V}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}
.
Here,
K_{\mathcal{X}}^{(i)}
,
K_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(i)}
,
i=1,2
,
M_{\mathcal{X}}
,
M_{\mathcal{Y}}
are non-negative constants depending on the choice of dimension reduction.

Remark 11
.

Not all of the assumptions are simultaneously needed.
We can break down the required assumptions and the corresponding forms of the constants based on the choice of dimension reduction as follows.

1.

Empirical output PCA,
\widehat{U}_{r}=\widehat{U}_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
: Without additional assumptions, we have
K_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(1)}=1
and

M_{\mathcal{Y}}=2\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|(F-\bar{F})\otimes(F-\bar{F})-C_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]+2\|F-\bar{F}\|_{L^{4}_{\gamma}}^{4}+6\|F-\bar{F}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{4}.

Assumption

8
is required for
K_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(2)}
to be finite, in which case
K_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(2)}=K_{D}
.

2.

Empirical output DIS,
\widehat{U}_{r}=\widehat{U}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}
: This does not require additional assumptions.
We have
K_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(1)}=K_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(2)}=1
, and
M_{\mathcal{Y}}=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}FD_{\mathcal{E}}F^{*}-H_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}].

3.

Exact input PCA,
\widehat{V}_{r}=V_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
:
Since the input PCA is exact, we trivially have

M_{\mathcal{X}}=0
.

Assumption

7
is required for
K_{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}
and
K_{\mathcal{X}}^{(2)}
to be finite, in which case

K_{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}=2\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|DF\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}],\quad K_{\mathcal{X}}^{(2)}=2\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D^{2}F\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|x\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}]+\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|DF\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}].

4.

Empirical input DIS,
\widehat{V}_{r}=\widehat{V}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}
:
Note that we use
Assumption

7
as a simple way of ensuring that
(C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}\widehat{v}_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}})_{i=1}^{\infty}
that are well-defined in
\mathcal{X}
.
This can be relaxed by instead directly assuming
(C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}\widehat{v}_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}})_{i=1}^{\infty}
are indeed well-defined in
\mathcal{X}
.
Without additional assumptions, we have
K_{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}=2
and

M_{\mathcal{X}}=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F^{*}D_{\mathcal{E}}F-H_{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})}^{2}]
.

Assumption

8
is then required for
K_{\mathcal{X}}^{(2)}
to be finite, in which case
K_{\mathcal{X}}^{(2)}=2K_{H}+1
.

Remark 12
.

We also note that factors of two show up in
K_{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)},K_{\mathcal{X}}^{(2)}
due to the application of the triangle inequality for squared quantities
\|F-\widetilde{F}_{\theta}\|^{2}
, i.e.,
\|F-\widetilde{F}_{\theta}\|^{2}\leq(\|F-G\|+\|G-\widetilde{F}_{\theta}\|)^{2}\leq 2\|F-G\|^{2}+2\|G-\widetilde{F}_{\theta}\|^{2}
(see the proof of
Corollary

17
).
These can be eliminated if we consider directly
\|F-\widetilde{F}_{\theta}\|
instead of
\|F-\widetilde{F}_{\theta}\|^{2}
, in which case applying the triangle inequality does not incur the additional factor.

Remark 13
.

The sampling error estimates,
\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{X}}(N,r)
and
\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{Y}}(N,r)
, are the minima of two different bounds,
as is typical in the analysis of PCA sampling error (e.g.,
[
50
]
).
These are often termed the
global
bound, which has a slow
\mathcal{O}(N^{-1/2})
convergence rate
and a
local
bound, which has a faster
\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})
convergence rate but only holds when the eigenvalues are sufficiently well separated (i.e.,
\lambda_{r}-\lambda_{r+1}>0
and
\mu_{r}-\mu_{r+1}>0
).
The constant in the local bound can become very large as
r
increases.
We note that both forms of the bounds are pessimistic, and more refined bounds can be derived by using more sophisticated techniques akin to those presented in
[
50
]
for the analysis of PCA, though these often require additional assumptions such as boundedness or sub-Gaussianity of
F
.

The rest of this section proceeds as follows. We begin by introducing the conditional expectation, a key tool that we use to analyze the RBNO architecture.
We proceed to consider the reconstruction, ridge function, and statistical sampling errors in detail.

3.2
Conditional expectation and universal approximation

The reduced basis architectures for given bases
U_{r}
and
V_{r}
are projections (with
P_{r}
) of ridge functions—
L^{2}_{\gamma}
functions
that are measurable in the restricted sigma algebra
\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})
(i.e., constant in the orthogonal complement directions of
V_{r}
).
In
L^{2}_{\gamma}
, the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]
of a function
F\in L^{2}_{\gamma}

is a projection, and hence
\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]

is an optimal representation of
F
in
L^{2}_{\gamma}
when restricted to the
\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})
measurable functions
[
7
]
.
When
\gamma
is Gaussian and
V_{r}
are orthonormal in the Cameron–Martin space
\mathcal{E}
,
the conditional expectation has the representation (Corollary 3.5.2,
[
8
]
)

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})](x)=\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\gamma}[F(Q_{r}x+(I-Q_{r})z)].

(46)

This is used to develop the theory of active subspaces in the finite-dimensional setting in
[
57
]
,
but also holds in the infinite-dimensional setting.

For
F\in W^{m,p}(\gamma,\mathcal{Y})
, when
(v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{E}
, the conditional expectations

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]
converges to
F
in
W^{m,p}(\gamma,\mathcal{Y})
as
r\rightarrow\infty
(Theorem 5.4.5,
[
8
]
).
Moreover, we can show that the Sobolev derivative can be interchanged with the conditional expectation here for
F\in H^{1}_{\gamma}
.

Lemma 14

(Interchanging Sobolev differentiation and conditional expectation)
.

Suppose
F\in H^{1}_{\gamma}
,
and let
(v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{E}
for which
(C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
are well-defined in
\mathcal{X}
.
Then, for the reduced basis
V_{r}=(v_{i})_{i=1}^{r}
and the corresponding projection
Q_{r}=V_{r}{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}v_{i}\otimes C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}v_{i}
,
the Sobolev derivative of the conditional expectation is given by

D_{\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[D_{\mathcal{E}}FQ_{r}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})].

The proof of this lemma is provided in
Section

A.4
.
This result implies that the conditional expectation can be simultaneously used to optimally approximate
F
and its projected Sobolev derivative
D_{\mathcal{E}}FQ_{r}
.
Thus, in developing our approximation results, we can first approximate
F\in H^{m}_{\gamma}

by its conditional expectation with outputs projected onto an orthonormal basis,
and then approximate the projected conditional expectation by a neural network.
To this end, the following lemma is useful, with the proof provided in
Section

C.1
.

Lemma 15

(Universal approximation of the projected conditional expectation)
.

Suppose
F\in H^{m}_{\gamma}
for some
m\geq 0
.
Let
(u_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
and
(v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
be orthonormal bases in
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{E}
, respectively,
for which
(C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
are well-defined in
\mathcal{X}
.
For the reduced bases
U_{r}=(u_{i})_{i=1}^{r}
and
V_{r}=(v_{i})_{i=1}^{r}
,
consider the projected conditional expectation
P_{r}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]

where
P_{r}=U_{r}{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}
.
Then, given
\psi\in\mathcal{A}^{\infty}_{b}
and
d_{L}\geq 2
, for any
\epsilon>0
,
there exists a
d_{L}
-layered neural network
\varphi_{\psi,\theta}
with activation function
\psi

such that the neural operator
\widetilde{F}_{\theta}=U_{r}\circ\varphi_{\psi,\theta}\circ{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}
satisfies

\|P_{r}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]-\widetilde{F}_{\theta}\|_{H^{m}_{\gamma}}\leq\epsilon.

(47)

Lemma

15
immediately allows us to prove the generic universal approximation result of
Theorem

6
.
Moreover, it allows us to focus on the approximation error committed by the optimal ridge function (the projected conditional expectation), knowing that the subsequent neural network approximation error of the projected conditional expectation can be made arbitrarily small.

3.3
Decomposition of the approximation errors

Recall that we are considering RBNOs of the form

\widetilde{F}_{\theta}=U_{r}\circ\varphi_{\psi,\theta}\circ{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}+\widehat{F},

where
U_{r}
and
V_{r}
define orthogonal projections
P_{r}=U_{r}{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}
and

Q_{r}=V_{r}{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}

on
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, respectively.
This form of the RBNO allows us to decompose the approximation errors in the following manner, the proof of which is presented in
Section

C.3
.

Proposition 16
.

Suppose
F\in H^{1}_{\gamma}
.
Let
(u_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
and
(v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
be orthonormal bases in
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{E}
, respectively,
for which
(C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
are well-defined in
\mathcal{X}
.
Additionally, let
\varphi\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathbb{R}^{r})
.
Then, with the reduced bases
U_{r}=(u_{i})_{i=1}^{r}
and
V_{r}=(v_{i})_{i=1}^{r}
,
the ridge function
\widetilde{F}=U_{r}\circ\varphi\circ{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}+\widehat{F}

satisfies

\|F-\widetilde{F}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|P_{r}(F-\widetilde{F})\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}]+\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|(I-P_{r})(F-\bar{F})\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}]+\|(I-P_{r})(\bar{F}-\widehat{F})\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}

(48)

and

\displaystyle|F-\widetilde{F}|_{H^{1}_{\gamma}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|P_{r}(D_{\mathcal{E}}F-D_{\mathcal{E}}\widetilde{F})Q_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-Q_{r})\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]+\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|(I-P_{r})D_{\mathcal{E}}f\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}].

(49)

Moreover, the terms involving
\widetilde{F}
can alternatively be written as

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|P_{r}(F-\widetilde{F})\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}(F-\widehat{F})-\varphi\circ{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}\|_{2}^{2}]

(50)

and

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|P_{r}(D_{\mathcal{E}}F-D_{\mathcal{E}}\widetilde{F})Q_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}D_{\mathcal{E}}FV_{r}-D\varphi\circ{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}\|_{F}^{2}]

(51)

where
\left(\varphi\circ{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}\right)(x)=\varphi\left({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}x\right)
,
\left(D\varphi\circ{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}\right)(x)=D\varphi\left({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}x\right)
,
and
\|\cdot\|_{2}
and
\|\cdot\|_{F}
denote the standard Euclidean and Frobenius norms in finite dimensions, respectively.

Here, the dimension reduction introduces errors of two types; reconstruction errors and ridge function errors.
The reconstruction errors,
\|P_{r}(F-\bar{F})\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}
,
\|(I-P_{r})D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}
,
and
\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-Q_{r})\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}

arise from truncating the representations of
F(x)-\bar{F}\in\mathcal{Y}
in the subspace spanned by
U_{r}
,
and
D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x)\in\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})

in the subspaces spanned by
U_{r}
on the output side
and by
V_{r}
on the input side.
These are independent of the choice of
\varphi
.
On the other hand, the ridge function errors
\|P_{r}(F-\widetilde{F})\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}
and

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|P_{r}(D_{\mathcal{E}}F-D_{\mathcal{E}}\widetilde{F})Q_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}]

are due to the projection of the input to the function itself, and can be analyzed using the conditional expectation.
Additionally, sampling errors are present in the mean estimate
\widehat{F}
, and also in constructing the reduced bases.

Recall that the conditional expectation is the optimal ridge function in
L^{2}_{\gamma}
whose derivative is also optimal in
L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))
.
As previously discussed, we may further decompose the error of the ridge function term into the optimal ridge function error committed by the conditional expectation,
and an additional error contribution due to the approximation of the conditional expectation by a neural network.
This leads to the following error decomposition that forms the basis of the proof for the main result in
Theorem

10
.

Corollary 17
.

Suppose
F\in H^{1}_{\gamma}
.
Let
(u_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
and
(v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
be orthonormal bases in
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{E}
, respectively,
for which
(C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
are well-defined in
\mathcal{X}
.
Additionally, let
\psi\in\mathcal{A}^{\infty}_{b}
and
d_{L}\geq 2
.
Then, for any
\epsilon>0
,
there exists a
d_{L}
-layered neural network
\varphi_{\psi,\theta}
with activation function
\psi

such that simultaneously,
the
L^{2}_{\gamma}
approximation error of the neural operator
\widetilde{F}_{\theta}=U_{r}\circ\varphi_{\psi,\theta}\circ{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}+\widehat{F}
satisfies

\displaystyle\|F-\widetilde{F}_{\theta}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}\leq\epsilon_{\theta}+2\|F-\widetilde{F}_{r}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}+\|(I-P_{r})(F-\bar{F})\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}+\|\bar{F}-\widehat{F}\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2},

(52)

and the
H^{1}_{\gamma}
seminorm approximation error satisfies

\displaystyle|F-\widetilde{F}_{\theta}|_{H^{1}_{\gamma}}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\theta}+2\|P_{r}(D_{\mathcal{E}}F-D_{\mathcal{E}}\widetilde{F}_{r})Q_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}

\displaystyle+\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-Q_{r})\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}+\|(I-P_{r})D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}.

(53)

where we have used

\widetilde{F}_{r}:=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]

to denote the conditional expectation with respect to the sigma algebra generated by
{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}
.

The proof of this result is provided in
.
We now proceed by studying the error contributions individually, and how they depend on the choice of dimension reduction.

3.4
Reconstruction errors

3.4.1
PCA subspaces

We begin by considering the reconstruction errors associated with the PCA bases.
It is well known that the PCA bases yield optimal linear reduced representations.
We start with the input case, where no sampling errors are involved.

Proposition 18
.

The projection given by the exact input PCA
minimizes the reconstruction error amongst all rank
r
orthogonal projections on
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{P}_{r}(\mathcal{X})
, in the sense that,

\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\gamma}[\|(I-Q_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})x\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}]=\min_{{Q}_{r}\in\mathcal{P}_{r}(\mathcal{X})}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\gamma}[\|(I-{Q}_{r})x\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}]=\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}.

(54)

Proof.

We reproduce the proof here for completeness. We note that for any
Q_{r}\in\mathcal{P}_{r}(\mathcal{X})
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\gamma}[\|(I-Q_{r})x\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}]=\mathrm{Tr}((I-Q_{r})C_{\mathcal{X}}(I-Q_{r})),

(55)

which, by Fan’s theorem (
Lemma

37
) is minimized by the projection onto the leading eigenvectors of
C_{\mathcal{X}}
, i.e.,
Q_{r}=Q_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
.
∎

As evident in the proof, the result gives the value of the Hilbert–Schmidt norm of the
exact PCA projection
I-Q_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
when viewed as a mapping from
\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
,

\|I-Q_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{X})}^{2}=\mathrm{Tr}((I-Q_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})C_{\mathcal{X}}(I-Q_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}))=\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}.

(56)

Although a rank
r
PCA optimally reconstructs the input
x
,
intuitively, we can see that it may not provide a good reconstruction
D_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-Q_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})
of
D_{\mathcal{E}}F

since the PCA on the input does not incorporate any information about the mapping
F
.
As an example, when
F
is a map depending only on the
(r+1)
th basis,
a truncated input PCA of rank
r
will always yield a projected derivative
D_{\mathcal{E}}FQ_{r}
of zero.
However, when measuring the reconstruction error of
D_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-Q_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})
in the Hilbert–Schmidt norm
\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})
,
smaller weights are placed on the input directions with small variance.
This allows one to place a bound on the reconstruction error of the derivative.

Proposition 19
.

Suppose
F\in H^{1}_{\gamma}\cap C^{1}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})

and
DF\in L^{2}(\gamma,\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}))
.
Then the derivative reconstruction error for the exact input PCA
is bounded by

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-Q_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})\|^{2}_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|DF\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}.

(57)

Moreover, when
F:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
is (globally) Lipschitz continuous,
i.e.,

\|F(x_{1})-F(x_{2})\|_{\mathcal{Y}}\leq L\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|_{\mathcal{X}},\qquad\forall x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathcal{X}

(58)

with Lipschitz constant
L\geq 0
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-Q_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})\|^{2}_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}]\leq L^{2}\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}.

(59)

Proof.

The approach taken here is analogous to that for the proof of Proposition 3.1 of
[
57
]
.
We will use the fact that
I-Q_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\in\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{X})
to write

\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-Q_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}\leq\|DF\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}\|I-Q_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{X})}.

Therefore,

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-Q_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|DF\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]\|I-Q_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{X})}^{2},

along with (
56
) gives the bound (
57
).
Moreover, since
F
is continuously differentiable, if
F
is Lipschitz with constant
L
, we have
\|DF\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}\leq L
,
from which the bound (
58
) follows.
∎

Since we consider a centered (mean-shifted) PCA for the output,
the output PCA basis optimally reconstructs the mean-shifted output
F-\bar{F}
.
On the other hand, when sampling errors are present,
the empirical PCA basis becomes suboptimal, where the gap in the error bound is termed the excess risk.
As discussed
[
50
,
5
,
32
]
,
the excess risk can be bounded using the Hilbert–Schmidt distance between
C_{\mathcal{Y}}
and its estimator
\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}
.
This is summarized in the following proposition.

Proposition 20
.

Suppose
F\in L^{2}_{\gamma}
.
Then, the exact output PCA basis minimizes the reconstruction error amongst all rank
r
orthogonal projections
\mathcal{P}_{r}(\mathcal{Y})
in the sense that,

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|(I-P_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})(F-\bar{F})\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}]=\min_{P_{r}\in\mathcal{P}_{r}(\mathcal{Y})}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|(I-P_{r})(F-\bar{F})\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}]=\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}},

while the reconstruction error for the empirical output PCA is bounded by
