\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|(I-\widehat{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})(F-\bar{F})\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}]\leq\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}+\min\left\{\sqrt{2r}\|C_{\mathcal{Y}}-\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})},\frac{2\|C_{\mathcal{Y}}-\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}}{\lambda_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}-\lambda_{r+1}^{\mathrm{PCA}}}\right\}.

(60)

Proof.

The reconstruction error for the exact output PCA is analogous to the input case shown in
Proposition

18
.
The excess risk corresponding to the empirical PCA with sample-based estimator
\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}
is a consequence of
Lemma

39
.
∎

The output PCA basis is constructed on the outputs of the map
F
, and hence is aware of the sensitivity of the map
F
in the output space.
That is, directions in which
F
changes significantly
are expected to be captured by the output PCA basis.
However, it is still difficult to derive similar bounds for the derivative reconstruction error using output PCA in terms of its eigenvalues.
For example, we can see that output PCA error is such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|(I-P_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathrm{Tr}((I-P_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})H_{\mathcal{Y}}(I-P_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}))

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathrm{Tr}((I-P_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})C_{\mathcal{Y}}(I-P_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}))+\mathrm{Tr}((I-P_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})(H_{\mathcal{Y}}-C_{\mathcal{Y}})(I-P_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}))

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}+\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|(I-P_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]-\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|(I-P_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})(F-\bar{F})\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}],

where the additional error term

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|(I-P_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]-\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|(I-P_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})(F-\bar{F})\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]\geq 0

due to the Poincaré inequality (
Theorem

51
).

Instead, we can look towards
Assumption

8
to provide a bound on the derivative reconstruction error in terms of the eigenvalues of the output PCA basis.
In particular, the constant
K_{D}
in
Assumption

8

quantifies the extent to which the output PCA basis captures the variation in derivatives.
Intuitively, for operators that are largely monotonic, the deviations
F-\bar{F}
do capture the derivatives well,
while this may not be the case for operators with highly oscillatory behavior.
Note that the Poincaré inequality again implies
K_{D}\geq 1
.
As previous mentioned, for polynomials, the constant
K_{D}
can be bounded by the degree of the polynomial, as shown in
Proposition

43
in
Section

A.5
.
We then have the following result.

Proposition 21
.

Suppose
F\in H^{1}_{\gamma}
satisfies
Assumption

8
.
Then, the derivative reconstruction error for the empirical output PCA is bounded by

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|(I-\widehat{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]\leq K_{D}\left(\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}+\min\left\{\sqrt{2r}\|C_{\mathcal{Y}}-\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})},\frac{2\|C_{\mathcal{Y}}-\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}}{\lambda_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}-\lambda_{r+1}^{\mathrm{PCA}}}\right\}\right).

(61)

Proof.

We recognize that

\|(I-\widehat{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F^{*}(I-\widehat{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})u_{i}\|_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}

for any
(u_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
that is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{Y}
.
By extending the empirical output PCA basis, we obtain a basis
(\widehat{u}_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}})_{i=1}^{\infty}
of
\mathcal{Y}
, which we can use to evaluate the Hilbert–Schmidt norm,

\|(I-\widehat{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}=\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F^{*}\widehat{u}_{i}\|_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}=\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\left\langle D_{\mathcal{E}}FD_{\mathcal{E}}F^{*}\widehat{u}_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}},\widehat{u}_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}}.

Taking an expectation and applying
Assumption

8
yields

\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[\left\langle D_{\mathcal{E}}FD_{\mathcal{E}}F^{*}\widehat{u}_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}},\widehat{u}_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}}\right]=\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\left\langle\widehat{u}_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}},H_{\mathcal{Y}}\widehat{u}_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}}\leq K_{D}\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\left\langle\widehat{u}_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}},C_{\mathcal{Y}}\widehat{u}_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}}.

Finally, the desired bound follows from
Proposition

20
.

∎

3.4.2
Derivative-informed subspaces

We now consider the reconstruction properties for projections defined using derivative-informed subspaces.
Since they effectively amount to a PCA on the derivative operators,
we have similar optimal reconstruction properties of the derivative.
The following proposition extends the results of
[
57
]
and
[
4
]

to the infinite-dimensional setting. It also includes the effect of sample errors,
which is considered for the input DIS (active subspace) in the finite-dimensional setting by
[
31
,
16
]
.
Again, the sampling errors are analyzed in
Section

3.6
.

Proposition 22
.

Suppose
F\in H^{1}_{\gamma}

and assume that for the exact and empirical input DIS bases,
(C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}v_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}})_{i=1}^{\infty}
and
(C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}\widehat{v}_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}})_{i=1}^{\infty}
are well-defined in
\mathcal{X}
.
Then, the projections onto the exact derivative-informed subspaces
minimize the derivative reconstruction errors.
That is,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-Q_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})\|^{2}_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}]

\displaystyle=\min_{Q_{r}\in\mathcal{P}_{r}(\mathcal{E})}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-Q_{r})\|^{2}_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}]=\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}},

(62)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|(I-P_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|^{2}_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}]

\displaystyle=\min_{P_{r}\in\mathcal{P}_{r}(\mathcal{Y})}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|(I-P_{r})D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|^{2}_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}]=\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}.

(63)

Moreover, in the presence of sampling errors, the derivative reconstruction errors for the empirical DIS
are bounded by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-\widehat{Q}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})\|^{2}_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}+\min\left\{\sqrt{2r}\|H_{\mathcal{X}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})},\frac{2\|H_{\mathcal{X}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})}^{2}}{\mu_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}-\mu_{r+1}^{\mathrm{DIS}}}\right\},

(64)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|(I-\widehat{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|^{2}_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}+\min\left\{\sqrt{2r}\|H_{\mathcal{Y}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})},\frac{2\|H_{\mathcal{Y}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}}{\lambda_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}-\lambda_{r+1}^{\mathrm{DIS}}}\right\}.

(65)

Proof.

We start with the input side.
For any projection
Q_{r}\in\mathcal{P}_{r}(\mathcal{E})
,

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-Q_{r})\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\mathrm{Tr}((I-Q_{r})D_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}FD_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-Q_{r}))]=\mathrm{Tr}((I-Q_{r})H_{\mathcal{X}}(I-Q_{r})),

where the interchange of trace with expectation is valid,
since each term in the infinite sum for the trace is non-negative, and
D_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}FD_{\mathcal{E}}F
is assumed to be integrable.
By
Lemma

38
, the trace is minimized by
Q_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}
with value

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-Q_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]=\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}.

The case for the output projection is analogous, since for
P_{r}\in\mathcal{P}_{r}(\mathcal{Y})
,

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|(I-P_{r})D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\mathrm{Tr}((I-P_{r})D_{\mathcal{E}}FD_{\mathcal{E}}F^{*}(I-P_{r}))]=\mathrm{Tr}((I-P_{r})H_{\mathcal{Y}}(I-P_{r}))

In the presence of sampling errors,
Lemma

39
yields the desired bounds for both the input and output subspaces.
∎

We can use the Poincaré inequality (
Theorem

51
) to obtain a reconstruction error bound of the output itself using the output DIS, analogous to the output PCA.
We note that this bound is, in general, not optimal due to the use of the Poincaré inequality.
In fact, the trailing eigenvalue sum for DIS is at most as small as the trailing output PCA eigenvalues.

Proposition 23
.

Suppose
F\in H^{1}_{\gamma}

Then, the reconstruction error for the empirical output DIS is bounded by

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|(I-\widehat{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})(F-\bar{F})\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}]\leq\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}+\min\left\{\sqrt{2r}\|H_{\mathcal{Y}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})},\frac{2\|H_{\mathcal{Y}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}}{\lambda_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}-\lambda_{r+1}^{\mathrm{DIS}}}\right\}.

(66)

Proof.

Since for any
P_{r}\in\mathcal{P}_{r}(\mathcal{Y})
, the function
(I-P_{r})F
has derivative
(I-P_{r})D_{\mathcal{E}}F
,
and mean
(I-P_{r})\bar{F}
,
we can apply the Poincaré equality (
Theorem

51
) to obtain

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|(I-P_{r})(F-\bar{F})\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|(I-P_{r})D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}].

The bound (
64
) yields the desired result.
Note that if we instead use the exact DIS,
P_{r}=P_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}
, the bound is simply

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|(I-P_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})(F-\bar{F})\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}]\leq\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}.

∎

3.5
Ridge function errors

3.5.1
Ridge function errors of the output values

We now consider the ridge function errors that arise due to the projection of the input.
This is studied by
[
57
]
for the approximation error in
L^{2}_{\gamma}
for finite-dimensional input and output spaces,
and subsequently used to derive estimates on DIPNet approximations
[
44
,
47
]
.
In this section, we provide extensions to the case of infinite-dimensional inputs and outputs,
and also provide bounds on the derivative error.

As previously discussed, given
V_{r}=(v_{i})_{i=1}^{r}
, a set of orthonormal vectors in
\mathcal{E}
and its encoding operator

{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}=(\left\langle v_{1},\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathcal{E}},\dots,\left\langle v_{r},\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathcal{E}})
,
the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]

is an optimal representation of
F
when restricted to the sigma algebra
\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})
.
Our strategy is to study how the conditional expectation approximates the function, and then use a RBNO to approximate the projection of the conditional expectation onto the output subspace spanned by
U_{r}
, i.e.,
P_{r}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]
.

In particular, the conditional expectation satisfies a subspace Poincaré inequality, which is shown by
[
57
]
in the finite-dimensional context.
We will verify that an analogous version holds in the context of separable Hilbert spaces.
The proof follows from the Poincaré inequality itself, and is given in
Section

C.4
.

Theorem 24

(Subspace Poincaré inequality)
.

Suppose
F\in H^{1}_{\gamma}
.
Let
(v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
be an orthonormal basis of the Cameron–Martin space
\mathcal{E}
for which
(C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
are well-defined in
\mathcal{X}
.
Then, with the reduced basis
V_{r}=(v_{i})_{i=1}^{r}
and its corresponding projection
Q_{r}=V_{r}{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}v_{i}\otimes C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}v_{i}
,
the operator
F
satisfies the subspace Poincaré inequality,

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[\|F-\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-Q_{r})\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\right].

(67)

Substituting the bounds from
Proposition

19
and
Proposition

22
on
the derivative reconstruction error, we can explicitly write out the bounds for the PCA and DIS bases.
This result is analogous to Proposition 3.1 of
[
57
]
.

Corollary 25

(Ridge function error in
L^{2}_{\gamma}
)
.

Suppose
F\in H^{1}_{\gamma}
.
Additionally, assume
(C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}v_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}})_{i=1}^{\infty}
and
(C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}\widehat{v}_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}})_{i=1}^{\infty}
are well-defined in
\mathcal{X}
for the exact and empirical input DIS bases.
Then,

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[\|F-\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({(V_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}\right]\leq\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}

(68)

when using the exact input DIS and

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[\|F-\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({(\widehat{V}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}\right]\leq\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}+\min\left\{\sqrt{2r}\|H_{\mathcal{X}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})},\frac{2\|H_{\mathcal{X}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})}^{2}}{\mu_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}-\mu_{r+1}^{\mathrm{DIS}}}\right\}

(69)

when using the empirical input DIS.

Alternatively, if
F\in H^{1}_{\gamma}\cap C^{1}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
and
DF\in L^{2}(\gamma,\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}))
,
then

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[\|F-\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({(V_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[\|DF\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\right]\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}

(70)

when using the exact input PCA.

3.5.2
Ridge function errors of the derivatives

We next consider the ridge function error of the derivative term
\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|P_{r}(D_{\mathcal{E}}F-D_{\mathcal{E}}\widetilde{F})Q_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]
.
Recall that
F\in H^{2}_{\gamma}
, the Sobolev derivative
D_{\mathcal{E}}F
is also a mapping in
H^{1}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))
.
Thus, the projected derivative
D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x)Q_{r}
has an optimal ridge function representation

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[D_{\mathcal{E}}FQ_{r}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})](x)=\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\gamma}[D_{\mathcal{E}}F(Q_{r}x+(I-Q_{r})z)Q_{r}]=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[D_{\mathcal{E}}F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})](x)Q_{r},

which also coincides with the derivative of the conditional expectation,
D_{\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]
.
Using this fact, we can apply the Subspace Poincaré inequality on the projected Sobolev derivative,
which bounds the derivative ridge function error by the Hessian acting on the orthogonal complement of the projection
Q_{r}
.

Proposition 26

(Subspace Poincaré inequality for the derivative)
.

Suppose
F\in H^{2}_{\gamma}
.
Let
(v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
be an orthonormal basis of the Cameron–Martin space
\mathcal{E}
for which
(C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
are well-defined in
\mathcal{X}
.
Then, with the reduced basis
V_{r}=(v_{i})_{i=1}^{r}
and its corresponding projection
Q_{r}=V_{r}{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}v_{i}\otimes C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}v_{i}
,
the operator
F
satisfies the inequality

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F-D_{\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\right]\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}F(w_{i},\cdot)(I-Q_{r})\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}],

(71)

for any orthonormal basis
(w_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
in
\mathcal{E}
, where
D_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}F(w_{i},\cdot)
denotes the linear operator
h\mapsto D_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}F(w_{i},h)

Proof.

Lemma

14
implies that

D_{\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[D_{\mathcal{E}}F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]Q_{r}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}FQ_{r}-D_{\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}FQ_{r}-\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[D_{\mathcal{E}}F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]Q_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F-\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[D_{\mathcal{E}}F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\right].

where we have made use of
\|Q_{r}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})}=1
.
By our assumptions,
D_{\mathcal{E}}F\in H^{1}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))
, so we can apply the subspace Poincaré inequality

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F-D_{\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[D_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}F(\cdot,(I-Q_{r})(\cdot))\|_{\mathrm{HS}_{2}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}],

where
D_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}F(x)(\cdot,(I-Q_{r})(\cdot)):(h_{1},h_{2})\mapsto D_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}F(x)(h_{1},(I-Q_{r})h_{2})
takes values in
\mathrm{HS}_{2}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})
.
Moreover, we can write the
\mathrm{HS}_{2}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})
norm as

\|D_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}F(\cdot,(I-Q_{r})(\cdot))\|_{\mathrm{HS}_{2}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\|D_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}F(x)(w_{i},\cdot)(I-Q_{r})\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2},

where
D_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}F(x)(w_{i},\cdot)(I-Q_{r}):h\mapsto D_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}(x)(w_{i},(I-Q_{r})h)
.
Since the sum consists of non-negative terms, interchanging expectation with summation yields (
71
).

∎

In the case of
Q_{r}
coming from the input PCA, we can further bound the Hessian action by the trailing input PCA eigenvalues at the cost of a (potentially large) constant,
analogous to that of the derivative reconstruction error.

Proposition 27

(Derivative ridge function error bound with input PCA)
.

Suppose
F\in H^{2}_{\gamma}
satisfies
Assumption

7
.
Then, for any orthogonal projection
P_{r}\in\mathcal{P}_{r}(\mathcal{Y})
, the exact input PCA satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[\|P_{r}(D_{\mathcal{E}}F-D_{\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({(V_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})}^{\!\dagger})])Q_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[\|D^{2}F\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|x\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}]\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}.

(72)

Proof.

Since
\|P_{r}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}=1
, we have

\|P_{r}(D_{\mathcal{E}}F-D_{\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({(V_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})}^{\!\dagger})])Q_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\leq\|(D_{\mathcal{E}}F-D_{\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({(V_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})}^{\!\dagger})])Q_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2},

such that we can ignore the output projection.
For any orthonormal basis
(w_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
of
\mathcal{E}
, we have

\|D_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}F(x)(w_{i},\cdot)(I-Q_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\leq\|D^{2}F(x)(w_{i},\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\|I-Q_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2},

where
\|I-Q_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}=\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
due to (
56
).
Moreover, we know

\displaystyle\|D^{2}F(x)(w_{i},\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}=\sup_{\|\hat{x}\|\neq 0}\frac{\|D^{2}F(x)(w_{i},\hat{x})\|_{\mathcal{Y}}}{\|\hat{x}\|_{\mathcal{X}}}\leq\|D^{2}F(x)\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}\|w_{i}\|_{\mathcal{X}}.

Let
w_{i}=v_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
. Due to our definition of
v_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
being orthonormal in
\mathcal{E}
, we have

\|w_{i}\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}=\|v_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}=\|C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1/2}C_{\mathcal{X}}^{1/2}v_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}=\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\|v_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\|_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}=\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}.

Furthermore, since
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\gamma}[\|x\|^{2}_{\mathcal{X}}]
, we can write

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\gamma}[\|D^{2}F(x)(w_{i},\cdot)

\displaystyle(I-Q_{r})\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\gamma}[\|x\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}]\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\gamma}[\|D^{2}F(x)\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}},

which concludes the proof.
∎

The case for the derivative-informed subspace is less straightforward.
Unlike the PCA basis, the DIS basis does not guarantee an optimal reconstruction of the input
x
(i.e., not necessarily small
\|I-Q_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}
), and we cannot make use of an analogous argument.
However, similar to the case with output PCA, we recognize that the derivative-informed subspace can also result in a bounded Hessian projection error when the derivatives
D_{\mathcal{E}}F

are able to represent the Hessian
D_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}F
on average.
This is the subject of
Assumption

9
, which directly translates to a bound on the derivative ridge function error in terms of the trailing eigenvalues of the DIS problem.

Proposition 28

(Derivative ridge function error bound with DIS)
.

Suppose
F\in H^{2}_{\gamma}
satisfies
Assumption

9

and
(C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}\widehat{v}_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}})_{i=1}^{\infty}
are well-defined in
\mathcal{X}
.
Then, for any orthogonal projection
P_{r}\in\mathcal{P}_{r}(\mathcal{Y})
, the empirical input DIS satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[\|P_{r}(D_{\mathcal{E}}F-D_{\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({(\widehat{V}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})}^{\!\dagger})])\widehat{Q}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq K_{H}\left(\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}+\min\left\{\sqrt{2r}\|H_{\mathcal{X}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})},\frac{2\|H_{\mathcal{X}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})}^{2}}{\mu_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}-\mu_{r+1}^{\mathrm{DIS}}}\right\}\right).

(73)

Proof.

As in the computations for the input PCA basis, we have

\|P_{r}(D_{\mathcal{E}}F-D_{\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({(\widehat{V}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})}^{\!\dagger})])\widehat{Q}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\leq\|(D_{\mathcal{E}}F-D_{\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({(\widehat{V}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})}^{\!\dagger})])\widehat{Q}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}.

We then use the Hessian subspace Poincaré inequality (
71
), which can be alternatively written as

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F-D_{\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({(\widehat{V}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\right]\leq\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}F(\cdot,\widehat{v}_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}})\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}].

Assumption

9
is sufficient to give

\displaystyle\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}F(\cdot,\widehat{v}_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}})\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]\leq K_{H}\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F\widehat{v}_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}]=K_{H}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-\widehat{Q}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}].

Proposition

22
then yields the desired bound.

∎

We note that, like the derivative inverse inequality in
Assumption

8
, this is again a strong assumption.
We can, like before, provide examples of mappings for which it is satisfied.
In particular, polynomial mappings of degree
n
also satisfy the inequality (
39
) with
K_{H}=n-1
. This is also summarized in
Proposition

43
of
Section

A.5
.

3.6
Sampling errors

We now discuss approaches for dealing with sampling errors associated with the estimators of the mean
\widehat{F}
, covariance
\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}
, and sensitivity operators
\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{X}}
and
\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{Y}}
.
Under mild moment assumptions, one can prove a bound on the expected sample errors.
To this end, we largely follow the approaches taken in
[
50
,
5
]
,
where the authors analyzed the sampling errors for the construction of the PCA.
The following useful result is presented in the proof of
[
5
, Lemma B.2]
,
which we summarize as a standalone lemma and present its proof in
Section

C.5.1
.

Lemma 29

(Monte Carlo error for vector-valued random variables)
.

Let
z
be a random variable taking values in a separable Hilbert Space
\mathcal{H}
with distribution
\nu
and mean
\bar{z}=\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\nu}[z]
.
Suppose that
z
has a bounded second moment,
\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\nu}[\|z\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}]<\infty
.
Then, given
N
i.i.d. samples
(z_{k})_{i=1}^{N}
,
z_{k}\sim\nu
,
the sample estimator
\widehat{z}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}z_{k}
has an expected error of

\mathbb{E}_{N}[\|\hat{z}-\bar{z}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}]=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\nu}[\|z-\bar{z}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}]}{N},

(74)

where the expectation
\mathbb{E}_{N}
is taken with respect to the random samples
(z_{k})_{k=1}^{N}
.

This allows us to bound all the sample errors in expectation. In particular, the usual Monte Carlo error of the mean estimator
\widehat{F}
follows as an immediate corollary.

Corollary 30
.

Suppose
F\in L^{2}_{\gamma}
.
Then, given
N
i.i.d. samples
(x_{k})_{k=1}^{N}
,
x_{k}\sim\nu
, the sample estimator
\widehat{F}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}f(x_{k})
has an expected error of

\mathbb{E}_{N}[\|\widehat{F}-\bar{F}\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}]=\frac{\|F-\bar{F}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}}{N}

(75)

where the expectation
\mathbb{E}_{N}
is taken with respect to the random samples
(x_{k})_{k=1}^{N}
.

Additionally, under the assumptions that the random variable
z
has bounded fourth moments, we can obtain a bound on error of the sample estimator
\widehat{z}
in terms of its fourth moment. The proof is again provided in
Section

C.5.1
.

Lemma 31
.

Let
z
be a random variable taking values in a separable Hilbert Space
\mathcal{H}
with distribution
\nu
and mean
\bar{z}=\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\nu}[z]
.
Suppose that
z
has a bounded fourth moment,
\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\nu}[\|z\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{4}]<\infty
.
Then, given
N
i.i.d. samples
(z_{k})_{i=1}^{N}
,
z_{k}\sim\nu
,
the sample estimator
\widehat{z}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}z_{k}
has an expected error of

\mathbb{E}_{N}[\|\hat{z}-\bar{z}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{4}]\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\nu}[\|z-\bar{z}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{4}]+3\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\nu}[\|z-\bar{z}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}]^{2}}{N^{2}},

(76)

where the expectation
\mathbb{E}_{N}
is taken with respect to the random samples
(z_{k})_{k=1}^{N}
.

We use the above results to obtain bounds on the sampling errors for the empirical PCA and DIS.
In particular, the empirical output PCA errors are considered in
[
5
]
in the uncentered case.
In the centered (mean-shifted) case, we additionally need to account for the error in the mean estimate,
\bar{F}-\widehat{F}
.
The result is a similar estimate as in
[
5
, Lemma B.2]
.

Proposition 32
.

Suppose
F\in L^{4}_{\gamma}
.
Then, there exists a constant
M_{C_{\mathcal{Y}}}
such that
the sampling error for the empirical output PCA based on
N
samples of

F(x_{k})
,
k=1,\dots,N
,
x_{k}\sim\gamma
i.i.d., satisfies the bound

\mathbb{E}_{N}[\|C_{\mathcal{Y}}-\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]\leq\frac{M_{C_{\mathcal{Y}}}}{N},

(77)

where the expectation
\mathbb{E}_{N}
is taken with respect to the random samples
(x_{k})_{k=1}^{N}
.

We can perform a similar calculation for the sample errors of
\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{X}}
and
\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{Y}}
, this time under the assumption that the Sobolev derivatives
\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}
have bounded fourth moments.

Proposition 33
.

Suppose
F\in W^{1,4}_{\gamma}
.
Then, there exists constants
M_{H_{\mathcal{X}}}
and
M_{H_{\mathcal{Y}}}

such that the sampling error for the
empirical sensitivity operators

\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{X}}=\sum_{k=1}^{N}D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x_{k})^{*}D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x_{k})\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{Y}}=\sum_{k=1}^{N}D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x_{k})D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x_{k})^{*}

based on
N
i.i.d. samples
(x_{k})_{k=1}^{N}
,
x_{k}\sim\gamma
, satisfy the bounds

\mathbb{E}_{N}[\|H_{\mathcal{X}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})}^{2}]\leq\frac{M_{H_{\mathcal{X}}}}{N}

(78)

for the input, and

\mathbb{E}_{N}[\|H_{\mathcal{Y}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]\leq\frac{M_{H_{\mathcal{Y}}}}{N}

(79)

for the output. The expectation
\mathbb{E}_{N}
is taken with respect to the random samples
(x_{k})_{k=1}^{N}
.

The proofs for both sampling error results,
Proposition

32
and
Proposition

33
, are given in
Section

C.5.2
.

4
Numerical experiments

In this section, we present several numerical experiments to validate the performance of RBNOs in learning an operator and its derivative.
Here, we focus on operators arising from the solution of PDEs given inputs distributed as Gaussian random fields.
We compare the generalization errors in terms of the
L^{2}_{\gamma}
norm and
H^{1}_{\gamma}
(semi)norm across different dimension reduction strategies, ranks, architectural choices, and training sample sizes.
The generalization errors are also decomposed into their reconstruction error and ridge function error (neural network error) components.
These numerical results seek to explore both agreement with our theory
and the practical settings that are not covered by the theoretical analysis.
In particular, error sources such as the statistical errors in the finite training set for the neural network and optimization errors associated with the nonconvex training problem are not analyzed, but will contribute to the overall generalization error.

4.1
PDE problems

For the numerical experiments, we consider PDE problems posed on 1D and 2D domains
\Omega
, where discretization errors can be controlled with modest discretization dimensions. This ensures that the discretized derivative operators (i.e., Jacobian matrices) can be computed in full for the purpose of generating test data.
Nevertheless, the set of PDE problems we consider feature nonlinear PDEs and non-trivial input-output dependence.

Here, we will let
x
denote the input function,
y
denote the solution,
and instead will use
s\in\mathbb{R}^{d},d=1,2
to denote the spatial variable
Additionally, we will use
\nabla
to denote the gradient and
\Delta
to denote the Laplacian, both with respect to the spatial variable
s
.
For the inputs, we will use Gaussian random fields
\gamma=\mathcal{N}(0,C_{\mathcal{X}})
over
\mathcal{X}=L^{2}(\Omega)
,
with
C_{\mathcal{X}}
taking the form of the inverse of an elliptic PDE operator,

C_{\mathcal{X}}=(-a_{\Delta}\Delta+a_{I})^{-\alpha}
,
where
a_{\Delta}>0,a_{I}>0
, and
\alpha>0
control the pointwise variance, correlation structure, and smoothness of the random field.

Semilinear elliptic PDE

Our first 1D example is the semilinear elliptic PDE posed on the unit interval
\Omega=(0,1)
:

\displaystyle-\nabla\cdot(c_{1}(s)\nabla y(s))+c_{2}y(s)^{3}

\displaystyle=x(s),\qquad s\in\Omega,

(80)

\displaystyle y(0)

\displaystyle=0,

(81)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial y}{\partial s}(1)

\displaystyle=0,

(82)

where
c_{1}(s)=0.0001+0.01\chi_{(0.5,1)}(s)
and
c_{2}=0.1
are the diffusion and reaction coefficients,
and
x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,(-2\Delta+10)^{-1})
.

Steady Burgers equation

For a second 1D example, we consider the steady Burgers equation posed on the unit interval
\Omega=(0,1)
:

\displaystyle-\nabla\cdot(c_{1}\nabla y(s))+y(s)\nabla y(s)

\displaystyle=c_{2}(s)x(s),\qquad s\in\Omega,

(83)

\displaystyle y(0)

\displaystyle=0,

(84)

\displaystyle y(1)

\displaystyle=0,

(85)

where
c_{1}=0.01
is the viscosity and the source term is a profile
c_{2}(s)=(0.025\sqrt{2\pi})^{-1}\exp(-(s-0.4)^{2}/0.05^{2})
centered at
s=0.4

and
scaled by the random field
x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,(-10\Delta+20)^{-1})
.

Linear elasticity

We also consider a 2D problem arising from linear elasticity with a random Young’s modulus.
To avoid overloading notation,
we use
c_{\lambda}
and
c_{\mu}
to denote the Lamé parameters (as opposed to the commonly used
\lambda
and
\mu
).
These are given as

c_{\lambda}=\frac{c_{E}c_{\nu}}{(1+c_{\nu})(1-2c_{\nu})},\quad c_{\mu}=\frac{c_{E}}{2(1+c_{\nu})},

where
c_{E}
is the Young’s modulus and
c_{\nu}
is the Poisson’s ratio.
We then introduce the stress tensor
\mathbf{T}
as a function of the displacement
y=(y_{1},y_{2})
such that

\mathbf{T}=c_{\lambda}\nabla\cdot y\mathbf{I}+c_{\mu}\left(\nabla y+\nabla y^{T}\right),

where
\mathbf{I}
is the identity tensor on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
We pose the PDE on the domain
\Omega=(0,1)^{2}\setminus\Omega_{R}
,
where
\Omega_{R}
is a disk of radius
R=0.2
centered at
(0.5,0.5)
.
The governing equations are then given by

\displaystyle-\nabla\cdot\mathbf{T}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{in }\Omega,

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\Gamma_{L},

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}n

\displaystyle=\tau\quad\text{on }\Gamma_{R},

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}n

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\Gamma_{T}\cup\Gamma_{B}\cup\partial\Omega_{R},

where
\Gamma_{L}
is the left boundary,
\Gamma_{R}
is the right boundary,

\Gamma_{T}
is the top boundary,
\Gamma_{B}
is the bottom boundary, and
\partial\Omega_{R}
is the boundary of the disk
\Omega_{R}
.
Additionally,
\tau
is the traction vector on the right boundary,
n
is the outward normal vector.
The input variable
x(s)
is a Gaussian random field defining the Young’s modulus

c_{E}(s)=1+40\exp(x(s))
where
x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,(-0.2\Delta+4)^{-2})
.
Moreover, we fix
c_{\nu}=0.3
, and take

\tau(s)=\left(\frac{1}{0.1\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left(-\frac{(s_{2}-0.5)^{2}}{2\cdot 0.1^{2}}\right),0\right).

This corresponds to a tensile load with a Gaussian profile applied to the right boundary.

4.2
Data generation and basis computation

We use the finite element method to discretize the PDEs and generate the training and test data.
This is implemented using FEniCS
[
37
]

for the finite element solvers and hIPPYlib/hiPPYflow
[
55
,
43
]

for training data generation.
In particular, for each input sample
x_{k}\sim\gamma

the PDE is solved to obtain the output
y_{k}=F(x_{k})
.
Moreover, given discretizations of the input and output spaces with dimensions
d_{\mathcal{X}}
and
d_{\mathcal{Y}}
,
the derivative of the solution operator,
DF(x_{k})
,
corresponds to a Jacobian matrix of size
d_{\mathcal{Y}}\times d_{\mathcal{X}}
.
This can be computed using direct and adjoint sensitivity methods.
That is, we can obtain a Jacobian-vector product
DF(x_{k})v
or a vector-Jacobian product
DF(x_{k})^{T}v
for any vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{X}}}
or
\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}
by solving the linearized state PDE or its adjoint, respectively.
We refer to
[
46
]
for more details on the computation of the derivatives.

For the 1D examples, we use a uniform mesh with
N_{\mathrm{el}}=256
linear elements.
In this case, the discretized dimensions of the input and output spaces are
d_{\mathcal{X}}=d_{\mathcal{Y}}=257
.
For the 2D examples, we use an unstructured mesh with linear triangular elements,
where the discretized dimensions of the input and output spaces are
d_{\mathcal{X}}=407
and
d_{\mathcal{Y}}=814
.
In either case, we have chosen the discretization dimension to be sufficiently large such that numerical errors in solving the PDE are unimportant, yet sufficiently small so that we can compute the Jacobian matrix,
DF(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}\times d_{\mathcal{X}}}
, in full by direct sensitivity methods.
That is, we generate training and testing data tuples of the form
(x_{k},y_{k},DF(x_{k}))
for
k=1,\ldots,N
, where
x_{k}\sim\gamma
.
For each training run, the training data is used to estimate the covariance operator
\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}
or the sensitivity operators
\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{X}}
and
\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{Y}}
, which are then used to solve the eigenvalue problem to obtain the basis functions.

We note that in practice, for high discretization dimensions, randomized methods such as those described in
[
52
]
can be used to efficiently solve the PCA and DIS eigenvalue problems.
This requires only the Jacobian-vector products and vector-Jacobian products on a small number of random vectors, rather than the full Jacobian matrix itself.
Similarly, for Sobolev training, one only needs to compute the reduced Jacobian matrix,
{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}DF(x_{k})V_{r}
,
corresponding to the action of the derivative operator on the reduced bases
U_{r}
and
V_{r}
obtained from the PCA/DIS procedures.
We refer to
[
44
,
46
]
for more detailed description on practical procedures for computing DIS and derivative training data.

4.3
Neural network architecture and training

In the numerical experiments, we use the Sobolev training formulation (
9
) to train the neural networks using both the output value and derivative data.
The
L^{2}_{\gamma}
and
H^{1}_{\gamma}
components of the loss function are normalized by scaling each data point by its norm,
i.e.,
a_{0,k}=\|{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}(F(x_{k})-b)\|_{2}^{2}

and
a_{1,k}=\|{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x_{k})V_{r}\|_{F}^{2}
,
as described in
Section

2.4
.
We have found that this approach works well to prevent the neural network from overfitting to outliers in the derivative data.

We train the neural networks using the Adam optimizer with a constant batch size of 25 for 3,000 epochs, using a piecewise constant learning rate schedule that is successively halved five times starting at the 2,250th epoch.
The hyperparameters, including
d_{W}
,
d_{L}
, and initial learning rates are selected independently for each PDE problem.
These are summarized in the
Table

2
.

:
Hyperparameters used for the latent neural networks in the numerical experiments for the three PDE problems considered.
Note the width is expressed as a multiple of the rank
r
.

4.4
Numerical results

4.4.1
Comparison of reconstruction errors

We begin by presenting a comparison of the different choices of dimension reduction strategies in reconstructing the output and the derivatives.
To this end, we compute the PCA/DIS bases using a large sample size of
N=20,\!000
,
which we take as a proxy for the exact PCA/DIS.
The resulting eigenvalues are shown in
Figure

1
for the PCA/DIS bases corresponding to the three PDE problems: semilinear elliptic, steady Burgers, and linear elasticity.
Additionally, we present samples of the inputs and outputs, along with a selection of the first few basis vectors in each case.
These are given in
Figure

2
for the 1D examples (semilinear elliptic and steady Burgers PDEs) and in
Figure

3
for the 2D linear elasticity problem.

In the spectra of the covariance/sensitivity operators in
Figure

1
,
we observe that the eigenvalues decay rapidly at an algebraic rate (i.e.,
\lambda_{i}\sim i^{-\beta}
for some
\beta>0
) in all cases.
On the input side, the PCA eigenvalues simply correspond to the eigenvalues of the covariance operator (second order/fourth order elliptic PDE operators).
These decay rates are independent of the input-output map.
On the other hand, the DIS eigenvalues tend to decay at a faster rate than the PCA eigenvalues, reflecting the sensitivity information of the underlying map.
This can be seen in the input basis vectors shown in
Figure

2
.
Whereas PCA basis vectors are global sinusoidal functions, the DIS basis vectors are localized to the regions of low diffusion coefficient in the semilinear elliptic example and
the near the Gaussian peak in the steady Burgers equation example.
On the output side, the spectra and corresponding basis vectors are similar between the PCA and DIS in the examples considered.
This reflects the fact that both the output PCA and DIS take into account the underlying mapping.
Moreover, we also observe that the DIS eigenvalues are larger than the PCA eigenvalues.

Figure 1
:
First 200 eigenvalues corresponding to the PCA and DIS bases for the three PDE problems considered. These are computed using a large sample size of
N
= 20,000, which we take as a proxy for the exact PCA/DIS.

(b)

:
Samples of input-output pairs along with the 1st, 2nd, 3rd, 5th, and 9th PCA/DIS basis vectors for the 1D PDE examples. Top: semilinear elliptic PDE. Bottom: steady Burgers equation. Note the localization of the input DIS basis vectors to the region
s\leq 0.5
in the semilinear elliptic PDE example where the diffusion coefficient is smallest,
and to the region near
s=0.4
that is the center of the Gaussian profile scaling the source in the Burgers example.

(b)

Figure 4
:
Reconstruction errors (normalized by the squared second moments of the test data) in the output and its derivatives for the three PDE problems. Left column: reconstruction error of the output by output PCA/DIS. Center column: reconstruction error of the derivative by the output PCA/DIS. Right column: reconstruction error of the derivative by the input PCA/DIS. Bounds for the reconstruction error in terms of the trailing eigenvalues are shown in the dashed lines. Here, true bounds are shown for the output PCA/DIS in output reconstruction and input/output DIS for derivative reconstruction cases.
For the reconstruction of the derivatives by input/output PCA basis, we do not explicitly compute the constant, but instead the plot shows the line corresponding to
K_{1}\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
and
K_{2}\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
for some prescribed
K_{1},K_{2}
to illustrate the decay rate of the reconstruction error bound.

In
Figure

4
, we present the reconstruction errors for the output (by the output PCA/DIS) and its derivative (by output PCA/DIS and input PCA/DIS) for the three PDE problems considered in this work.
That is, we plot the reconstruction errors,

\displaystyle\frac{\|(I-P_{r})(F-\bar{F})\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}}{\|F\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}},\quad\frac{\|(I-P_{r})D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}}{\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}},\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-Q_{r})\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}}{\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}},\quad

for different ranks
r
,
where each is normalized by the squared second moment of the output or the derivative.
Here, the errors are computed using a test set of
N_{\text{test}}=22,\!000
output and derivative samples.
The corresponding reconstruction error bounds of
Section

3.4
are also plotted for reference.
Specifically, for the output reconstruction by output PCA/DIS, and the derivative reconstruction by input/output DIS, the bounds are simply the trailing eigenvalue sums and are computed and plotted exactly in
Figure

4
.
On the other hand, for the derivative reconstruction by input/output PCA,
the bounds are additionally scaled by a constant (
\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|DF\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]
and
K_{D}
, respectively).
We do not compute these explicitly, and instead plot the lines
K_{1}\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
and
K_{2}\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
for some prescribed values of
K_{1},K_{2}
to illustrate the decay rate of the reconstruction error bound.

For the output reconstruction, output PCA and DIS perform comparably, with significant reductions in the reconstruction error as the rank
r
increases.
The two only differ slightly in the steady Burgers example, where PCA errors noticeably smaller for the lower ranks.
Similarly, the two output bases are also comparable when reconstructing the derivative,
where the reconstruction errors using the PCA and DIS bases are almost identical for the semilinear elliptic PDE and linear elasticity examples.
However, for the steady Burgers example, the DIS reconstruction errors are consistently smaller than the PCA, though both errors do decay rapidly.
On the other hand, when reconstructing the derivative using the input PCA/DIS,
the two approaches perform quite differently, where the DIS bases produce significantly lower errors than the PCA bases.
As previously mentioned, this is to be expected, since the input PCA basis does not incorporate any information of the underlying map and hence cannot be expected to accurately capture the derivative information of the operator.

We also make a few remarks on the reconstruction error bounds. First, we note that for the cases of output reconstruction by the output PCA and derivative reconstruction by the input/output DIS,
the bounds are, in fact, equalities, and match the observed errors.
In the case of the output reconstruction by the output DIS,
the bounds, though slightly conservative, do provide a good approximation of the observed errors, especially for the higher ranks.
For the derivative reconstruction by the output PCA, the bound required the additional assumption of
Assumption

8
, postulating the existence of a constant that relates the map’s derivative
D_{\mathcal{E}}F
to its output
F
.
While this is not proven for the examples, the reconstruction errors do appear to decay at a rate consistent with a bound of the form
K_{D}\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
,
suggesting that the assumption may be reasonable.
Finally, for the derivative reconstruction bounds of the input PCA, we observe that the
K\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
bounds are overly conservative for the 1D example problems. The observed errors instead decay at a faster rate that tracks with the DIS rate,
suggesting that the PCA bases may be serendipitously aligned with the sensitivity of the operator in these cases.
On the other hand, for the 2D linear elasticity problem,
the observed errors follow more closely with the
K\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
bound,
which is a slower decay than the DIS.

4.4.2
Excess risks in reconstruction errors

Next, we consider the excess risks associated with the sampling errors in the empirical PCA/DIS.
That is, we compute the empirical PCA and DIS using smaller sample sizes ranging from
N=80
to
N=640
,
and then compare the excess in reconstruction errors when compared to the proxy of the exact PCA/DIS, which we compute with
N=20,\!000
.
That is, for each empirical output PCA
\widehat{U}_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
, we evaluate the excess risk in the output reconstruction error,

\frac{\|(I-\widehat{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})(F-\bar{F})\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}-\|(I-P_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})(F-\bar{F})\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}}{\|F\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}},\quad

while for the empirical output/input DIS,
\widehat{U}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}
and
\widehat{V}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}
, we evaluate the excess risk in the derivative reconstruction error,

\frac{\|(I-\widehat{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}-\|(I-P_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}}{\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}}

and

\frac{\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-\widehat{Q}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}-\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-Q_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}}{\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}},

all normalized by the squared second moment of the output or the derivative, respectively.
Here, the errors are again estimated using the test set of
N_{\text{test}}=22,\!000
function and derivative samples.
This is repeated over 10 independent runs to obtain results on the average excess risk.
We present these in
Figure

5
,
Figure

6
, and
Figure

7
for the semilinear elliptic, steady Burgers, and linear elasticity problems, respectively.

The excess risk corresponding to the semilinear elliptic and linear elasticity examples both appear to follow the local rate of
\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})
for both PCA and DIS and for both ranks considered.
Moreover, the excess risks decrease as the ranks increase,
implying that the inverse dependency on the spectral gap in the bounds
Proposition

32

and
Proposition

33
is not strongly felt in these examples.
With the rapid decay of eigenvalues, spectral gaps for the larger ranks are orders of magnitude smaller than that of the smaller ranks and should otherwise have a great impact on the excess risk bound.
This suggests that the particular form of the excess risk bound is pessimistic in these scenarios, and that more care is needed in deriving sharper estimates, such as those in
[
6
,
50
]
.

The results for the steady Burgers example is less conclusive.
The
\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})
rate is observed in some cases, while the error appears to follow more closely the rate of
\mathcal{O}(N^{-1/2})
for the output DIS with
r=10
and the input DIS for both
r=10
and
r=30
.
However, we also note that the resulting trends are likely polluted with sampling errors in computing both the PCA/DIS used as the reference, as well as in the estimation of the excess risks themselves by finite test data and finitely many runs.
In particular, the excess risks for the input DIS are on the order of
10^{-10}
using just
N=80
, and hence small errors in the computations can lead to significant variations in the estimated excess risks.
For this reason, one should be careful in drawing conclusions from the steady Burgers example.

Figure 5
:
Excess risks in the output and derivative reconstruction errors for the semilinear elliptic PDE problem for ranks
r=10
and
r=30
.
Values are normalized by the squared second moments of the test data.
For reference, the global
N^{-1/2}
rate is shown in the dotted line while the local
N^{-1}
rate is shown in the dashed line. The filled regions correspond to the 10%–90% quantile ranges of the 10 independent runs.

4.4.3
Neural network generalization errors

We now present experiments in which we attempt to learn the operators and their derivatives using the RBNO architecture.
For these experiments, we train RBNOs for the four possible pairs of input/output bases
across a range of different ranks.
For each rank, we consider training sample sizes of
N=640
up to
N=16,\!000
.
We then compute the errors in predicting the output and its derivative, i.e.,

\frac{\|F-\widetilde{F}_{\theta}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}}{\|F\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F-D_{\mathcal{E}}\widetilde{F}_{\theta}\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}}{\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}}.

The end-to-end neural operator generalization errors are estimated on a test dataset consisting of
N_{\text{test}}=4,\!000
function and derivative samples.
These error results are presented in two different ways.
For fixed ranks, we plot the generalization errors as a function of the number of training samples, while for fixed sample sizes, we plot the generalization errors as a function of the rank.
The results are shown in
Figure

8
,
Figure

9
, and
Figure

10
for the semilinear elliptic, steady Burgers, and linear elasticity problems, respectively.

We begin by making a few general observations.
First, for any fixed rank
r
, both the output and derivative generalization errors
decrease as the training sample size
N
increases,
often continuing until a plateau is reached (i.e a floor value in the error where further increasing the sample size does not lead to a significant decrease in the error).
This likely corresponds to the limit of either the ridge function approximation (i.e., truncated inputs/outputs) or the approximation power of the neural network itself,
as the plateau tends to occur later for larger ranks and the corresponding floor values in error are lower.
We also note that, often, output error reaches the plateau long before the derivative error, and the derivative errors continue to improve with increasing sample sizes.
This is especially noticeable in the steady Burgers example,
where fast decrease in the derivative errors with respect to the training sample size begins
after
the output errors begin to plateau.
The observed behavior may suggest that the neural networks tend to first learn the output values before they learn the derivatives.

We also recognize that, unlike the reconstruction errors,
the generalization errors of the neural operators given a fixed training sample size
do not decrease monotonically with respect to rank, as is typical of bias-variance trade-offs in neural network training.
For smaller ranks, the generalization errors can be made small with fewer training samples, but ultimately reaching the limit of the truncated representation.
On the other hand, the ridge functions can be made more expressive with a larger rank,
but require more training samples to learn an accurate neural network representation.
Thus, for a fixed number of training samples,
there is an optimal rank that achieves the smallest generalization error for the output and derivative predictions.
As seen in Figures
8
to
10
, the optimal ranks are larger when the training sample sizes are larger.
Moreover, the optimal ranks for approximating the output tends to be larger than the optimal ranks for approximating the derivatives, which is consistent with the observation that the derivatives are more difficult to learn.

Finally, we discuss the effect of the choice of dimension reduction.
In particular, the choice of input dimension reduction, which determines the optimal ridge function error, has the greatest impact on the generalization errors.
In most cases, the input DIS significantly outperforms the input PCA in terms of the errors for both the output and the derivatives.
This suggests that despite the fact that the DIS basis does not explicitly incorporate the second-derivative information required to bound the derivative ridge function errors (as in
Proposition

28
), it remains an effective choice of dimension reduction for learning the derivatives in practice.
The differences in errors between the input DIS and PCA do, however, tend to decrease as the rank increases. For large ranks, the generalization errors are likely dominated by the neural network’s ability to learn the underlying mapping rather than the dimension reduction.

On the other hand, the choice of output dimension reduction only modestly affects the generalization errors, since it is only involved in the reconstruction of the output/derivatives.
Across the examples considered, this difference is only significant for the steady Burgers example at low ranks (
r=10,15
) and for sufficiently large training sets,
where the training errors can be made sufficiently small such that the difference between the reconstruction errors is apparent.
In such cases, the two strategies perform as expected—the PCA leads to smaller errors in the output while the DIS leads to smaller derivative errors.

5
Conclusions

In this work, we considered the analysis of derivative-informed operator surrogates
that use reduced bases representations of the input and output spaces and a neural network that is constructed to approximate the finite-dimensional mapping between the truncated inputs and outputs.
The main contributions of the present work are
H^{1}_{\gamma}
approximation error bounds developed for different choices of dimension reduction—namely, PCA and DIS.

To this end, we first presented a universal approximation theorem for generic orthonormal bases, which shows that approximation of operators in
H^{m}_{\gamma}
for any
m\geq 0
to arbitrary accuracy using the reduced basis architecture is possible.
To provide more insight on how the dimension reduction strategy affects the approximation errors, we proceeded with a basis-specific analysis, decomposing the error into dimension reduction error and neural network approximation error.
The dimension reduction errors were considered in detail for the PCA and DIS bases, including the statistical errors associated with the sample-based estimators of the PCA/DIS operators.
The neural network approximation error was analyzed using the universal approximation results for Sobolev norms from
[
26
]
, which we extended to accommodate deep architectures and non-saturating activation functions.
The different results were combined to provide an end-to-end error bound. We note that, of the various combinations,
the mildest assumptions were required to bound the
H^{1}_{\gamma}
approximation error when using PCA for input dimension reduction and DIS for output dimension reduction.
In developing these results, we also filled gaps in existing results for the derivative informed subspaces in the
L^{2}_{\gamma}
case, especially in the separable Hilbert space setting.

We also presented numerical results to validate our theoretical predictions,
comparing the reconstruction errors, excess risks, as well as the end-to-end errors of the resulting neural operators in both
L^{2}_{\gamma}
and
H^{1}_{\gamma}
.
For the reconstruction errors, we observed that the PCA/DIS eigenvalue decay does indeed translate to appropriate bounds in the reconstruction errors.
In the case of empirical PCA/DIS, we observed that the excess risk of the reconstructions typically decrease with sample size
N
at a rate consistent with the local regime of
\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})
as opposed to the global regime of
\mathcal{O}(N^{-1/2})
.
We did not, however, observe the effect of the inverse dependence on the spectral gap (
\lambda_{r}-\lambda_{r+1}
) in our numerical examples.
This suggests that our approach for bounding the excess risk is pessimistic,
and that more refined approaches such as those taken in
[
6
,
50
]

may be necessary to provide sharper and more practical bounds.

When used to construct operator surrogates, we observed that the choice of dimension reduction (PCA vs DIS) is especially important for the performance of the neural operator in both output and derivative learning.
This has important practical implications—an effective choice of dimension reduction allows for smaller rank and hence smaller networks, which in turn requires fewer training samples and lower training times.
This is especially true for the derivative error, which often required more training samples to learn accurately compared to the output itself.
In this regard, the DIS significantly outperformed the PCA for input dimension reduction, since it explicitly accounts for the sensitivity of the underlying map,
while the PCA and DIS provided similar performance when used for output dimension reduction for the examples considered.

We note that several of our results for dimension reduction required additional assumptions. For example,
Assumption

8
and
Assumption

9
were needed in order to bound higher-order derivatives by lower-order ones.
Although the error bounds resulting from these assumptions
are realized in the numerical examples considered,
it would be of interest to identify more fundamental properties of operators that can be used to ascertain the reconstruction/ridge function bounds for the derivatives.
One possible example is holomorphy.
It is known that holomorphic operators are amenable to linear dimension reduction

[
33
,
32
,
53
,
1
,
24
,
2
]
, possibly allowing one to derive favorable approximation rates in the infinite-dimensional Sobolev norms in terms of both the latent dimension
r
and the size of the neural network.

We also note that neither the statistical learning errors nor the training (optimization) errors of the neural network are treated theoretically in this work.
However, both are important for the understanding of derivative-informed operator learning, since many performance benefits arise due to the inclusion of derivative information in the
Sobolev training
process itself.
Thus, we believe operator learning with derivatives remains open to further theoretical and numerical explorations, with many interesting outstanding research questions.

Appendices

Appendix A
Useful supporting results

A.1
Cameron–Martin space

We note some useful facts about the Cameron–Martin space.

Lemma 34
.

Suppose
v_{i}\in\mathcal{X}
are eigenvectors of
C_{\mathcal{X}}
such that
C_{\mathcal{X}}v_{i}=\lambda_{i}v_{i}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then
\tilde{v}_{i}=C_{\mathcal{X}}^{1/2}v_{i}
is an orthonormal basis for the Cameron–Martin space
\mathcal{E}
.

Proof.

Consider any
h\in\mathcal{E}
. Then
h=C_{\mathcal{X}}^{1/2}w
for some
w\in\mathcal{X}
. Moreover,
(v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
is an orthonormal basis for
\mathcal{X}
such that
\varepsilon_{n}:=w-\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}\left\langle v_{i},w\right\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow 0
.
Now note that

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{n}

\displaystyle=C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1/2}h-\sum_{i=1}^{n}C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1/2}C_{\mathcal{X}}^{1/2}v_{i}\left\langle C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1/2}C_{\mathcal{X}}^{1/2}v_{i},C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1/2}h\right\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle=C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1/2}\left(h-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{v}_{i}\left\langle\tilde{v}_{i},h\right\rangle_{\mathcal{E}}\right)

\displaystyle=:C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1/2}\tilde{\varepsilon}_{n},

where
\tilde{\varepsilon}_{n}=h-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{v}_{i}\left\langle\tilde{v}_{i},h\right\rangle_{\mathcal{E}}
is complementary component of the projection onto the span of
(\tilde{v}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
in
\mathcal{E}
.
It remains to see that
\|\tilde{\varepsilon}_{n}\|_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}=\left\langle\tilde{\varepsilon}_{n},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{E}}=\left\langle\varepsilon_{n},\varepsilon_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow 0
.
∎

Lemma 35
.

The inclusion operator
\iota:\mathcal{E}\ni w\mapsto w\in\mathcal{X}
is Hilbert–Schmidt.

Proof.

We use the basis generated by the covariance
(C_{\mathcal{X}}^{1/2}v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}=(\lambda_{i}^{1/2}v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
to evaluate the Hilbert–Schmidt norm,

\|\iota\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{X})}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\langle\lambda_{i}^{1/2}v_{i},\lambda_{i}^{1/2}v_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}=\mathrm{Tr}(C_{\mathcal{X}})<\infty.

∎

A.2
Convergence in Sobolev norms

Here, we provide a supporting result for the convergence of functions in the Sobolev norm
H^{1}_{\gamma}=H^{1}(\gamma,\mathcal{Y})
.

Lemma 36
.

Let
F\in H^{1}_{\gamma}

and let
(F_{n})_{i=1}^{\infty}
be a sequence of

H^{1}_{\gamma}
functions such that

F_{n}\rightarrow F
in

L^{2}(\gamma,\mathcal{Y})

and
D_{\mathcal{E}}F_{n}\rightarrow\Psi
for some

\Psi\in L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))
.
Then,
F_{n}\rightarrow F
in

H^{1}_{\gamma}

and
D_{\mathcal{E}}F=\Psi
.

Proof.

Recall that for any sequence
(\varphi_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
in
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
that converges to
F
in
L^{2}_{\gamma}:=L^{2}(\gamma,\mathcal{Y})

and is Cauchy in
H^{1}_{\gamma}
, we have
\varphi_{n}\rightarrow F
in
H^{1}_{\gamma}
(see Section 5.2 of
[
8
]
).
In particular, this means
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}D_{\mathcal{E}}\varphi_{n}=D_{\mathcal{E}}F
.
It remains to construct the sequence
\varphi_{n}\in\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
using
F_{n}\in H^{1}_{\gamma}
.
For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we will use a subsequence
F_{n_{k}}
such that
\|F_{n_{k}}-F\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}\leq 1/k

and
\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F_{n_{k}}-\Psi\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}\leq 1/k
.
Moreover, by the density of
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
in
H^{1}_{\gamma}
, we can find
\varphi_{n_{k}}
such that

\|\varphi_{n_{k}}-F_{n_{k}}\|_{H^{1}_{\gamma}}\leq 1/k
.
Thus we have

\|F-\varphi_{n_{k}}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}\leq\|F-F_{n_{k}}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}+\|F_{n_{k}}-\varphi_{n_{k}}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}\leq 2/k.

Moreover,

\|D_{\mathcal{E}}\varphi_{n_{k}}-\Psi\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}\leq\|D_{\mathcal{E}}\varphi_{n_{k}}-D_{\mathcal{E}}F_{n_{k}}\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}+\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F_{n_{k}}-\Psi\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}\leq 2/k.

That is,
\varphi_{n_{k}}\rightarrow F
in
L^{2}_{\gamma}
and
D_{\mathcal{E}}\varphi_{n_{k}}
converges to
\Psi
in
{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}
.
By the uniqueness of the limit, this means
\varphi_{n_{k}}\rightarrow F
in
H^{1}_{\gamma}
and
D_{\mathcal{E}}F=\Psi
.
∎

A.3
Trace minimization and eigenvalue problems

In this section, we state Fan’s theorem, which is a result on the maximization of quadratic forms over orthonormal sets.
This is related to trace minimization and is used extensively for the reconstruction error results.

Lemma 37

(Fan’s theorem, as stated in
[
5
]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{H},\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}})
be a separable Hilbert space and
A:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
be a non-negative, self-adjoint, compact operator.
Denote by
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots
the eigenvalues of
A
, and for any
r\in\mathbb{N}
(where
\mathbb{N}=\{1,2,\dots\}
), let
S_{r}
the set of collections of
r
orthonormal elements of
\mathcal{H}
. Then,

\max_{(u_{i})_{i=1}^{r}\in S_{r}}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\left\langle Au_{i},u_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}.

(86)

Fan’s theorem asserts that of all orthogonal projections,
projections onto dominant eigenvectors of a non-negative, self-adjoint, compact operator
A

minimizes the trace of
(I-P_{r})A(I-P_{r})
, which shows up in the analysis of the reconstruction errors.
This is summarized in the following lemma.

Lemma 38

(Trace minimization)
.

Let
(\mathcal{H},\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}})
be a separable Hilbert space and
A:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
be a non-negative, self-adjoint, trace-class operator with eigenpairs
(\lambda_{i},u_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
given in order of decreasing eigenvalues.
Let also
\mathcal{P}_{r}(\mathcal{H})
denote the class of rank
r
orthogonal projections in
\mathcal{H}
.
Then,

\min_{P_{r}\in\mathcal{P}_{r}(\mathcal{H})}\mathrm{Tr}((I-P_{r})A(I-P_{r}))=\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i},

(87)

and the minimum is attained by
P_{r}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}u_{i}\otimes u_{i}
.

Proof.

Note that for trace-class operator
A
and
I-\widetilde{P}_{r}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})
, their composition is also trace-class.
By the cyclic property of the trace, we then have

\mathrm{Tr}((I-\widetilde{P}_{r})A(I-\widetilde{P}_{r}))=\mathrm{Tr}(A(I-\widetilde{P}_{r}))=\mathrm{Tr}(A)-\mathrm{Tr}(\widetilde{P}_{r}A\widetilde{P}_{r}).

For any
\widetilde{P}_{r}\in\mathcal{P}_{r}(\mathcal{H})
, the projection can be written as
\widetilde{P}_{r}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\tilde{u}_{i}\otimes\tilde{u}_{i}
for some orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}
,
(\tilde{u}_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
,
where
(\tilde{u}_{i})_{i=1}^{r}
spans the range of
\widetilde{P}_{r}
.
Then, using the same basis to compute the second trace, we have

\mathrm{Tr}(\widetilde{P}_{r}A\widetilde{P}_{r})=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\left\langle\tilde{u}_{i},A\tilde{u}_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},

which by
Lemma

37
, has a maximal value of
\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}
.
Moreover, this is attained by
(u_{i})_{i=1}^{r}
coming from the eigenvectors of
A
with the largest eigenvalues.
Thus, the minimum of
\mathrm{Tr}((I-\widetilde{P}_{r})A(I-\widetilde{P}_{r}))

is given by

\mathrm{Tr}(A)-\sum_{i=1}^{r}(u_{i},Au_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}=\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i},

which is attained by the projection
P_{r}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}u_{i}\otimes u_{i}
.
∎

When the eigenvalue problem is solved for an approximation
\widehat{A}
(e.g. an empirical estimate of
A
),
the corresponding projection is sub-optimal.
The excess error can be bounded by the difference between
A
and
\widehat{A}
as measured in the
\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})
norm.

Lemma 39
.

Let
(\mathcal{H},\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}})
be a separable Hilbert space.
Suppose
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})
and

\widehat{A}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})

are non-negative, self-adjoint, and trace-class,
with eigenpairs

(\lambda_{i},u_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}

and

(\widehat{\lambda}_{i},\widehat{u}_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
, respectively,
both given in order of decreasing eigenvalues.
Then, given
r\in\mathbb{N}
, the projection
\widehat{P}_{r}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\widehat{u}_{i}\otimes\widehat{u}_{i}

satisfies

\mathrm{Tr}(A(I-\widehat{P}_{r}))\leq\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}+\sqrt{2r}\|A-\widehat{A}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}

(88)

and

\mathrm{Tr}(A(I-\widehat{P}_{r}))\leq\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}+\frac{2\|A-\widehat{A}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}^{2}}{\lambda_{r}-\lambda_{r+1}}.

(89)

Proof.

The proof follows that presented in
[
50
]
in the context of covariance estimation, noting that it applies generally for self-adjoint trace-class operators.
We reproduce the proof here for completeness.
We start by writing

\mathrm{Tr}(A(I-\widehat{P}_{r}))=\mathrm{Tr}(A(I-P_{r}))+\mathrm{Tr}(A(P_{r}-\widehat{P}_{r}))=\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}+\mathrm{Tr}(A(P_{r}-\widehat{P}_{r})),

where
P_{r}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}u_{i}\otimes u_{i}
comes from the exact optimal projection.
We now seek to bound the excess error

\mathscr{E}_{r}=\mathrm{Tr}(A(P_{r}-\widehat{P}_{r})),

(90)

often referred to as the excess risk in covariance estimation.
Since
\widehat{P}_{r}
maximizes
\mathrm{Tr}(\widehat{A}\widehat{P}_{r})
amongst rank
r
orthogonal projections,
we know
\mathrm{Tr}(\widehat{A}\widehat{P}_{r})\leq\mathrm{Tr}(\widehat{A}P_{r})

and so
\mathrm{Tr}(\widehat{A}(P_{r}-\widehat{P}_{r}))\leq 0.

Subtracting this from the excess error yields

\mathscr{E}_{r}=\mathrm{Tr}(A(P_{r}-\widehat{P}_{r}))\leq\mathrm{Tr}((A-\widehat{A})(P_{r}-\widehat{P}_{r})).

Using the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, we have

\mathscr{E}_{r}\leq\mathrm{Tr}((A-\widehat{A})(P_{r}-\widehat{P}_{r}))=\left\langle A-\widehat{A},P_{r}-\widehat{P}_{r}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}\leq\|A-\widehat{A}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}\|P_{r}-\widehat{P}_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}.

(91)

Expanding the projection error gives

\|P_{r}-\widehat{P}_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}^{2}=\|P_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}^{2}+\|\widehat{P}_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}^{2}-2\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\langle P_{r}e_{i},\widehat{P}_{r}e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}.

where
\|P_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}^{2}=r
and
\|\widehat{P}_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}^{2}=r
.
Moreover, using
e_{i}=u_{i}
,

\left\langle P_{r}u_{i},\widehat{P}_{r}u_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\left\langle u_{i},\widehat{P}_{r}u_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\geq 0.

Thus,
\|P_{r}-\widehat{P}_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}\leq\sqrt{2r}
, giving (
88
).

For (
89
), we first note that the bound is trivially infinite if
\lambda_{r+1}=\lambda_{r}
,
so we proceed assuming
\lambda_{r}>\lambda_{r+1}
.
We claim that the excess error upper bounds the projection error as

\|P_{r}-\widehat{P}_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}^{2}\leq\frac{2\mathscr{E}_{r}}{\lambda_{r}-\lambda_{r+1}}.

(92)

Combining this claim with (
91
), we have

\mathscr{E}_{r}^{2}\leq\|A-\widehat{A}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}^{2}\|P_{r}-\widehat{P}_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}^{2}\leq\frac{2\mathscr{E}_{r}}{\lambda_{r}-\lambda_{r+1}}\|A-\widehat{A}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}^{2}.

Dividing through by
\mathscr{E}_{r}
gives (
89
).

It remains then to prove the claim (
92
).
To this end, we begin by introducing the notation
A_{i}=u_{i}\otimes u_{i}
,
P_{r}^{\perp}=I-P_{r}
, and
\widehat{P}_{r}^{\perp}=I-\widehat{P}_{r}
.
We also have the identities

P_{r}-\widehat{P}_{r}=\widehat{P}_{r}^{\perp}-P_{r}^{\perp}

and

\mathrm{Tr}(P_{r}\widehat{P}_{r}^{\perp})=\mathrm{Tr}(\widehat{P}_{r}{P}_{r}^{\perp}).

This latter identity follows from the fact

\mathrm{Tr}(P_{r}\widehat{P}_{r}^{\perp})=\mathrm{Tr}(P_{r}(\widehat{P}_{r}^{\perp}-P_{r}^{\perp}))=\mathrm{Tr}(-P_{r}(\widehat{P}_{r}-P_{r}))=\mathrm{Tr}((I-P_{r})(\widehat{P}_{r}-P_{r}))=\mathrm{Tr}(P_{r}^{\perp}\widehat{P}_{r}),

since
\mathrm{Tr}(\widehat{P}_{r}-P_{r})=0
.
Now, expanding the norm
\|P_{r}-\widehat{P}_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\|P_{r}-\widehat{P}_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}((P_{r}-\widehat{P}_{r})(P_{r}-\widehat{P}_{r}))=\mathrm{Tr}((P_{r}-\widehat{P}_{r})(\widehat{P}_{r}^{\perp}-P_{r}^{\perp}))=2\mathrm{Tr}(P_{r}\widehat{P}_{r}^{\perp})

where we have used the fact that
\mathrm{Tr}(P_{r}P_{r}^{\perp})=\mathrm{Tr}(\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{P}_{r}^{\perp})=0
and

\mathrm{Tr}(P_{r}\widehat{P}_{r}^{\perp})=\mathrm{Tr}(\widehat{P}_{r}{P}_{r}^{\perp}).

Moreover, since

\mathrm{Tr}(P_{r}\widehat{P}_{r}^{\perp})=\mathrm{Tr}(P_{r}\widehat{P}_{r}^{\perp}\widehat{P}_{r}^{\perp}P_{r})=\|P_{r}\widehat{P}_{r}^{\perp}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}^{2}

, we can further expand this as

\displaystyle\|P_{r}-\widehat{P}_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}^{2}=2\|P_{r}\widehat{P}_{r}^{\perp}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}^{2}=2\sum_{i=1}^{r}\|A_{i}\widehat{P}_{r}^{\perp}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}^{2}.

(93)

On the other hand, the excess error is given by

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{r}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}\left\langle A_{i},P_{r}-\widehat{P}_{r}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}\left\langle A_{i},\widehat{P}_{r}^{\perp}-{P}_{r}^{\perp}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}-\sum_{j=r+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}\left\langle A_{j},\widehat{P}_{r}-P_{r}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}\mathrm{Tr}(A_{i}\widehat{P}_{r}^{\perp})-\sum_{j=r+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}\mathrm{Tr}(A_{j}\widehat{P}_{r}).

Moreover, we have

\beta\mathrm{Tr}(P_{r}\widehat{P}_{r}^{\perp})-\beta\mathrm{Tr}(\widehat{P}_{r}{P}_{r}^{\perp})=0

for any
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus, subtracting this from the sum yields

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{r}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\lambda_{i}-\beta)\mathrm{Tr}(A_{i}\widehat{P}_{r}^{\perp})+\sum_{j=r+1}^{\infty}(\beta-\lambda_{j})\mathrm{Tr}(A_{j}\widehat{P}_{r})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\lambda_{i}-\beta)\|A_{i}\widehat{P}_{r}^{\perp}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}^{2}+\sum_{j=r+1}^{\infty}(\beta-\lambda_{j})\|A_{j}\widehat{P}_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}^{2}

Taking
\beta=\lambda_{r+1}

and comparing the resulting expression with (
93
), we see that

\displaystyle\frac{2\mathscr{E}_{r}}{\lambda_{r}-\lambda_{r+1}}\geq 2\sum_{i=1}^{r}\frac{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{r}}{\lambda_{r}-\lambda_{r+1}}\|A_{i}\widehat{P}_{r}^{\perp}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}^{2}\geq 2\sum_{i=1}^{r}\|A_{i}\widehat{P}_{r}^{\perp}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}^{2}=\|P_{r}-\widehat{P}_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})}^{2},

since
\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{r+1}\geq\lambda_{r}-\lambda_{r+1}\geq 0
for
i\leq r
and
\lambda_{r+1}-\lambda_{j}\geq 0
for
j\geq r+1
.
This proves the claim.

∎

A.4
Interchanging differentiation and integration

For the analysis of the
H^{1}_{\gamma}
approximation properties of conditional expectations,
we need to consider the interchange of (Sobolev) differentiation and (conditional) expectation.
We first look at the sufficient conditions for interchanging Fréchet differentiation and integration.
A general result is difficult to find, so we state and prove a version that is sufficient for our purposes.

Theorem 40

(Interchanging Fréchet differentiation and integration)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{1}
,
\mathcal{X}_{2}
and
\mathcal{Y}
be separable Banach spaces,
F:\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{X}_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}

be a continuously (Fréchet) differentiable function, and
\nu
be a probability measure on
\mathcal{X}_{2}
.
Suppose that
F
additionally satisfies the following assumptions

1.

The function
F(x_{1},\cdot):\mathcal{X}_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}

is integrable with respect to
\nu
for every
x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}_{1}

2.

The partial derivative
\partial_{x_{1}}F(x_{1},\cdot):\mathcal{X}_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{Y})

is integrable with respect to
\nu
for every
x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}_{1}

3.

There exists an open set
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{X}_{1}
and a function
g\in L^{1}(\mathcal{X}_{2},\nu;\mathbb{R})

such that

\|\partial_{x_{1}}F(x_{1},x_{2})\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{Y})}\leq g(x_{2})

(94)

for every
x_{1}\in\mathcal{U}
and
x_{2}\in\mathcal{X}_{2}
.

Then, for all
x_{1}\in\mathcal{U}
,

D_{x_{1}}\mathbb{E}_{x_{2}\sim\nu}[F(x_{1},x_{2})]=\mathbb{E}_{x_{2}\sim\nu}[\partial_{x_{1}}F(x_{1},x_{2})].

(95)

Proof.

Let
F_{1}(x_{1}):=\mathbb{E}_{x_{2}\sim\nu}[F(x_{1},x_{2})]
and
\Psi(x_{1}):=\mathbb{E}_{x_{2}\sim\nu}[\partial_{x_{1}}F(x_{1},x_{2})]
,
where both are well-defined by the integrability assumptions.
In particular, at any
x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}_{1}
,
\Psi(x_{1})
is a linear operator on
\mathcal{X}_{1}
, and for any
h\in\mathcal{X}_{1}
,

\|\Psi(x_{1})h\|=\|\mathbb{E}_{x_{2}\sim\nu}[\partial_{x_{1}}F(x_{1},x_{2})h]\|\leq\mathbb{E}_{x_{2}\sim\nu}[\|\partial_{x_{1}}F(x_{1},x_{2})\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{Y})}]\|h\|.

Here, we are using the shorthand
\|\cdot\|
to denote the implied Banach space norm.
Thus, integrability implies

\mathbb{E}_{x_{2}\sim\nu}[\|\partial_{x_{1}}F(x_{1},x_{2})\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{Y})}]<\infty,

such that
\Psi(x_{1})\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{Y})
for any
x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}_{1}
.

Consider now the residual for
h\in\mathcal{X}_{1}
,

\frac{\|R(x_{1},h)\|}{\|h\|}:=\frac{\|F_{1}(x_{1}+h)-F_{1}(x_{1})-\Psi(x_{1})h\|}{\|h\|},

as
h\rightarrow 0
.
Combining the terms inside the expectation, we have

\frac{\|R(x_{1},h)\|}{\|h\|}=\frac{\|\mathbb{E}_{x_{2}\sim\nu}[F(x_{1}+h,x_{2})-F(x_{1},x_{2})-\partial_{x_{1}}F(x_{1},x_{2})h]\|}{\|h\|}.

Again, by Bochner integrability, we can bring the norm inside the expectation

\frac{\|R(x_{1},h)\|}{\|h\|}\leq\mathbb{E}_{x_{2}\sim\nu}\left[\frac{\|F(x_{1}+h,x_{2})-F(x_{1},x_{2})-\partial_{x_{1}}F(x_{1},x_{2})h\|}{\|h\|}\right].

Since
F
is differentiable, the integrand

\frac{\|R_{1}(x_{1},x_{2},h)\|}{\|h\|}:=\frac{\|F(x_{1}+h,x_{2})-F(x_{1},x_{2})-\partial_{x_{1}}F(x_{1},x_{2})h\|}{\|h\|}\rightarrow 0

as
h\rightarrow 0
for every
x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}_{1}
and
x_{2}\in\mathcal{X}_{2}
.
It remains to bound this by an
L^{1}
function to apply the dominated convergence theorem.
Applying the triangle inequality and the mean value theorem gives

\frac{\|R_{1}(x_{1},x_{2},h)\|}{\|h\|}\leq\frac{\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|\partial_{x_{1}}(F(x_{1}+th,x_{2}))\|\|h\|+\|\partial_{x_{1}}F(x_{1},x_{2})\|\|h\|}{\|h\|}.

For
x_{1}\in\mathcal{U}
and
h
sufficiently small, we have
x_{1}+th\in\mathcal{U}
for all
t\in[0,1]
.
This implies

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|\partial_{x_{1}}(F(x_{1}+th,x_{2}))\|\leq g(x_{2})
.
Thus,

\frac{\|R_{1}(x_{1},x_{2},h)\|}{\|h\|}\leq 2g(x_{2}).
