Applying the dominant convergence theorem thus gives
\|R(x_{1},h)\|/\|h\|\rightarrow 0
,
and so
DF_{1}(x_{1})=\Psi(x)=\mathbb{E}_{x_{2}\sim\nu}[D_{x_{1}}F(x_{1},x_{2})]
.
∎

Using
Theorem

40
, we can proceed to consider conditional expectations for functions
in
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
.

Lemma 41

(Interchanging Fréchet differentiation and conditional expectation)
.

Suppose
F\in\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}

and let
(v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
be an orthonormal basis of the Cameron–Martin space
\mathcal{E}
for which
(C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
are well-defined in
\mathcal{X}
.
Then, for the reduced basis
V_{r}=(v_{i})_{i=1}^{r}
and its corresponding projection
Q_{r}=V_{r}{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}
, the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]\in C^{\infty}_{b}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
,
and its derivatives are given by

D^{k}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[D^{k}F(Q_{r}\cdot,\dots,Q_{r}\cdot)|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})],

where
D^{k}F(x)(Q_{r}\cdot,\dots,Q_{r}\cdot):(h_{1},\dots,h_{k})\mapsto D^{k}F(x)(Q_{r}h_{1},\dots,Q_{r}h_{k})
.

Proof.

We start with the first derivative. The conditional expectation is given by

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})](x)=\int F(Q_{r}x+(I-Q_{r})y)\gamma(dy).

First, since the derivatives of
F\in\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
are continuous and of finite rank, the mappings
x\mapsto D^{k}F(x)
take values in a separable subspace of
\mathcal{L}_{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
and hence are Bochner measurable.
By boundedness of
F
and its derivatives, the function
(x,y)\mapsto F(Q_{r}x+(I-Q_{r})y)
satisfies the hypotheses of
Theorem

40
.
We can therefore interchange differentiation and integration to obtain

D\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})](x)=\int DF(Q_{r}x+(I-Q_{r})y)Q_{r}\gamma(dy)=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[DFQ_{r}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})].

Let
L_{k}
denote the bound on
\|D^{k}F(x)\|_{\mathcal{L}_{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}
. To see boundedness of the derivative, we have

\|D\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})](x)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}\leq\int\|DF(Q_{r}x+(I-Q_{r})y)Q_{r}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}\gamma(dy)\leq L_{1}.

On the other hand, to see continuity, we have

\displaystyle\|D\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})](x_{2})-D\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})(x_{1})]\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\int\|DF(Q_{r}x_{2}+(I-Q_{r})y)Q_{r}-DF(Q_{r}x_{1}+(I-Q_{r})y)Q_{r}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}\gamma(dy)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\int\|DF(Q_{r}x_{2}+(I-Q_{r})y)-DF(Q_{r}x_{1}+(I-Q_{r})y)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}\gamma(dy)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\int L_{2}\|x_{2}-x_{1}\|_{\mathcal{X}}\;\gamma(dy)

\displaystyle\qquad=L_{2}\|x_{2}-x_{1}\|_{\mathcal{X}},

where the last inequality follows from the mean value theorem and boundedness of the second derivative.
Higher-order derivatives follow by induction,
starting from the hypothesis that

D^{k}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[D^{k}F(Q_{r}\cdot,\dots,Q_{r}\cdot)|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]

for some
k\geq 1
.
Then, we can view
x\mapsto D^{k}F(x)(Q_{r}\cdot,\dots,Q_{r}\cdot)
as another separably-valued map with bounded derivatives,
for which the same arguments can be used to show

D\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[D^{k}F(Q_{r}\cdot,\dots,Q_{r}\cdot)|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[D^{k+1}F(Q_{r}\cdot,\dots,Q_{r}\cdot)|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})].

Thus,
\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]
has continuous and bounded
k
th order derivatives for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

∎

Finally, the result can be extended to Sobolev differentiation of functions in
H^{1}_{\gamma}
through a density argument, giving us
Lemma

14
.

Proof.

(of
Lemma

14
)
By the density argument, we can find a sequence of
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
functions,
(F_{n})_{i=1}^{\infty}
convergent to
F
in
H^{1}_{\gamma}
.
This also means
\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F_{n}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]
converges to
\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]
in
L^{2}_{\gamma}
,
since

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F_{n}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]-\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad=\int\left\|\int F_{n}(Q_{r}x+(I-Q_{r})y)-F(Q_{r}x+(I-Q_{r})y)\gamma(dy)\right\|^{2}\gamma(dx)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\iint\|F_{n}(Q_{r}x+(I-Q_{r})y)-F(Q_{r}x+(I-Q_{r})y)\|^{2}\gamma(dy)\gamma(dx)

\displaystyle\qquad=\|F_{n}-F\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}.

Since
F_{n}\in\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
we can interchange the integration and Fréchet differentiation as in
Lemma

41
, such that

D_{\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F_{n}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[D_{\mathcal{E}}F_{n}Q_{r}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]
.
Moreover, we can verify
\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[D_{\mathcal{E}}F_{n}Q_{r}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]
converges to
\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[D_{\mathcal{E}}FQ_{r}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]
, since

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[

\displaystyle D_{\mathcal{E}}F_{n}Q_{r}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]-\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[D_{\mathcal{E}}FQ_{r}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int\left\|\int D_{\mathcal{E}}F_{n}(Q_{r}x+(I-Q_{r})y)-D_{\mathcal{E}}F(Q_{r}x+(I-Q_{r})y)Q_{r}\gamma(dy)\right\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\gamma(dx)

\displaystyle\leq\iint\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F_{n}(Q_{r}x+(I-Q_{r})y)-D_{\mathcal{E}}F(Q_{r}x+(I-Q_{r})y)\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\|Q_{r}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})}^{2}\gamma(dy)\gamma(dx)

\displaystyle=\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F_{n}-D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}.

Applying
Lemma

36
, we obtain
D_{\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[D_{\mathcal{E}}FQ_{r}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]
.
∎

A corollary of this result is that the conditional expectation preserves the

H^{m}_{\gamma}
norm.

Corollary 42
.

Suppose
F\in H^{m}_{\gamma}

and let
(v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
be an orthonormal basis of the Cameron–Martin space
\mathcal{E}
for which
(C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
are well-defined in
\mathcal{X}
.
Then, for the reduced basis
V_{r}=(v_{i})_{i=1}^{r}
, we have
\|\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{H^{m}_{\gamma}}\leq\|F\|_{H^{m}_{\gamma}}
.

Proof.

As previously shown, the conditional expectation preserves the
L^{2}_{\gamma}
norm.
Moreover, repeatedly applying the previous lemma yields

D_{\mathcal{E}}^{k}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[D_{\mathcal{E}}^{k}F(Q_{r}(\cdot),\dots,Q_{r}(\cdot))|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})].

Thus, applying the result for
L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}_{k}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))
yields

\displaystyle\|D_{\mathcal{E}}^{k}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}_{k}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[D_{\mathcal{E}}^{k}F(Q_{r}(\cdot),\dots,Q_{r}(\cdot))|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}_{k}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|D_{\mathcal{E}}^{k}F(Q_{r}(\cdot),\dots,Q_{r}(\cdot))\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}_{k}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|D_{\mathcal{E}}^{k}F\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,,\mathrm{HS}_{k}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2},

which holds for any
k\leq m
.
∎

A.5
Example: Polynomial forms

In this section, we show that
n
th order polynomial forms on
\mathcal{X}
satisfy the derivative inverse inequality (
Assumption

8
) and the Hessian inverse inequality (
Assumption

9
) with constants
K_{D}=n
and
K_{H}=n-1
.
Let
H_{n}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
denote the
n
th Hermite polynomial, defined for
n=0,1,\dots
by

H_{n}(x)=\frac{(-1)^{n}}{\sqrt{n!}}\exp\left(\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right)\frac{d^{n}}{dx^{n}}\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right).

(96)

With this choice of normalization, the Hermite polynomials form an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\gamma_{1})
where
\gamma_{1}
is the standard Gaussian measure on
\mathbb{R}
.
Moreover, one has
H_{n}^{\prime}(x)=\sqrt{n}H_{n-1}(x)
, which implies that the derivatives of Hermite polynomials are also orthogonal under the
L^{2}(\gamma_{1})
inner product.

We can also define multivariate Hermite polynomials as tensor product of univariate Hermite polynomials.
To this end, for a multi-index
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\dots)
,
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
,
let us define
|\alpha|=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}
and
\mathrm{supp}\,\alpha=\{i\in\mathbb{N}:\alpha_{i}\neq 0\}
.
Then, for
\alpha\in\Lambda:=\{\alpha:|\alpha|<\infty\}
, we define

H_{\alpha}(x)=\prod_{i\in\mathrm{supp}\,\alpha}H_{\alpha_{i}}(x_{i}).

(97)

In the case of separable infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
,
the family of Hermite polynomials
H_{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\Lambda
, along with an orthonormal basis of the Cameron–Martin space
\mathcal{E}
, can be used to construct an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}_{\gamma}=L^{2}(\gamma,\mathcal{Y})
.
For example, given the PCA basis
(v_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}})_{i=1}^{\infty}
(normalized in
\mathcal{E}
),
one defines the basis functions
H_{\gamma,\alpha}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,

H_{\gamma,\alpha}(x):=\prod_{i\in\mathrm{supp}\,\alpha}H_{\alpha}\left(\left\langle x,C_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}v_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\right).

(98)

Any function
F\in L^{2}_{\gamma}
can then be represented as

F(x)=\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda}c_{\alpha}H_{\gamma,\alpha}(x)

(99)

with coefficients
c_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{Y}
,
which is known as the Wiener Chaos expansion.
Moreover,

\|F\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}=\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda}\|c_{\alpha}\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}.

(100)

Note that the derivatives of
H_{\gamma,\alpha}
along
v_{k}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
, which are orthonormal in
\mathcal{E}
, are given by

D_{\mathcal{E}}H_{\gamma,\alpha}(x)v_{k}^{\mathrm{PCA}}=\sqrt{\alpha_{k}}H_{\gamma,\alpha-e_{k}}(x),

(101)

where the multi-index
\alpha-e_{k}
is defined as

(\alpha-e_{k})_{j}=\begin{cases}\max(\alpha_{j}-1,0)&j=k,\\
\alpha_{j}&j\neq k.\end{cases}

(102)

This means that

\|D_{\mathcal{E}}H_{\gamma,\alpha}(x)\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}\int H_{\gamma,\alpha-e_{k}}(x)^{2}\gamma(dx)=|\alpha|\|H_{\gamma,\alpha}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}=|\alpha|

(103)

We refer to
[
42
, Chapter 1]
for a more complete exposition.

Proposition 43
.

Let
F\in L^{2}_{\gamma}
be represented by Hermite polynomials up to degree
n
,

F(x)=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq n}c_{\alpha}H_{\gamma,\alpha}(x).

Then,
F
satisfies the derivative inverse inequality (
8
) with constant
K_{D}=n
and the Hessian inverse inequality (
39
) with constant
K_{H}=n-1
.

Proof.

We first note that since the Wiener Chaos expansion for
F
is of finite polynomial order,
F\in H^{k}_{\gamma}
for any
k\geq 0
.
We start by considering the derivative inverse inequality.
For
F=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq n}c_{\alpha}H_{\gamma,\alpha}
,
its mean shifted form is given by

F-\bar{F}=\sum_{1\leq|\alpha|\leq n}c_{\alpha}H_{\gamma,\alpha}

Then, given any
u\in\mathcal{Y}
,
we consider the function
u^{*}\circ(F-\bar{F})
, where
u^{*}\in\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
is defined as
u^{*}(y)=\left\langle y,u\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}}
.
This has the representation

u^{*}\circ(F-\bar{F})=\sum_{1\leq|\alpha|\leq n}\left\langle u,c_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}}H_{\gamma,\alpha}
.
Now,

\left\langle u,H_{\mathcal{Y}}u\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}(u^{*}\circ F)\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}(u^{*}\circ(F-\bar{F}))\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}].

Analogous to (
103
),

[
42
, Proposition 1.2.2]
gives

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}(u^{*}\circ(F-\bar{F}))\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]\leq n\|u^{*}\circ(F-\bar{F})\|_{L^{2}(\gamma)}^{2}=n\left\langle u,C_{\mathcal{Y}}u\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}},

which shows that
K_{D}=n
.

The Hessian inverse inequality is shown similarly.
We first note that the
D_{\mathcal{E}}F
has a Wiener chaos expansion of degree
n-1
,

D_{\mathcal{E}}F=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq n-1}A_{\alpha}H_{\gamma,\alpha},

where
A_{\alpha}\in\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})
.
Moreover, let
G:=D_{\mathcal{E}}Fv=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq n-1}A_{\alpha}vH_{\gamma,\alpha}

for which

[
42
, Proposition 1.2.2]
again gives

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}(D_{\mathcal{E}}Fv)\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}G\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]\leq(n-1)\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|G\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}]=(n-1)\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}Fv\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}].

Thus,
K_{H}=n-1
.
∎

Appendix B
Universal approximation theorem in Sobolev norms

B.1
Original theorem of Hornik

Hornik
[
26
]
proved a universal approximation of functions using a single-layered, arbitrary-width neural network in any weighted Sobolev norm on finite-dimensional input and output spaces with a finite measure
\nu
.
For
f\in C^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
, this Sobolev norm is
defined in
[
26
]
as

\|f\|_{W^{m,p}(\nu,\mathbb{R})}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f(x)|^{p}d\nu(x)+\sum_{1\leq|\alpha|_{1}\leq m}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\partial^{\alpha}f(x)|^{p}d\nu(x)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},

(104)

where we are using the multiindex notation for derivatives, with
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{d})
and

\partial^{\alpha}f(x)=\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|}f}{\partial x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\dots\partial x_{d}^{\alpha_{d}}}(x).

Note that for
p=2
this corresponds to the use of Frobenius norms on the derivatives
D^{k}f
,
k\leq m
.

The original result
[
26
, Theorem 4]

considers activation functions that are non-constant with bounded value and derivatives up to some order
m\geq 0
,
and states that single-layered neural networks with such activation functions are
universal approximators of the space

C^{m,p}_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}}):=\{F\in C^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}}):\|g\|_{W^{m,p}(\nu,\mathbb{R})}<\infty\}.

We paraphrase this result as follows.

Theorem 44

(Universal approximation with smooth, bounded activation functions)
.

Suppose
\nu
is a finite measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}}
and

g\in C^{m,p}_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}})
for some
m\geq 0
,
p\geq 1
.
Let
\psi\in C_{b}^{m}(\mathbb{R})
be a non-constant activation function.
Then, for any
\epsilon>0
,
there exists a single-layered neural network
\varphi_{\psi,\theta}
with activation function
\psi

and some width
d_{W}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\|g-\varphi_{\psi,\theta}\|_{W^{m,p}(\nu,\mathbb{R})}\leq\epsilon,

(105)

where

\varphi_{\psi,\theta}(x)=\mathbf{W}_{2}\psi(\mathbf{W}_{1}+\mathbf{b}_{1})+\mathbf{b}_{2}

with weights and biases

\mathbf{W}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}\times d_{W}}
,
\mathbf{W}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{W}\times 1}
,

\mathbf{b}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{W}}
and
\mathbf{b}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
.

B.2
Extension to smooth ReLU-like activation functions

In practice, smooth, non-saturating activation functions, such as GeLU, softplus, and SiLU, are often used.
We can extend the universal approximation theorem of
[
26
]
to include these activation functions by introducing a second layer into the neural network.

We will extend the notion of
C^{m,p}_{\nu}
to include the vector-valued case by defining

C^{m,p}_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}},\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}}):=\{f\in C^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}},\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}}),\|f\|_{W^{m,p}(\nu,\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}})}<\infty\},

where the Sobolev norm is analogously defined

\|f\|_{W^{m,p}(\nu,\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}})}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|f(x)\|_{2}^{p}d\nu(x)+\sum_{|\alpha|\leq m}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\partial^{\alpha}f(x)\|_{2}^{p}d\nu(x)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

(106)

We then have the following.

Theorem 45

(Universal approximation with smooth, ReLU like activation functions)
.

Suppose
\nu
is a finite measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}}
and
g\in C^{m,p}_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}},\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}})
for some
m\geq 0
,
p\geq 1
.
Let
\psi
be an activation function in the class
\mathcal{A}^{\infty}_{b}
.
Then, for any
\epsilon>0
,
there exists a two-layered neural network
\varphi_{\psi,\theta}

with activation function
\psi
and some width
d_{W}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\|g-\varphi_{\psi,\theta}\|_{W^{m,p}(\nu,\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}})}\leq\epsilon,

(107)

where

\varphi_{\psi,\theta}(x)=\mathbf{W}_{3}\psi(\mathbf{W}_{2}\psi(\mathbf{W}_{1}+\mathbf{b}_{1})+\mathbf{b}_{2})+\mathbf{b}_{3},

with weights

\mathbf{W}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}\times d_{W}}
,

\mathbf{W}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{W}\times d_{W}}
,

\mathbf{W}_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{W}\times d_{\mathrm{out}}}
,
and biases

\mathbf{b}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{W}}
,
and
\mathbf{b}_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}}
.

Proof.

This result provides extensions to the previous theorem by considering
\psi\in\mathcal{A}^{\infty}_{b}
.
The extension follows from the fact that we can construct a new activation function

\tilde{\psi}(t)=\psi(-\psi(t)+b)
, using some
b\in\mathbb{R}
,
that is non-constant and smooth with bounded derivatives up to order
k
.

We begin by verifying that
\tilde{\psi}
is non-constant.
Since
\psi\in\mathcal{A}^{\infty}_{b}
, we know
|\psi(0)|\leq a_{-}
, and
\psi
is increasing and non-saturating for positive inputs. Thus,
we choose
b>a_{-}
, such that
-\psi(0)+b>0
and
-\psi(t)+b<-\psi(0)+b
for
t>0
.
With this choice of
b
,

-\psi(t)+b
takes positive, changing values when
t
is in some small interval
(0,\delta)
,
and so
\psi(-\psi(t)+b)
is non-constant.

To see boundedness, we note that for
t>0
,
-\psi(t)+b
strictly decreases, such that
-\psi(t)+b\leq-\psi(0)+b
, where
-\psi(0)+b>0
.
In other words, for
t>0
,

|\psi(-\psi(t)+b)|\leq\max(|\psi(-\psi(0)+b)|,a_{-})
.
For
t\leq 0
, we have
|-\psi(t)+b|\leq a_{-}+b
, such that
\psi(-\psi(t)+b)
is again bounded.

When
m\geq 1
,
boundedness of derivatives can be verified using the Faà di Bruno formula (see, for example,
[
51
]
), a generalization of the chain rule for arbitrary order of the derivatives.
Specifically, the
\ell
th derivatives are polynomial combinations of the derivatives of
\psi
, i.e.,
products of
\psi^{(k)}(-\psi(t)+b)
and
\psi^{(k)}(t)
with derivative orders
k
up to
\ell
, all of which are bounded.
For example, the first derivative of
\tilde{\psi}
is

\frac{d}{dt}\tilde{\psi}(t)=-\psi^{\prime}(-\psi(t)+b)\psi^{\prime}(t),

which is bounded since
\psi^{\prime}(t)
is bounded.
Cases for derivatives of order
\ell\leq m

follow by repeating this process.
We then remark that products and compositions of continuous functions are continuous, from which we can conclude that
\tilde{\psi}\in C^{m}_{b}(\mathbb{R})
.

We can therefore apply the universal approximation result of
Theorem

44
using the activation function
\tilde{\psi}
.
That is, given any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a neural network,

\varphi_{\tilde{\psi},\theta}(s)=\mathbf{W}_{2}\tilde{\psi}(\mathbf{W}_{1}s+\mathbf{b}_{1})+\mathbf{b}_{2}

with some hidden layer size
d_{1}
, which approximates
g\in C^{m}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}},\mathbb{R})
to
\epsilon
error.
Since
\tilde{\psi}(t)=\psi(-\psi(t)+b)
, a componentwise application of this gives

\varphi_{\tilde{\psi},\theta}(s)=\mathbf{W}_{2}\psi(-\mathbf{I}\psi(\mathbf{W}_{1}s+\mathbf{b}_{1})+\mathbf{b})+\mathbf{b}_{2},

where
\mathbf{I}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{1}}
is the identity matrix and
\mathbf{b}=(b,\dots,b)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
.
Thus,
\varphi_{\tilde{\psi},\theta}
is a neural network with two hidden layers and activation function
\psi
.
Finally, this can be extended to the case when the output is in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}}
by stacking individual approximations for each component.
∎

B.3
Extension to deep neural networks

Additionally, the result can be extended to deep neural networks by considering smooth approximations to the identity function
\mathrm{Id}(x)=x
.
We will use a form similar to that found in
[
28
]
.

Lemma 46
.

Let
m\geq 0
,
\psi\in\mathcal{A}_{b}^{\infty}
, and
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
be such that
\psi^{\prime}(t_{0})\neq 0
.
For
h\in(0,1]
, consider the function
\mathrm{Id}_{h}(t):\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, defined as

\mathrm{Id}_{h}(t)=\frac{\psi(t_{0}+ht)-\psi(t_{0}-ht)}{2h\psi^{\prime}(t_{0})}.

(108)

Then, for any
\epsilon,B>0
, there exists
h\in(0,1]
such that
\mathrm{Id}_{h}
approximates the identity mapping
\mathrm{Id}(t)=t
to arbitrary accuracy, i.e.,

\|\mathrm{Id}_{h}-\mathrm{Id}\|_{C^{m}([-B,B])}\leq\epsilon.

(109)

Additionally,
\mathrm{Id}_{h}
is Lipschitz continuous with bounded derivatives up to order
m
. That is

|\mathrm{Id}_{h}(t_{1})-\mathrm{Id}_{h}(t_{2})|\leq L|t_{1}-t_{2}|,\quad\forall t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{R},\qquad|\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{(k)}(t)|\leq C_{k},\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R},k=1,\dots,m,

where the Lipschitz constant
L=L(\psi,t_{0})
and the bound
C_{k}=C_{k}(\psi,t_{0})
are independent of
h
.

Proof.

Let
m\geq 0
,
\psi\in\mathcal{A}_{b}^{\infty}
,
B>0
, and
\epsilon>0
be given.
Additionally, let
a_{k}\geq 0
denote the bound on the
k
th derivative of
\psi
, i.e.,
|\psi^{(k)}|\leq a_{k}
.
We begin by noting for
t_{0}
such that
\psi^{\prime}(t_{0})\neq 0
and
h\in(0,1]
,
the function
\mathrm{Id}_{h}\in C^{m}(\mathbb{R})
with derivatives

\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{(k)}(t)=h^{k-1}\left(\frac{\psi^{(k)}(t_{0}+ht)+(-1)^{k-1}\psi^{(k)}(t_{0}-ht)}{2\psi^{\prime}(t_{0})}\right),

(110)

for
1\leq k\leq m
.

The approximation is constructed such that

\mathrm{Id}_{h}(0)=0
and
\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{\prime}(0)=1
,
i.e., it is a good approximation of the identity function near the origin.
To see this, we apply Taylor’s theorem,

\mathrm{Id}_{h}(t)=\mathrm{Id}_{h}(0)+\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{\prime}(0)t+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{\prime\prime}(\xi)t^{2}=t+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{\prime\prime}(\xi)t^{2},

where
\xi\in[0,t]
. Substituting in the expression for the second derivative, we arrive at the following bound for
t\in[-B,B]
,

|\mathrm{Id}_{h}(t)-t|\leq h\left|\frac{\psi^{\prime\prime}(t_{0}+h\xi)-\psi^{\prime\prime}(t_{0}-h\xi)}{2\psi^{\prime}(t_{0})}\right|\frac{t^{2}}{2}\leq\frac{a_{2}B^{2}}{|\psi^{\prime}(t_{0})|}h

which can be made arbitrarily small by choosing
h
small.
For the first derivative, we consider a Taylor expansion on
\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{\prime}
, such that

\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{\prime}(t)=\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{\prime}(0)+\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{\prime\prime}(\xi)t,

where
\xi\in[0,t]
and
\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{\prime}(0)=1
.
This implies

|\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{\prime}(t)-1|\leq h\left|\frac{\psi^{\prime\prime}(t_{0}+h\xi)-\psi^{\prime\prime}(t_{0}-h\xi)}{2\psi^{\prime}(t_{0})}\right|t\leq\frac{a_{2}B}{|\psi^{\prime}(t_{0})|}h

for
t\in[-B,B]
.
For higher-order derivatives, we note that
\mathrm{Id}^{(k)}(t)=0
for any
k\geq 2
.
Moreover, inspecting (
110
), we see that

|\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{(k)}(t)|\leq h^{k-1}\left|\frac{\psi^{(k)}(t_{0}+ht)+(-1)^{k-1}\psi^{(k)}(t_{0}-ht)}{2\psi^{\prime}(t_{0})}\right|\leq\frac{a_{k}}{|\psi^{\prime}(t_{0})|}h^{k-1}.

Choosing
h
sufficiently small such that all function and derivative errors are less than
\epsilon
yields the desired error bound over the interval
[-B,B]
.

To see Lipschitz continuity and boundedness of the derivatives, we again inspect the expression (
110
).
In particular, for
1\leq k\leq m
, and
h\in(0,1]
,

|\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{(k)}(t)|\leq\frac{a_{k}}{|\psi^{\prime}(t_{0})|},

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
This implies the boundedness of all
k
th order derivatives for
1\leq k\leq m
, and hence Lipschitz continuity of
\mathrm{Id}_{h}
.
Moreover, it is clear that the bounds depend only on
\psi
and
t_{0}
, and not on
h
.
∎

The identity approximation (
108
) can be thought of as a single neural network layer, and thus can be appended to a neural network
\varphi
to form a deep neural network
\tilde{\varphi}=\mathrm{Id}_{h}\circ\varphi
approximating
\varphi
itself.

Lemma 47
.

Suppose
\nu
is a finite measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}}
and
\varphi\in C^{m,p}_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}})
for some
m\geq 1
,
p\geq 1
.
Additionally, assume that
\varphi
has bounded derivatives of order
1\leq k\leq m
.
Then, for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists
h\in(0,1]
such that

\|\mathrm{Id}_{h}\circ\varphi-\varphi\|_{W^{m,p}(\nu,\mathbb{R})}\leq\epsilon.

Moreover,
\mathrm{Id}_{h}\circ\varphi\in C^{m,p}_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}})
and also has bounded derivatives of order
1\leq k\leq m
.

Proof.

Consider
\mathrm{Id}_{h}
as defined in (
108
).
Moreover, let
M_{k}
denote the bound on the
k
th derivative of
\varphi
, i.e.,
\|D^{k}\varphi\|_{F_{k}}\leq M_{k}
,
and let
L_{k}
denote the bound on the
k
th derivative of
\mathrm{Id}_{h}
, i.e.,
|\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{(k)}(t)|\leq L_{k}
,
both of which are independent of
B
and
h
.

For some
B
, let
h\in(0,1]
be chosen such that
\|\mathrm{Id}_{h}-\mathrm{Id}\|_{C^{m}([-B,B])}\leq\delta
.
Consider first the
k=0
term of the
W^{m,p}
norm,

\|\mathrm{Id}_{h}\circ\varphi-\varphi\|_{L^{p}(\nu,\mathbb{R})}^{p}=\int|\mathrm{Id}_{h}\circ\varphi-\varphi|^{p}d\nu

We can decompose this as

\int|\mathrm{Id}_{h}\circ\varphi-\varphi|^{p}d\nu=\int_{|\varphi|\leq B}|\mathrm{Id}_{h}\circ\varphi-\varphi|^{p}d\nu+\int_{|\varphi|>B}|\mathrm{Id}_{h}\circ\varphi-\varphi|^{p}d\nu,

which is bounded as

\int|\mathrm{Id}_{h}\circ\varphi-\varphi|^{p}d\nu\leq\delta^{p}+\int_{|\varphi|>B}((1+L_{1})|\varphi|)^{p}d\nu,

(111)

where
L_{1}
is the bound on the first derivative of
\mathrm{Id}_{h}
and an upper bound on the Lipschitz constant.
Note that with the help of the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem, the second term can be made arbitrarily small by choosing
B
large enough such that
\nu(|\varphi|>B)
is small.

For the first derivative, we have for any
i=1,\dots d_{\mathrm{in}}
,

\int|\partial_{x_{i}}(\mathrm{Id}\circ\varphi)-\partial_{x_{i}}\varphi|^{p}d\nu\leq\int_{|\varphi|\leq B}|\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{\prime}\circ\varphi-1|^{p}|\partial_{x_{i}}\varphi|^{p}d\nu+\int_{|\varphi|>B}|\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{\prime}\circ\varphi-1|^{p}|\partial_{x_{i}}\varphi|^{p}d\nu.

(112)

Then, the sum is bounded by

\int|\partial_{x_{i}}(\mathrm{Id}\circ\varphi)-\partial_{x_{i}}\varphi|_{2}^{p}d\nu\leq\delta^{p}+\int_{|\varphi|>B}(L_{k}M_{k})^{p}d\nu.

For the higher-order derivatives, we recall the Faà di Bruno formula for the
k
th derivative of a composition of functions
[
23
]
.
For
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
g:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, this takes the form

\frac{\partial^{k}(g\circ f)}{\partial x_{i_{1}}\dots\partial x_{i_{k}}}=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi}g^{(|\pi|)}(f)\prod_{J\in\pi}\frac{\partial^{|J|}f}{\prod_{j\in J}\partial x_{i_{j}}},

where
\Pi
is the set of partitions of
\{1,\dots,k\}
and
|\pi|,|J|
refer to the number of elements in
\pi
and
J
, respectively.
Note that there is only one partition of size one, i.e.,
\pi_{k}=\{\{1,\dots,k\}\}
,
which yields the term

g^{\prime}(f)\frac{\partial^{k}f}{\partial x_{i_{1}}\dots\partial x_{i_{k}}}.

We can therefore decompose the sum as

\frac{\partial^{k}(g\circ f)}{\partial x_{i_{1}}\dots\partial x_{i_{k}}}=g^{\prime}(f)\frac{\partial^{k}f}{\partial x_{i_{1}}\dots\partial x_{i_{k}}}+\sum_{\pi\in\Pi\setminus\pi_{k}}g^{(|\pi|)}(f)\prod_{J\in\pi}\frac{\partial^{|J|}f}{\prod_{j\in J}\partial x_{i_{j}}}.

Applying this to the composition
\mathrm{Id}_{h}\circ\varphi
,
the error in each component of the
k
th derivative is given by

\frac{\partial^{k}}{\partial x_{i_{1}}\dots\partial x_{i_{k}}}(\mathrm{Id}_{h}\circ\varphi)-\frac{\partial^{k}}{\partial x_{i_{1}}\dots\partial x_{i_{k}}}(\varphi)=(\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{\prime}\circ\varphi-1)\frac{\partial^{k}\varphi}{\partial x_{i_{1}}\dots\partial x_{i_{k}}}+\sum_{\pi\in\Pi\setminus\pi_{k}}\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{(|\pi|)}(\varphi)\prod_{J\in\pi}\frac{\partial^{|J|}\varphi}{\prod_{j\in J}\partial x_{i_{j}}}.

Consider the first term, whose
L^{p}
norm can be decomposed into

\int_{|\varphi|\leq B}|\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{\prime}\circ\varphi-1|^{p}\left|\frac{\partial^{k}\varphi}{\partial x_{i_{1}}\dots\partial x_{i_{k}}}\right|^{p}d\nu+\int_{|\varphi|>B}|\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{\prime}\circ\varphi-1|^{p}\left|\frac{\partial^{k}\varphi}{\partial x_{i_{1}}\dots\partial x_{i_{k}}}\right|^{p}d\nu.

Again, by the boundedness assumptions, we have

\int|\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{\prime}\circ\varphi-1|^{p}\left|\frac{\partial^{k}\varphi}{\partial x_{i_{1}}\dots\partial x_{i_{k}}}\right|^{p}d\nu\leq\delta^{p}M_{k}+\int_{\|\varphi\|>B}((1+L_{1})M_{k})^{p}d\nu.

Moreover, for every entry in the summation of the second term, we have

\displaystyle\int|\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{(|\pi|)}(\varphi)|^{p}\left|\prod_{J\in\pi}\frac{\partial^{|J|}\varphi}{\prod_{j\in J}\partial x_{i_{j}}}\right|^{p}d\nu

\displaystyle=\int_{|\varphi|\leq B}|\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{(|\pi|)}(\varphi)|^{p}\left|\prod_{J\in\pi}\frac{\partial^{|J|}\varphi}{\prod_{j\in J}\partial x_{i_{j}}}\right|^{p}d\nu

\displaystyle+\int_{|\varphi|>B}|\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{(|\pi|)}(\varphi)|^{p}\left|\prod_{J\in\pi}\frac{\partial^{|J|}\varphi}{\prod_{j\in B}\partial x_{i_{j}}}\right|^{p}d\nu

Note that for
\pi\in\Pi\setminus\{\pi_{k}\}
,
|\pi|>1
, and hence
\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{(|\pi|)}\leq\delta

inside
[-B,B]^{d}
and is bounded outside.
Thus, we have the following bound,

\int|\mathrm{Id}_{h}^{(|\pi|)}(\varphi)|^{p}\left|\prod_{J\in\pi}\frac{\partial^{|J|}\varphi}{\prod_{j\in J}\partial x_{i_{j}}}\right|^{p}d\nu\leq\delta^{p}\prod_{J\in\pi}M_{|J|}^{p}+\int_{|\varphi|>B}\left(L_{|\pi|}\prod_{J\in\pi}M_{|J|}\right)^{p}d\nu.

(113)

To obtain the overall error bound in the
W^{m,p}(\nu,\mathbb{R})
, we combine the bounds in (
111
), (
112
), and (
113
) (summed over all partial derivatives),
choosing first
B
such that
\nu(\|\varphi\|>B)
is small, and then choosing
h
such that
\delta
is small.

We now check that
\mathrm{Id}_{h}\circ\varphi\in C^{m,p}_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}})
and has bounded derivatives.
In particular, since
\mathrm{Id}_{h}
is Lipschitz, the fact that
\varphi\in L^{p}(\nu,\mathbb{R})
implies that
\mathrm{Id}_{h}\circ\varphi\in L^{p}(\nu,\mathbb{R})
.
Moreover, to see boundedness of the derivatives of
\mathrm{Id}_{h}\circ\varphi
,
it suffices to inspect the Faà di Bruno formula and note that both the derivatives of
\mathrm{Id}_{h}
and
\varphi
up to order
m
are bounded.
Thus, overall,
\mathrm{Id}_{h}\circ\varphi\in C^{m,p}_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}})
and has bounded derivatives of order
1\leq k\leq m
.

∎

We can now proceed with the proof of
Theorem

2
.

Proof.

(of
Theorem

2
)
This result follows from the previous theorem by considering the identity approximation
\mathrm{Id}_{h}
as defined in
Lemma

46
.
Beginning with the two-layer approximation result of
Theorem

45
,
we can construct a deep neural network by appending the identity approximation to the output of the two-layer network.
That is, let
\varphi_{1}
be the two-layer approximation to
g
as defined in
Theorem

45
,
and let
\mathrm{Id}_{h}
be an approximation to the identity function as defined in
Lemma

46
.
Moreover, due to the choice of activation function,
\varphi_{1}
has bounded derivatives of order
1\leq k\leq m
.
Then, we define
\varphi_{2}=\mathrm{Id}_{h}\circ\varphi_{1}
as the deep neural network with
d_{L}=3
layers,
noting that when applied componentwise,
\mathrm{Id}_{h}
is a single layer neural network with activation function
\psi
whose width is twice the size of the input dimension.
The approximation error is given by

\|g-\varphi_{2}\|_{W^{m,p}(\nu,\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}})}\leq\|g-\varphi_{1}\|_{W^{m,p}(\nu,\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}})}+\|\varphi_{1}-\mathrm{Id}_{h}\circ\varphi_{1}\|_{W^{m,p}(\nu,\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}})}.

By
Theorem

45
,
we can choose
\varphi_{1}
such that
\|g-\varphi_{1}\|_{W^{m,p}(\nu,\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}})}\leq\epsilon/2
,
and by
Lemma

46
, we can choose
h_{1}
such that

\|\mathrm{Id}_{h_{1}}\circ\varphi_{1}-\varphi_{1}\|_{W^{m,p}(\nu,\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}})}\leq\epsilon/2
.
Since the composition
\mathrm{Id}_{h_{1}}\circ\varphi\in C^{m,p}_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}})
also has bounded derivatives of order
1\leq k\leq m
, the process can be repeated to construct a deep neural network with
d_{L}\geq 2
of the form

\varphi_{\psi,\theta}=\mathrm{Id}_{h_{d_{L}-2}}\circ\dots\circ\mathrm{Id}_{h_{1}}\circ\varphi_{1}

with arbitrary approximation error
\epsilon
.
The layers of the neural network can be padded by zeros to ensure a constant width
d_{W}
.
∎

B.4
Examples of activation functions in
\mathcal{A}^{\infty}_{b}

Many of the common, ReLU-like activation functions are in the class
\mathcal{A}^{\infty}_{b}
, including the softplus, GeLU, and SiLU functions.

Example 48

(The softplus activation)
.

The function
\psi(t)=\log(1+e^{t})
is in
\mathcal{A}^{\infty}_{b}
.

Proof.

We first note that
\psi(t)
is monotonically increasing and bounded below by
0
. We can verify its derivatives are all bounded.
Note that the first derivative of the softplus function is the (logistic) sigmoid,

\sigma(t):=1/(1+e^{-t})
.
That is,

\psi^{\prime}(t)=\frac{e^{t}}{1+e^{t}}=\frac{1}{1+e^{-t}}=\sigma(t).

Evidently, this is continuous and bounded.

Subsequent derivatives of
\psi
are derivatives of the sigmoid, which has the well known property

\sigma^{\prime}(t)=\sigma(t)(1-\sigma(t)).

We can verify by induction that higher derivatives of
\sigma
are all of the form
p(\sigma)\sigma(1-\sigma)
, where
p(t)
is a polynomial function.
Clearly this is true for the first derivative. Now, suppose the
k
th derivative is of the form
p_{k}(\sigma)\sigma(1-\sigma)
.
Then, the
(k+1)
th derivative is given by

p_{k}^{\prime}(\sigma)\sigma^{2}(1-\sigma)^{2}+p_{k}(\sigma)\sigma(1-\sigma)^{2}-p_{k}(\sigma)\sigma^{2}(1-\sigma).

Factorizing out
\sigma(1-\sigma)
yields an expression of the form
p_{k+1}(\sigma)\sigma(1-\sigma)
, where
p_{k+1}
is a polynomial with one higher degree.
Since
\sigma
is bounded, we can conclude that
\psi^{(k)}(t)
is continuous and bounded for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
∎

Example 49

(The SiLU activation)
.

The sigmoid linear unit (SiLU) function,
\psi(t)=t\sigma(t)
, where
\sigma(t)=1/(1+e^{-t})
is the logistic sigmoid, is in
\mathcal{A}^{\infty}_{b}
.

Proof.

We can verify that
\psi(0)=0
, and that
\psi(t)
strictly increases for
t\geq 0
.
On the other hand, as
t\rightarrow-\infty
,
\psi(t)\rightarrow 0
, so it must be bounded on the interval
(-\infty,0]
.
For the derivatives, we note that

\psi^{\prime}(t)=\sigma(t)+t\sigma^{\prime}(t).

Recall that
\sigma^{\prime}(t)=\sigma(t)(1-\sigma(t))=(1+e^{-t})^{-1}\cdot(1+e^{t})^{-1}
, such that
t\sigma^{\prime}(t)
tends to zero as
t
approaches
\pm\infty
.
Thus,
\psi^{\prime}(t)
is continuous and bounded.
Higher derivatives can be verified by induction.
The derivatives of
\sigma(t)
are continuous and bounded, as shown in the softplus example. Furthermore, derivatives of the
t\sigma^{\prime}(t)
term can be shown to take the form
(p(\sigma)+tq(\sigma))\sigma(1-\sigma)
, where
p
and
q
are polynomials.
This is true for the zeroth derivative (i.e.,
t\sigma^{\prime}(t)=t\sigma(1-\sigma)
is of this form).
Suppose the
k
th derivative is of the form

(p_{k}(\sigma)+tq_{k}(\sigma))\sigma(1-\sigma)
.
Then, the
(k+1)
th derivative is given by

(p_{k}^{\prime}(\sigma)+tq_{k}^{\prime}(\sigma))\sigma^{2}(1-\sigma)^{2}+q_{k}(\sigma)\sigma(1-\sigma)+(p_{k}(\sigma)+tq_{k}(\sigma))(\sigma(1-\sigma)^{2}-\sigma^{2}(1-\sigma)).

Factorizing out
\sigma(1-\sigma)
yields an expression of the form

(p_{k+1}(\sigma)+tq_{k+1}(\sigma))\sigma(1-\sigma),

where
p_{k+1}
and
q_{k+1}
are polynomials with one higher degree.
Recall that for any polynomial
p
, the function
p(\sigma(t))\sigma(t)(1-\sigma(t))
is bounded. Moreover, for any polynomial
q
, the function

tq(\sigma(t))\sigma(t)(1-\sigma(t))
approaches zero as
t\rightarrow\pm\infty
.
Thus, higher derivatives of
\psi
are also continuous and bounded.
∎

Example 50
.

The GeLU activation
The Gaussian error linear unit (GeLU) function,
\psi(t)=t\Phi(t)
, where
\Phi(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{t}e^{-x^{2}/2}dx
is the cumulative distribution function of the standard Gaussian, is in
\mathcal{A}^{\infty}_{b}
.

Proof.

Similar to the SiLU, we can verify that
\psi(0)=0
, and that
\psi(t)
strictly increases for
t\geq 0
. Moreover, as
t\rightarrow-\infty
,
\psi(t)\rightarrow 0
, so it again must be bounded on the interval
(-\infty,0]
.
For the first derivative, we have

\psi^{\prime}(t)=\Phi(t)+(2\pi)^{-1/2}te^{-t^{2}/2},

which is bounded since
te^{-t^{2}/2}
approaches zero as
t
approaches
\pm\infty
.
Continuity and boundedness of higher-order derivatives can once again be verified by induction, noting that this time, they take the form
p(t)e^{-t^{2}/2}
, where
p(t)
is a polynomial.

∎

Appendix C
Proofs for the error analysis

C.1
Proof of the universal approximation of conditional expectations

Proof.

Let
\epsilon>0
be arbitrary, and consider first some
\eta>0
.
Since
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
is dense in
H^{m}_{\gamma}
, we can find
F_{\eta}\in\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
such that
\|F-F_{\eta}\|_{H^{m}_{\gamma}}\leq\eta
.
This also means that
\|P_{r}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]-P_{r}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F_{\eta}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{H^{m}_{\gamma}}\leq\eta
.

We then focus on approximating
P_{r}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F_{\eta}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]
.
By
Lemma

41
, since
F_{\eta}\in\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, we have
\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F_{\eta}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]\in C^{\infty}_{b}
.
We therefore look for an approximation of the function

g(s):={U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F_{\eta}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})](V_{r}s)

by a neural network, where
g\in C^{\infty}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathbb{R}^{r})
.
In particular, the universal approximation result
of
Theorem

45

implies that there exists a neural network
\varphi=\varphi_{\psi,\theta}
approximating
g
such that

\|g-\varphi\|_{H^{m}(\gamma_{r},\mathbb{R}^{r})}\leq\eta

where
\gamma_{r}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{r})
refers to the standard Gaussian on
\mathbb{R}^{r}
.
Moreover, the
L^{2}_{\gamma}
error between the projected conditional expectation and the neural operator can be expressed as

\int\|P_{r}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F_{\eta}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})](x)-U_{r}\varphi({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}x)\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}\gamma(dx)=\int\|{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F_{\eta}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})](V_{r}{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}x)-\varphi({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}x)\|_{2}^{2}\gamma(dx),

since
\|P_{r}u\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}=\|{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}u\|_{2}^{2}
. We can now perform a change of variable to
s={V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}x
. Since
V_{r}
is orthonormal in
\mathcal{E}
, is distributed according to
\gamma_{r}
, such that

\int\|P_{r}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F_{\eta}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})](x)-U_{r}\varphi({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}x)\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}\gamma(dx)=\int\|{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F_{\eta}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})](V_{r}s)-\varphi(s)\|_{2}^{2}\gamma_{r}(ds)

Moreover, since we have defined
g={U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F_{\eta}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})](V_{r}s)
, we have

\int\|{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F_{\eta}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})](V_{r}s)-\varphi(s)\|_{2}^{2}\gamma_{r}(ds)=\int\|g(s)-\varphi(s)\|_{2}^{2}\gamma_{r}(ds).

Similarly, for
k
th derivative,

\displaystyle\int\|D^{k}P_{r}(\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F_{\eta}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})](x))-D^{k}(U_{r}\varphi({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}x))\|_{\mathrm{HS}_{k}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\gamma(dx)

\displaystyle\quad=\int\|P_{r}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[D^{k}F_{\eta}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})](Q_{r}x)(Q_{r}\cdot,\dots,Q_{r}\cdot)-U_{r}D^{k}\varphi({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}x)({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}\cdot,\dots,{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{HS}_{k}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\gamma(dx)

where we have interchanged differentiation and conditional expectation is given by

Lemma

14
in
Section

A.4
.
We then follow the same approach, projecting the norm into the reduced subspace and then performing the change of variables
s={V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}x
to obtain a similar bound for the derivatives,

\displaystyle\int\|D^{k}P_{r}(\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F_{\eta}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})](x))-D^{k}(U_{r}\varphi({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}x))\|_{\mathrm{HS}_{k}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\gamma(dx)

\displaystyle\quad=\int\|{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[D^{k}F_{\eta}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})](V_{r}{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}x)(V_{r}\cdot,\dots,V_{r}\cdot)-D^{k}\varphi({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}x)\|_{F_{k}}^{2}\gamma(dx)

\displaystyle\quad=\int\|{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[D^{k}F_{\eta}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})](V_{r}s)(V_{r}\cdot,\dots,V_{r}\cdot)-D^{k}\varphi(s)\|_{F_{k}}^{2}\gamma_{r}(ds)

\displaystyle\quad=\int\|D^{k}g(x)-D^{k}\varphi(s)\|_{F_{k}}^{2}\gamma_{r}(ds).

where we have used
\|\cdot\|_{F_{k}}=\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{HS}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathbb{R}^{r})}
to denote the tensor Frobenius norms on
\mathbb{R}^{r}
,
noting that
\|\cdot\|_{F_{1}}=\|\cdot\|_{F}
is the standard matrix Frobenius norm.

Finally, since the Fréchet derivatives coincide with the weak derivatives, we have

\|P_{r}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]-U_{r}\circ\varphi\circ{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}\|_{H^{m}_{\gamma}}=\|g-\varphi\|_{H^{m}(\gamma_{r},\mathbb{R}^{r})}\leq\eta.

Combined with the fact that
\|P_{r}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]-P_{r}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F_{\eta}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{H^{m}_{\gamma}}\leq\eta
, it suffices to choose
\eta=\epsilon/2
.
∎

C.2
Proof of the generic universal approximation

The universal approximation of projected conditional expectations in
H^{m}_{\gamma}
can be used to obtain the generic universal approximation result of
Theorem

6
as follows.

Proof.

Let
\epsilon
be arbitrary. Given
F\in H^{m}_{\gamma}
, we have

\|F\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}|\left\langle u_{i},F\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}}|^{2}\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[|\left\langle u_{i},F\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}}|^{2}\right]<\infty

where the interchange of sum and expectation is possible since all terms are non-negative.
Moreover, for any
r>0
, we have

\|F-P_{r}F\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}|\left\langle u_{i},F\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}}|^{2}\right]=\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[|\left\langle u_{i},F\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}}|^{2}\right].

Thus,
\|F-P_{r}F\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}
converges monotonically to zero as
r\rightarrow\infty
.
Analogously, for any
k\leq m
,

\|D_{\mathcal{E}}^{k}F\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}_{k}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[\left|\left\langle u_{j},D_{\mathcal{E}}^{k}F(w_{i_{1}},\dots,w_{i_{k}})\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}}\right|^{2}\right]<\infty,

(114)

such that

\|D_{\mathcal{E}}^{k}F-P_{r}D_{\mathcal{E}}^{k}F\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}_{k}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}=\sum_{j=r+1}^{\infty}\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[\left|\left\langle u_{i},D_{\mathcal{E}}^{k}F(w_{i_{1}},\dots,w_{i_{k}})\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}}\right|^{2}\right]<\infty,

(115)

also converges monotonically to zero.
This allows us to find some
r_{\mathcal{Y}}

such that
\|F-P_{r_{\mathcal{Y}}}F\|_{H^{m}_{\gamma}}\leq\epsilon/3
.
We then consider an input rank
r_{\mathcal{X}}\geq r_{\mathcal{Y}}
,
such that
the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma(V_{r_{\mathcal{X}}})]
also satisfies

\|F-\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma(V_{r_{\mathcal{X}}})]\|_{H^{m}_{\gamma}}\leq\epsilon/3
.
This is possible since the conditional expectations converge to
F
as
r\rightarrow\infty
by Theorem 5.4.5 of
[
8
]
.
We can now increase the output rank to
r_{\mathcal{X}}
in order to make the input and output ranks equal, i.e.,
r=r_{\mathcal{X}}\geq r_{\mathcal{Y}}
.
Note that

\|F-P_{r}F\|_{H^{m}_{\gamma}}\leq\|F-P_{r_{\mathcal{Y}}}F\|_{H^{m}_{\gamma}}\leq\epsilon/3

due to (
114
) and (
115
) and

\|P_{r}F-\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[P_{r}F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{H^{m}_{\gamma}}\leq\|F-\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{H^{m}_{\gamma}}\leq\epsilon/3,

since
\|P_{r}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}=1
.

Lemma

15
then implies the existence of a neural network
\varphi_{\psi,\theta}
such that the neural operator

\widetilde{F}_{\theta}=U_{r}\circ\varphi_{\psi,\theta}\circ{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}

approximates the projected conditional expectation to error

\|\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[P_{r}F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]-\widetilde{F}_{\theta}\|_{H^{m}_{\gamma}}\leq\epsilon/3.

Thus, combining the error terms in a triangle inequality yields the desired error bound,

\displaystyle\|F-\widetilde{F}_{\theta}\|_{H^{m}_{\gamma}}\leq\|F-P_{r}F\|_{H^{m}_{\gamma}}+\|P_{r}F-\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[P_{r}F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{H^{m}_{\gamma}}+\|\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[P_{r}F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]-\widetilde{F}_{\theta}\|_{H^{m}_{\gamma}}\leq\epsilon.

∎

C.3
Proofs for the error decompositions

We begin by proving
Proposition

16
.

Proof.

(of
Proposition

16
)
The results follow from decomposing each term into its component within the projection subspace and its orthogonal complement.
We begin with the
L^{2}_{\gamma}
error. For
\widetilde{F}=U_{r}\circ\varphi\circ{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}+\widehat{F}
, we can write the misfit
\|F(x)-\widetilde{F}(x)\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}
as

\|F(x)-\widetilde{F}(x)\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}=\|P_{r}(F(x)-\widetilde{F}(x))\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}+\|(I-P_{r})(F(x)-\widehat{F})\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2},

since
(I-P_{r})U_{r}=0
.
Moreover, using the decomposition

F-\widehat{F}=F-\bar{F}+\bar{F}-\widehat{F}
, we have

\|(I-P_{r})(F-\widehat{F})\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}=\|(I-P_{r})(F-\bar{F})\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}+2\left\langle(I-P_{r})(F-\bar{F}),(\bar{F}-\widehat{F})\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}}+\|(I-P_{r})(\bar{F}-\widehat{F})\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}.

Taking the expectation over
\gamma
removes the cross term, leaving the desired result.

For the derivative error, we have
D\widetilde{F}(x)=U_{r}D\varphi({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}x){V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}
.
Thus, using
(v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
as the basis for evaluating the Hilbert–Schmidt norm, we have

\displaystyle\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x)-D_{\mathcal{E}}\widetilde{F}(x)

\displaystyle\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\|(D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x)-D_{\mathcal{E}}\widetilde{F}(x))v_{i}\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\|(D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x)-D_{\mathcal{E}}\widetilde{F}(x))v_{i}\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}+\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\|(D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x)-D_{\mathcal{E}}\widetilde{F}(x))v_{i}\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|(D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x)-D_{\mathcal{E}}\widetilde{F}(x))Q_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}+\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x)(I-Q_{r})\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2},

since
D_{\mathcal{E}}\widetilde{F}(x)(I-Q_{r})=0
.
We further decompose the first term into

\displaystyle\|(D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x)-D_{\mathcal{E}}\widetilde{F}(x))Q_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|P_{r}(D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x)-D_{\mathcal{E}}\widetilde{F}(x))Q_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}

\displaystyle+\|(I-P_{r})D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x)Q_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}.

Finally, we note that

\|(I-P_{r})D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x)Q_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\leq\|(I-P_{r})D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x)\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}.

Combining the individual components and taking the expectation yields the overall bound.

To write the terms involving
\widetilde{F}
in the latent spaces, we make use of Parseval’s identity with the basis
(u_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
.
For the function value, we have

\|P_{r}(F-\widetilde{F})\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}=\|U_{r}{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}(F-U_{r}\varphi({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}x)-\widehat{F})\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}=\|{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}(F-\widehat{F})-\varphi({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}x)\|_{2}^{2}.

For the derivative, we additionally use
(v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
as the basis for the
\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})
norm, giving

\displaystyle\|P_{r}(D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x)-D_{\mathcal{E}}\widetilde{F})Q_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|{U_{r}}^{\!\dagger}D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x)V_{r}-D\varphi({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}x)\|_{F}^{2}.

∎

Corollary

17
then follows as a result of the error decomposition in
Proposition

16

along with the universal approximation of conditional expectations in
Lemma

15
.

Proof.

(of
Corollary

17
)
By
Proposition

16
, we can decompose the approximation error into

\|F-\widetilde{F}_{\theta}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}\leq\|P_{r}(F-\widetilde{F}_{\theta})\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}+\|(I-P_{r})(F-\bar{F})\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}+\|\bar{F}-\widehat{F}\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2},

and

\displaystyle|F-\widetilde{F}_{\theta}|_{H^{1}_{\gamma}}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\|P_{r}(D_{\mathcal{E}}F-D_{\mathcal{E}}\widetilde{F}_{\theta})Q_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}

\displaystyle+\|(I-P_{r})D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}+\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-Q_{r})\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}.

We first make use of the conditional expectation
\widetilde{F}_{r}=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]
as an intermediate to the approximation
F
. The triangle inequality, along with the inequality
(|a|+|b|)^{2}\leq 2|a|^{2}+2|b|^{2}
yields

\displaystyle\|P_{r}(F-\widetilde{F}_{\theta})\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}\leq 2\|P_{r}(F-\widetilde{F}_{r})\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}+2\|P_{r}(\widetilde{F}_{r}-\widetilde{F}_{\theta})\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}

and

\displaystyle\|P_{r}(D_{\mathcal{E}}F-D_{\mathcal{E}}\widetilde{F}_{\theta})Q_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|P_{r}(D_{\mathcal{E}}F-D_{\mathcal{E}}\widetilde{F}_{r})Q_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+2\|P_{r}(D_{\mathcal{E}}\widetilde{F}_{r}-D_{\mathcal{E}}\widetilde{F}_{\theta})Q_{r}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}.

By
Lemma

15
, there exists neural network
\varphi_{\psi,\theta}

such that
U_{r}\circ\varphi_{\psi,\theta}\circ{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}
approximates

P_{r}(\widetilde{F}_{r}-\widehat{F})=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[P_{r}(F-\widehat{F})|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]

in
\|\cdot\|_{H^{1}_{\gamma}}^{2}
to error
\epsilon_{\theta}/2
.
This means that
\widetilde{F}_{\theta}=U_{r}\circ\varphi_{\psi,\theta}\circ{V_{r}}^{\!\dagger}+\widehat{F}
satisfies the desired error bounds.
∎

C.4
Proof of the subspace Poincaré inequality

We begin by stating the Poincaré inequality, which holds for Gaussian measures on separable Hilbert spaces
[
8
]
.

Theorem 51

(Poincaré inequality for Gaussian measures)
.

Suppose
F\in H^{1}_{\gamma}
. Then,

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|F-\bar{F}\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}],

(116)

where
\bar{F}=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F]
is the mean of
F
.

The subspace Poincaré inequality then follows from the Poincaré inequality itself.

Proof.

(Of
Theorem

24
)
The procedure is almost identical to that of
[
57
]
,
with justifications to ensure the operations are valid in the separable Hilbert space setting.
We first consider the case where
F\in\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
,
for which we can define

G(x,y):=F(Q_{r}x+(I-Q_{r})y)
, such that

\mathbb{E}_{y\sim\gamma}[G(x,y)]=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})](x)
.
Since
Q_{r}
and
I-Q_{r}
are continuous linear operators, we have

\partial_{y}G(x,y)=DF(Q_{r}x+(I-Q_{r})y)(I-Q_{r}).

Moreover, since
Q_{r}x+(I-Q_{r})y\sim\gamma
whenever
(x,y)\sim\gamma\otimes\gamma
, we know

\iint\|G(x,y)\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}\gamma(dy)\gamma(dx)=\iint\|F(Q_{r}x+(I-Q_{r})y)\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}\gamma(dy)\gamma(dx)=\|F\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}<\infty.

An analogous calculation can be made for
\partial_{y}G
.
Therefore, we have

\int|G(x,y)|^{2}\gamma(dy)<\infty

and
\int\|\partial_{y}G(x,y)\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\gamma(dy)<\infty

for almost every
x
.
This suggests that
G(x,\cdot)\in H^{1}_{\gamma}
for almost every
x
,
with a Sobolev derivative equal to
\partial_{y}G(x,\cdot)(I-Q_{r})
.
Applying the Poincaré inequality on separable Hilbert spaces gives

\int\|G(x,y)-\mathbb{E}_{y\sim\gamma}[G(x,y)]\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}\gamma(dy)\leq\int\|\partial_{y}G(x,y)\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\gamma(dy)

for almost every
x
.
Integrating again over
x\sim\gamma
gives

\displaystyle\iint\|F(Q_{r}x+(I-Q_{r})y)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})](x)\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}\gamma(dy)\gamma(dx)

\displaystyle\leq\iint\|DF(Q_{r}x+(I-Q_{r})y)(I-Q_{r})\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\gamma(dy)\gamma(dx).

Since

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})](x)=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})](Q_{r}x+(I-Q_{r})y)

and
Q_{r}x+(I-Q_{r})y\sim\gamma
, we obtain the inequality (
67
) for
F\in\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
.

We use a density argument to extend the result to arbitrary
F\in H^{1}_{\gamma}
.
We first note that (
67
) is equivalently written as

\|F-\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}\leq\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-Q_{r})\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}.

From here, consider an approximating sequence
F_{n}\in\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
converging to
F
in
H^{1}_{\gamma}
.
An application of the triangle inequality gives

\|F-\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}\leq\|F_{n}-\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F_{n}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}+\|F-F_{n}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}+\|\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F-F_{n}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}.

Applying the subspace Poincaré inequality for
F_{n}
yields

\|F-\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}\leq\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F_{n}(I-Q_{r})\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}+\|F-F_{n}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}+\|\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F-F_{n}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}.

Another application of the triangle inequality yields

\displaystyle\|F-\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}\leq

\displaystyle\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-Q_{r})\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}+\|(D_{\mathcal{E}}F_{n}-D_{\mathcal{E}}F)(I-Q_{r})\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}

\displaystyle+\|F-F_{n}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}+\|\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[F-F_{n}|\sigma({V_{r}}^{\!\dagger})]\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}.

Since
F_{n}\rightarrow F
in
H^{1}_{\gamma}
, the latter three terms in involving the difference of
F_{n}
and
F
can be made arbitrarily small (see also
Corollary

42
). This leads to the subspace Poincaré inequality for general
F\in H^{1}_{\gamma}
.
∎

C.5
Proofs for the sampling error bounds

C.5.1
Proofs for the Monte Carlo errors

Here, we present the proofs in the analyses of the sampling errors in
Section

3.6
.
We begin with the proofs for the Monte Carlo error bounds,
Lemma

29
and
Lemma

31
.
The proof of
Lemma

29
is reproduced below from
[
5
]
.

Proof.

(of
Lemma

29
)
Given the assumption that the second moment is bounded, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{N}\left[\|\hat{z}-\bar{z}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{N}\left[\left\langle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}z_{k}-\bar{z},\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}z_{k}-\bar{z}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\nu}[\|z\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}]+\frac{N^{2}-N}{N^{2}}\left\langle\bar{z},\bar{z}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-2\left\langle\bar{z},\bar{z}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}+\left\langle\bar{z},\bar{z}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\left(\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\nu}\left[\|z\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\|\bar{z}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\left(\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\nu}\left[\|z-\bar{z}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]\right),

where we have made use of the independence of
z_{i},z_{j}
for
i\neq j
.
∎

We then proceed with the proof for the error in the fourth moments.

Proof.

(of
Lemma

31
)
Assume first that
z
is a centered random variable, i.e.,
\bar{z}=0
.
Then,

\mathbb{E}_{N}[\|\hat{z}-\bar{z}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{4}]=\frac{1}{N^{4}}\mathbb{E}_{N}\left[\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{N}z_{i},\sum_{j=1}^{N}z_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\left\langle\sum_{k=1}^{N}z_{k},\sum_{l=1}^{N}z_{l}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right]=\frac{1}{N^{4}}\sum_{i,j,k,l=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{N}\left[\left\langle z_{i},z_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\left\langle z_{k},z_{l}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right].

For
(z_{i})_{i=1}^{N}
that are i.i.d., the expectation is zero whenever a single index differs from the remaining three, e.g.,
i\neq j,k,l
, since

\mathbb{E}_{N}\left[\left\langle z_{i},z_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\left\langle z_{k},z_{l}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right]=\left\langle\mathbb{E}_{N}[z_{i}],\mathbb{E}_{N}[z_{j}\left\langle z_{k},z_{l}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}]\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\left\langle\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\nu}[z],\mathbb{E}_{N}[z_{j}\left\langle z_{k},z_{l}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}]\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=0.

Thus, the only non-zero terms are those where all indices are equal, i.e.,
i=j=k=l
,
and those where two indices are equal, e.g.,
i=j\neq k=l
.
The former contributes
N
terms of the form
\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\nu}[\|z\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{4}]
.
The latter case corresponds to
\binom{N}{2}\cdot\binom{4}{2}=3N(N-1)
terms,
where for every term,
the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality implies that

\mathbb{E}_{N}\left[\left\langle z_{i},z_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\left\langle z_{k},z_{l}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}_{N}\left[\|z_{i}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\|z_{j}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\|z_{k}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\|z_{l}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{N}\left[\|z_{i}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}_{N}\left[\|z_{j}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\nu}\left[\|z\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]^{2},

where we have again made use of independence.
Combining the two contributions, we arrive at

\mathbb{E}_{N}\left[\|\hat{z}-\bar{z}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{4}\right]\leq\frac{N\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\nu}[\|z\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{4}]+3N(N-1)\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\nu}[\|z\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}]^{2}}{N^{4}}\leq\frac{K_{z}}{N^{2}},

where it suffices to take

K_{z}=\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\nu}[\|z\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{4}]+3\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\nu}[\|z\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}]^{2}.

When
z
is uncentered, we note that

\hat{z}-\bar{z}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}(z_{k}-\bar{z})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=k}^{N}\tilde{z}_{k}

where
\tilde{z}_{k}=z_{k}-\bar{z}
are centered i.i.d. random variables.
Thus, applying the result for the centered case above to the random variable
\tilde{z}
yields the desired result.
∎

C.5.2
Proofs for PCA and DIS sampling errors

The proofs for
Proposition

32
and
Proposition

33
then follow from the previous results
Lemma

29
and
Lemma

31
.

Proof.

(of
Proposition

32
)
Lemma B.2 in
[
5
]
considers the uncentered case.
To derive the result for the centered case, we decompose the covariance estimator
\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}
as

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}(F(x_{k})-\widehat{F})\otimes(F(x_{k})-\widehat{F})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}(F(x_{k})-\bar{F}+\bar{F}-\widehat{F})\otimes(F(x_{k})-\bar{F}+\bar{F}-\widehat{F})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}(F(x_{k})-\bar{F})\otimes(F(x_{k})-\bar{F})+(\bar{F}-\widehat{F})\otimes(\bar{F}-\widehat{F}).

Let
\bar{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}(F(x_{k})-\bar{F})\otimes(F(x_{k})-\bar{F})
, then

\|C_{\mathcal{Y}}-\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\leq 2\|C_{\mathcal{Y}}-\bar{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}+2\|(\widehat{F}-\bar{F})\otimes(\widehat{F}-\bar{F})\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}.

(117)

In particular, the first term reverts to the case of uncentered covariances
\|C_{\mathcal{Y}}-\bar{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}
, which we present here for completeness.
Since
\|y\otimes y\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}=\|y\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}
for
y\in\mathcal{Y}
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|(F-\bar{F})\otimes(F-\bar{F})\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|F-\bar{F}\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{4}]<\infty.

This allows us to apply
Lemma

29
using the random variable
z=(F-\bar{F})\otimes(F-\bar{F})
,
taking values in the Hilbert space
\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})
, whose mean is
C_{\mathcal{Y}}=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[(F-\bar{F})\otimes(F-\bar{F})]
.
This gives the bound

\mathbb{E}_{N}[\|C_{\mathcal{Y}}-\bar{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|(F-\bar{F})\otimes(F-\bar{F})-C_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]}{N}.

For the second term in (
117
), we have

\|(\widehat{F}-\bar{F})\otimes(\widehat{F}-\bar{F})\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}=\|\widehat{F}-\bar{F}\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}

such that

\mathbb{E}_{N}[\|(\widehat{F}-\bar{F})\otimes(\widehat{F}-\bar{F})\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}_{N}[\|\widehat{F}-\bar{F}\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{4}]\leq\frac{\|F-\bar{F}\|_{L^{4}_{\gamma}}^{4}+3\|F-\bar{F}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{4}}{N^{2}}.

Combining the bounds for the two terms from (
117
) yields

\mathbb{E}_{N}[\|C_{\mathcal{Y}}-\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]\leq\frac{2\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|(F-\bar{F})\otimes(F-\bar{F})-C_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]}{N}+\frac{2\|F-\bar{F}\|_{L^{4}_{\gamma}}^{4}+6\|F-\bar{F}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{4}}{N^{2}}.

Since
N^{-2}
decays faster than
N^{-1}
, we can simply take the constant as

M_{C_{\mathcal{Y}}}=2\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|(F-\bar{F})\otimes(F-\bar{F})-C_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]+2\|F-\bar{F}\|_{L^{4}_{\gamma}}^{4}+6\|F-\bar{F}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{4}.

∎

The strategy for the sampling error estimates in the DIS case is similar (and in fact, simpler, since we do not need to account for the centering by the empirical mean).

Proof.

(of
Proposition

33
)
Recall that for
H_{\mathcal{X}}=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[D_{\mathcal{E}}F^{*}D_{\mathcal{E}}F]
and
H_{\mathcal{Y}}=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[D_{\mathcal{E}}FD_{\mathcal{E}}F^{*}]
,
their empirical estimators

\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{X}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x_{k})^{*}D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x_{k}),\qquad\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{Y}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x_{k})D_{\mathcal{E}}F(x_{k})^{*},

are sample averages of the random variables
D_{\mathcal{E}}F^{*}D_{\mathcal{E}}F
and
D_{\mathcal{E}}FD_{\mathcal{E}}F^{*}
that take values in the Hilbert spaces
\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})
and
\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})
, respectively.
Note that for Hilbert–Schmidt operators
A\in\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2})
and
B\in\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{3})
, which map between separable Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{3}
, the norm of their composition satisfies
\|AB\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{3})}\leq\|A\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2})}\|B\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{3})}
. Thus, we have

\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F^{*}D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})}\leq\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2},\qquad\|D_{\mathcal{E}}FD_{\mathcal{E}}F^{*}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}\leq\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}.

Squaring and taking expectations gives us

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F^{*}D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})}^{2}]\leq\mathbb{E}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{4}]<\infty

and

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}FD_{\mathcal{E}}F^{*}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]\leq\mathbb{E}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{4}]<\infty

by our moment assumption. This allows us to apply
Lemma

29
, which yields the desired result with constants

M_{H_{\mathcal{X}}}=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F^{*}D_{\mathcal{E}}F-H_{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})}^{2}],\qquad M_{H_{\mathcal{Y}}}=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}FD_{\mathcal{E}}F^{*}-H_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}].

∎

C.6
Proof of the main result

We now conclude with the proof of the main theorem,
Theorem

10
, which combines all the results from the previous sections.

Proof.

First note that,
under the assumption that
F\in H^{2}_{\gamma}\cap W^{1,4}_{\gamma}
,
for almost every realization of the samples

(x_{k})_{k=1}^{N},x_{k}\sim\gamma
i.i.d., (i.e., almost surely with respect to
\gamma^{N}
),
the empirical estimator
\widehat{F}\in\mathcal{Y}
,
the empirical covariance
\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}
and sensitivity operator
\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{Y}}
lead to orthonormal bases
(\widehat{u}_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
in
\mathcal{Y}
,
and the empirical sensitivity operator
\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{X}}
leads to an orthonormal basis
(\widehat{v}_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}})_{i=1}^{\infty}
in
\mathcal{E}
.
Additionally,

Assumption

7
ensures
that
\widehat{v}_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}
are elements of
\mathcal{X}
.
We can therefore apply
Corollary

17
to obtain the existence of a neural network
\varphi_{\psi,\theta}
such
(
52
) and (
53
)
hold for
\widehat{U}_{r}=\widehat{U}_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}},\widehat{U}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}
and
\widehat{V}_{r}=V_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}},\widehat{V}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}
.

Taking expectations
\mathbb{E}_{N}
for the terms in (
52
) and (
53
),
we have

\mathbb{E}_{N}[\|\bar{F}-\widehat{F}\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}]\leq\frac{\|F-\bar{F}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}}{N}

due to
Corollary

30
,
while the terms

2\mathbb{E}_{N}[\|F-\widehat{F}_{r}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}],\quad 2\mathbb{E}_{N}[\|\widehat{P}_{r}(D_{\mathcal{E}}F-D_{\mathcal{E}}\widehat{F}_{r})\widehat{Q}_{r}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}],\quad\mathbb{E}_{N}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-\widehat{Q}_{r})\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]

depend on the input dimension reduction
\widehat{V}_{r}
and

\mathbb{E}_{N}[\|(I-\widehat{P}_{r})(F-\bar{F})\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}],\quad\mathbb{E}_{N}[\|(I-\widehat{P}_{r})(D_{\mathcal{E}}F-D_{\mathcal{E}}\bar{F})\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}],

depend on the output dimension reduction
\widehat{U}_{r}
.
We can then consider each dimension reduction strategy separately, substituting in the results from the reconstruction, ridge function, and sampling errors developed in
Section

3
to obtain the final approximation errors.

Empirical output PCA

\widehat{U}_{r}=\widehat{U}_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
:
For the output reconstruction error,
Proposition

20
gives

\|(I-\widehat{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})(F-\bar{F})\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}\leq\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}+\min\left\{\sqrt{2r}\|C_{\mathcal{Y}}-\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})},\frac{2\|C_{\mathcal{Y}}-\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}}{\lambda_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}-\lambda_{r+1}^{\mathrm{PCA}}}\right\}.

To bound the derivative reconstruction error, we additionally require
Assumption

8
, which combined with
Proposition

21
gives

\displaystyle\|(I-\widehat{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq K_{D}\left(\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}+\min\left\{\sqrt{2r}\|C_{\mathcal{Y}}-\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})},\frac{2\|C_{\mathcal{Y}}-\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}}{\lambda_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}-\lambda_{r+1}^{\mathrm{PCA}}}\right\}\right).

Taking expectations with respect to the sampling error with the help of
Proposition

32
gives

\mathbb{E}_{N}\left(\min\left\{\sqrt{2r}\|C_{\mathcal{Y}}-\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})},\frac{2\|C_{\mathcal{Y}}-\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}}{\lambda_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}-\lambda_{r+1}^{\mathrm{PCA}}}\right\}\right)\leq\min\left\{\sqrt{\frac{2rM_{C_{\mathcal{Y}}}}{N}},\frac{2M_{C_{\mathcal{Y}}}}{(\lambda_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}-\lambda_{r+1}^{\mathrm{PCA}})N}\right\}

with
M_{C_{\mathcal{Y}}}=2\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|(F-\bar{F})\otimes(F-\bar{F})-C_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]+2\|F-\bar{F}\|_{L^{4}_{\gamma}}^{4}+6\|F-\bar{F}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{4}
.
Thus, the overall constants are

K_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(1)}=1
,
K_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(2)}=K_{D}
, and

Empirical output DIS

\widehat{U}_{r}=\widehat{U}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}
:
From
Proposition

22
and
Proposition

23
, we have

\displaystyle\|(I-\widehat{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})(F-\bar{F})\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}+\min\left\{\sqrt{2r}\|H_{\mathcal{Y}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})},\frac{2\|H_{\mathcal{Y}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}}{\lambda_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}-\lambda_{r+1}^{\mathrm{DIS}}}\right\},

\displaystyle\|(I-\widehat{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})D_{\mathcal{E}}F\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}+\min\left\{\sqrt{2r}\|H_{\mathcal{Y}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})},\frac{2\|H_{\mathcal{Y}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}}{\lambda_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}-\lambda_{r+1}^{\mathrm{DIS}}}\right\}.

Taking expectations with respect to the sampling error using
Proposition

33
gives

\mathbb{E}_{N}\left[\min\left\{\sqrt{2r}\|H_{\mathcal{Y}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})},\frac{2\|H_{\mathcal{Y}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}}{\lambda_{r+1}^{\mathrm{DIS}}-\lambda_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}}\right\}\right]\leq\min\left\{\sqrt{\frac{2rM_{H_{\mathcal{Y}}}}{N}},\frac{2M_{H_{\mathcal{Y}}}}{(\lambda_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}-\lambda_{r+1}^{\mathrm{DIS}})N}\right\},

where
M_{H_{\mathcal{Y}}}=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}FD_{\mathcal{E}}F^{*}-H_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]
. Thus, the overall constants are

K_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(1)}=K_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(2)}=1
and

M_{\mathcal{Y}}=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}FD_{\mathcal{E}}F^{*}-H_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}].

Exact input PCA

\widehat{V}_{r}=V_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}
:
Under
Assumption

7
,
the results in

Proposition

19
,

Corollary

25
,
and
Proposition

27

give

\displaystyle\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-Q_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}})\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[\|DF\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\right]\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}},

\displaystyle\|F-\widehat{F}_{r}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[\|DF\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\right]\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}},

\displaystyle\|\widehat{P}_{r}(D_{\mathcal{E}}F-D_{\mathcal{E}}\widehat{F}_{r})Q_{r}^{\mathrm{PCA}}\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\left[\|D^{2}F\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|x\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}]\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{PCA}}.

Moreover, since there is no sampling error in the exact PCA, these bounds are deterministic, such that
M_{\mathcal{X}}=0
and

K_{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}=2\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|DF\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}],\quad K_{\mathcal{X}}^{(2)}=2\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D^{2}F\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}]\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|x\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}]+\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|DF\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})}^{2}].

Empirical input DIS

\widehat{V}_{r}=\widehat{V}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}
:

Proposition

22
and
Corollary

25

give

\displaystyle\|F-\widehat{F}_{r}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}+\min\left\{\sqrt{2r}\|H_{\mathcal{X}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})},\frac{2\|H_{\mathcal{X}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})}^{2}}{\mu_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}-\mu_{r+1}^{\mathrm{DIS}}}\right\},

\displaystyle\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F(I-\widehat{Q}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}})\|_{L^{2}(\gamma,\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}+\min\left\{\sqrt{2r}\|H_{\mathcal{X}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})},\frac{2\|H_{\mathcal{X}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})}^{2}}{\mu_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}-\mu_{r+1}^{\mathrm{DIS}}}\right\},

To bound the derivative ridge function error, we additionally need
Assumption

9
, which combined with
Proposition

28
gives

\displaystyle\|\widehat{P}_{r}(D_{\mathcal{E}}F-D_{\mathcal{E}}\widehat{F}_{r})\widehat{Q}_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}(\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Y}))}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq K_{H}\left(\sum_{i=r+1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}+\min\left\{\sqrt{2r}\|H_{\mathcal{X}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})},\frac{2\|H_{\mathcal{X}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})}^{2}}{\mu_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}-\mu_{r+1}^{\mathrm{DIS}}}\right\}\right).

Taking expectations with respect to the sampling distribution
and applying
Proposition

33
then yields

\mathbb{E}_{N}\left[\min\left\{\sqrt{2r}\|H_{\mathcal{X}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})},\frac{2\|H_{\mathcal{X}}-\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})}^{2}}{\mu_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}-\mu_{r+1}^{\mathrm{DIS}}}\right\}\right]\leq\min\left\{\sqrt{\frac{2rM_{H_{\mathcal{X}}}}{N}},\frac{2M_{H_{\mathcal{X}}}}{(\mu_{r}^{\mathrm{DIS}}-\mu_{r+1}^{\mathrm{DIS}})N}\right\},

where
M_{H_{\mathcal{X}}}=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F^{*}D_{\mathcal{E}}F-H_{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})}^{2}]
.
Thus, the overall constants are
K_{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}=2
,
K_{\mathcal{X}}^{(2)}=2K_{H}+1
, and
M_{\mathcal{X}}=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\|D_{\mathcal{E}}F^{*}D_{\mathcal{E}}F-H_{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})}^{2}]
.

As suggested in
Remark

11
, we have used
Assumption

7
to ensure the DIS yields
C^{-1}_{\mathcal{X}}\widehat{v}_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}
that are elements of
\mathcal{X}
.
We do not need the full
Assumption

7
in the input DIS result. Instead, we can relax the assumption by directly assuming that
F
is such that the eigenvalue problem involving
\widehat{H}_{\mathcal{X}}
yields
C^{-1}_{\mathcal{X}}\widehat{v}_{i}^{\mathrm{DIS}}
that are indeed elements of
\mathcal{X}
.
∎
